## Preface

The book originally came into being as a declaration of the author's love for the town of Ingelfingen in Hohenlohe, where he spent large parts of his early youth. There, Prince Friedrich Ludwig still plays an important role in the consciousness of the population today; he is constantly present through the castle and the Mariannenvorstadt (Marianne's Suburb) that he planned. This serves as an explanation for the starting point of this account.

A complete biography of the Prince and his family requires that the wider circumstances of his life also be grasped. These are Hohenlohe with its ancestral seat of Ingelfingen, the Kingdom of Prussia, and the emerging Kingdom of Württemberg; it is without doubt an exciting period of German history in which Friedrich Ludwig lived. On the one hand it is the "age of Goethe," on the other the French Revolution is unfolding, and Napoleon, through his military campaigns, defines an entire epoch. Goethe lived from 1749 to 1832, Napoleon from 1760 to 1821; our Prince Friedrich Ludwig from 1746 to 1818 — they are contemporaries. All three also had something to do with one another. Friedrich Ludwig, although initially merely Hereditary Prince of a very small dominion (Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen), took a lively part above all as a Prussian military commander in the upheavals of this period.

FL has gone down in history chiefly for the following reasons: he was a high-ranking soldier in Prussia's service, a (co-)loser at Jena and Auerstedt in 1806, and then a Prince who resigned after his Hohenlohe dominion was appropriated by the Duchy of Württemberg, elevated to a kingdom. Finally, he attempted to become an entrepreneur in Upper Silesia.

Accordingly, political conditions play a role in this book: the perception of the French Revolution and the experience of the Coalition Wars against revolutionary France, and the effects of the Napoleonic campaigns;

[p. 8]

added to this are territorial consolidations on the territory of the former Holy Roman Empire of the German Nation, such as the Confederation of the Rhine, and the Partitions of Poland with the rise and temporary fall of Prussia. These external events are accompanied by an intimated change in the attitude of the nobility, who no longer live purely in feudal fashion off the dues of peasants (and vintners) and off forest holdings, but instead seek out tasks for themselves in the military and in state administration, sometimes also engage themselves in (proto-)industrial activity, and send the next generation to university. — Personal dramas, as with Friedrich Ludwig and his wife Marianne, née Countess Hoym, unfortunately also occur along the way.

In engaging with this subject, it mattered a great deal to find a balance between the personal material on Friedrich Ludwig (hereafter mostly abbreviated as FL) and on Marianne and their immediate circle, on the one hand, and, on the other, the information on contemporary historical background that seemed necessary, which is interspersed and marked as "Excursus." Christian von Massenbach occupies a special role. For this reason a separate excursus, "Massenbach," is devoted to him as FL's companion and counterpart. — A balance was also needed between the personal material and the extensive economic information from Gerlinde Kraus's book (2001); the author undertook the labor of archival research in Upper Silesia for that work, with admirable success!

Archival work is also a distinctive feature of this book: the intensive use (in part also verbatim reproduction) of the extraordinarily rich sources in the form of letters and documents in the Hohenlohe Central Archive at Neuenstein (HZAN) and, via the Kalliope network of archives (a German union catalogue of archival finding aids), as well as elsewhere (see p. 251f.).

[p. 9]

## 1 Introduction to Hohenlohe and Ingelfingen

### 1.1 The Town of Ingelfingen[^1]

Ingelfingen, the birthplace of Friedrich Ludwig, had belonged to the House of Hohenlohe for around 400 years, from 1409 to 1806. The place itself, however, is considerably older. Ingolf was the name of the clan leader of the Alemanni; "Ingiluingen" is the early form of the place-name. The first documentary mention dates from 1080; in 1302 the place is referred to as an "oppidum", and town and market rights were granted in 1328 and 1334 respectively. What emerged was a town with a town wall and three town gates; the town's coat of arms bears an abbot's staff, which relates to the local church of St. Nicholas and to Comburg Abbey near Schwäbisch Hall.

Lichteneck Castle served as the residence of the ruling authority or of its local administrators (bailiffs, or, somewhat confusingly, also called "Keller"). An information panel, on which some details about the castle are recorded today, attests that Ingelfingen was a wine-growing town — and still is.

Lichteneck was built in 1251 by Kraft von Krautheim-Boxberg. He was the brother-in-law of the progenitor of the House of Hohenlohe, which held many possessions here. The surrounding vineyards (so viticulture already 800 years ago!) belonged in part to the Benedictine abbey of Comburg near Schwäbisch Hall. The castle was pledged as security several times. The bailiffs (referred to as Schultheiss, Keller, or Amtmann — in earlier times exclusively noblemen) resided here. The destruction of the castle, about which little is known, must have taken place between 1390 and 1460.

For administrative purposes, buildings were erected in the town, among them the "Alte Amtskellerei" (Old Bailiff's Office), now better known as the "Altes Schloss" (Old Castle), then the "Schwarzer Hof" (Black Court) next to the castle, and finally the "Amtshaus" (Bailiff's House) on Bühlhofer Straße.

[p. 10]

### 1.2 The House of Hohenlohe and the Lords of Ingelfingen

Concerning the House of Hohenlohe as a whole, and its division — arising from the old law of inheritance — into smaller local dominions, we are very precisely informed. The inheritance was divided among the male descendants in equal shares. A first great division of the territory in 1555 led to the formation of the Neuenstein line, which took part in the Reformation, and the Waldenburg line, which later, in 1667, reversed this step for itself.

The fragmentation of the territory continued. An institution eventually became necessary to guarantee administration in accordance with the feudal system (the whole of Hohenlohe was to be regarded as an imperial fief). The Senior of the House of Hohenlohe was always the reigning Count or Prince who was oldest in years; he was to exercise oversight over this. A "Feudal Court" (*Lehenhof*), consisting of a "Councillor" (*Rath*), a secretary, and a treasurer, assisted the Senior (here, "Rath" designates prominent "officials" who worked directly for the ruler). The entire feudal records were put in order beginning in 1773 by the archivist Court Councillor Hanselmann. By 1782 matters had progressed far enough to conclude a formal contract. One had recognized "the value of common action" in representing the House to the outside world. The Senior now appears as the official feudal tenant. But to return:

Count Ludwig Casimir (1527–1568) had inherited the castle and bailiwick of Neuenstein, Langenburg Castle, and further "bailiwicks," among them the town of Ingelfingen. He must also be mentioned here, since in Hohenlohe the people of Ingelfingen are called the "Kasimirlich" (the people of Künzelsau are called "Maddeslich," presumably after the Matthias fair they held; the people of Niedernhall, "Distelfinken" [thistle-finches]; the people of Crailsheim, "Horaffen" [horn-apes]...). Whether this, in the case of the Ingelfingers, goes back to the fact that the Count had many descendants there, or whether many people of Ingelfingen named their sons after the pious Count in his honor — that shall play no role here.[^2]

[p. 11]

*[illustration: Stadtwappen von Ingelfingen — a shield bearing a stylized hunting horn]*

*[illustration: Blick von der Ruine Lichteneck auf den Ortskern mit Schloss und Kirche.]*

[p. 12]

Casimir's three sons divided their father's inheritance among themselves by drawing lots. Count Albrecht Wolfgang received Langenburg, Friedrich Eberhard received Kirchberg, and Christian Kraft (1663–1743) received Ingelfingen, which already possessed a castle, as well as Schrozberg. He thus became the founder of the House of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen. The "bailiwick of Ingelfingen" then comprised Ingelfingen itself (with Lipfersberg, Kocherstein, and the Scheurachshof), Criesbach, Crispenhofen, and Hermuthausen. It was, and remained, a small holding.

In another respect, the Christian Kraft depicted below (p. 14) had drawn the lucky lot: as compensation for the lack of a residence at Schrozberg, and on account of the obligation to establish a dower seat for his mother at Ingelfingen, a sum of 10,000 florins was included in his inheritance. He then immediately began enlarging the castle and renewing the residence of Ingelfingen, and was able to move there from Langenburg in April 1702. The rooms in the castle did not quite suffice for the removal goods, so in 1704 he purchased rooms in the town hall from the town, which remained occupied for about 100 years (see p. 66f.). The New Castle, which today serves as the town hall, was finally completed around 1712.[^3] For his large family, among them many princesses, the building adjoining the castle to the east (the Lang House) was purchased in 1728, largely demolished, and rebuilt. For his son Heinrich August he purchased from a citizen of Ingelfingen the "Alte Amtskellerei" by the (former) Upper Town Gate, which has since been erroneously known as the Old Castle.

When Christian Kraft died in 1743, his four sons succeeded him with equal rights in the rather small territory of Ingelfingen. Philipp Heinrich (1702–1781) resided below in the New Castle, Heinrich August (1715–1796) above in the Old Castle, Christian Ludwig Moritz (1704–1758) in Schrozberg, and August Wilhelm (1720–1769), a general in the service of the Duke of Saxe-Gotha, far away in Ohrdruff in Thuringia (all four brothers had received an officer's training). Prince Philipp died in 1781 (he

[p. 13]

had been elevated to Prince in 1764, the Waldenburg line already in 1744), and had moved into the New Castle; he was succeeded, as the last of the brothers, by Prince Heinrich August, who died as Senior of the House of Hohenlohe in 1796. As an officer in the Franconian Circle Regiment, he had fought against Prussia in the Seven Years' War, and had spent years imprisoned in Magdeburg (one of his three daughters was born in Magdeburg). To avert the disadvantages of the previous law of inheritance, he introduced primogeniture in 1774, as did the other Hohenlohe lines around this time — that is, from then on the firstborn was, as a rule, to succeed his father alone. This was Friedrich Ludwig (1746–1818, Prince from 1796). He had two younger brothers, Prince Friedrich Carl (1752–1815) and Prince Georg (1757–1807), and four sisters, of whom only Princess Sophie Christiane (1762–1831) reached adulthood and was to become especially important to Friedrich Ludwig.

A brief account of the court household: for 1738 there exists a list of court personnel, numbering as many as 68 persons at that time.[^4] Adding their family members and the manservants and maidservants, this comes to 104. Further added to this were the "clergy" financed by the court and administrative staff of various ranks, so that in total 172 persons were closely bound to the court and economically dependent upon it. Heinrich August and his wife Wilhelmine Eleonore, Princess of Hohenlohe-Neuenstein-Öhringen, were a rather modest and unassuming ruling couple. Whether the court household retained this size under Heinrich August is rather unlikely. Nevertheless, this makes it understandable that the citizens of Ingelfingen were worried, when in 1805 the transfer of the court household to Öhringen threatened a disadvantage for their town (see p. 64).

[p. 14]

*[illustration: portrait of Christian Kraft zu Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, an elderly man in a powdered wig and dark coat; painter and year unknown]*

Christian Kraft zu Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen. (Painter and year unknown)

*[illustration: hand-drawn map of the Hohenlohe territories in northern Württemberg, showing Mergentheim, Weikersheim, Dörzbach, Schöntal, Neuestadt, Öhringen, Langenburg, Schwäbisch Hall, Rothenburg and (Massenbach), with Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen's territory shaded dark]*

The territory of all the Hohenlohe principalities in northern Württemberg, with the names of a few better-known places. Rothenburg and, to the south of it, Hohenlohe-Schillingsfürst already lie in Bavaria. Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen is shaded dark. – Massenbach is indicated here only because of its relative proximity (on the person of Christian von Massenbach, see further below).

[p. 15]

Prince Heinrich August was already being assisted in the business of government some time before his death by his son, Hereditary Prince Friedrich Ludwig. An example of this may be found in a letter from the year 1788, which the old Prince addressed to his son. It concerned the sale of a mill: he placed the decision in his son's hands, "since in such matters, especially economic ones, which will in due course fall to you, I would not wish to act against your will, seeing that at my already advanced age no reckoning can be made on my life."[^5] The trust placed in the son, and the monument erected on the occasion of the golden wedding anniversary, point to a good relationship between father and son. In 1796 FL succeeded his father.

### 1.3 The Last Ingelfingen Princely Couple

Prince Friedrich Ludwig (1746–1818), through the building activity he had already promoted as Hereditary Prince, became the renewer and expander of the town. Since 1782 the Prince's wife had been Marianne, née Countess of Hoym zu Droyßig, daughter of Princess Christiane von Osten-Sacken. The suburb was named Mariannenvorstadt (Marianne's Suburb) after her. The Countess was eighteen years old at her marriage, bore eight children over the seventeen years of the marriage, separated from her family in 1799, and obtained a divorce (in order to marry once more, in Mecklenburg). A complete overview of the family may be found on p. 21.

In the volume on Ingelfingen Castle, the archivist Rainer Gross writes:

> *Marianne, née Countess of Hoym, was a highly gifted, cheerful young woman who moved through house and society with natural yet elegant ease. On every visit to Ingelfingen she brought fresh life into the otherwise sedate, patriarchal order of the castle. She had*

[p. 16]

*[illustration: old engraving overview plan showing, from left, the church, the castle complex with its garden in front, the demolished Steubertor, and the long "Alte Kelter" building]*

Overview plan showing, from left, the church, the castle complex, the castle garden before it, the Steubertor demolished for the town's expansion, and the elongated building of the "Alte Kelter," from which the Mariannenvorstadt will take its beginning toward the right (east).

*[illustration: photograph of a tall fluted obelisk monument with small statues at its base, standing among trees]*

The monument erected in the castle garden on the occasion of the wedding anniversary of FL's parents.

[p. 17]

*unaffected manners, loved to take part in hunts or to paint. In Ingelfingen she knew how to free herself from the Sunday afternoon church services and other constraints. She likewise warded off the stiff court customs for her children. Her daughters were never made to wear a corset. Her warm relationship with her children was not to be impaired by rigid court protocol.*[^6]

A covered walkway leads from the castle to the princely gallery in the nearby church. It can be recognized as an arcade on the second upper floor of the "Schwarzer Hof," and again along the town wall just before the church (the "Schwarzer Hof" is part of a building complex that housed the seat of the princely administration). Marianne would therefore not have needed to leave the castle at all for the Sunday afternoon church service. But she preferred to spend that time otherwise, and she was permitted to do so.

However, the growing family was not often gathered in Ingelfingen, with the possible exception of the year 1796 (before that the Prince had been occupied, not too far away, in the Wars of the Coalition against France) and then once more during his time in Ansbach. Only one of the eight children is verifiably known to have been born in Ingelfingen (1794), and that child died after four weeks. The family did nonetheless come to visit from time to time; thus the old Prince wrote to his son in 1788:

> *The latest newspapers give strong hope of a general peace, which pleases me all the more since you promise to come here in that event. When this occurs to me and seems even half credible, I am filled with joy at the prospect of truly enjoying your company once more, my dearest son. In the event of peace it may well happen that your two brothers may also come here, which would probably be the last time I could have all my children together.*[^7]

[p. 18]

*[illustration: composite oval portrait of the princely couple — Friedrich Ludwig on the left in uniform with sash and star, Marianne on the right]*

*Composite portrait of the princely couple. (Painter and year unknown, very probably a court painter)*

*[illustration: photograph of the Ingelfingen town center showing the old schoolhouse, the church, and the castle with its administrative buildings]*

*Frontal view of the Ingelfingen town center with (from left) the old schoolhouse, the church, and the castle together with its administrative buildings.*

[p. 19]

Among the children were the daughter Sophie Christiane; Friedrich Karl, Austrian Field Marshal; Georg, Prussian Major-General; and Friedrich Ludwig himself. From another letter of the old Prince of Hohenlohe:

> *In the coming week I expect your dear wife with her dear children, which I look forward to with childlike joy and can scarcely await. Would to God that you too were among them, then my pleasure would be complete.*[^8]

The course of events brought it about that in 1805 Friedrich Ludwig had to give up the Ingelfingen residence in order to carry on the House of Hohenlohe-Öhringen there. He then resigned as early as 1806, and his son Friedrich August succeeded him there as Prince August II (1784–1853). Another son, Prince Adolf zu Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen (1797–1873), appears initially to have taken charge of Ingelfingen; his interests, however, lay elsewhere: as a Prussian general and member of the Council of State, he rose to become Minister-President of Prussia. — The castle and castle garden in Ingelfingen (and much else besides) now belonged to whichever Prince of Hohenlohe-Öhringen held the title. A branch office of the administration remained in Ingelfingen until the middle of the nineteenth century.

[p. 20]

## 2 The Prince's Career

### 2.1 Early Youth in Ingelfingen and Öhringen

Friedrich Ludwig was born on 31 January 1746 in Ingelfingen as the eldest son of Heinrich August von Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen. His mother Wilhelmine was a Princess of Hohenlohe-Neuenstein-Öhringen; in the choice of partners the family had kept to its own circle — as so often in Hohenlohe. It is also worth noting that his godfathers were the Margrave of Ansbach and Bayreuth and King George of England.

The boy grew up at first in the Ingelfingen residence with an "Informator" (tutor), Conradi. When the latter accepted a parish post in Rupertshofen (belonging to another Hohenlohe court) in 1755, the child was brought to the court at Öhringen, where the prince's own son had already died in 1755. There he was instructed together with his cousin Count Castell-Rüdinghausen by the tutor Johann Christoph Riegel; a French "language master" was also employed (he later of course wrote and spoke French, and many of his letters, in keeping with the custom of the time, are composed in French, including ones within the family, such as those between his wife and himself). The Court Painter Schillinger and his assistant Wagner also took an "active part" in portions of the instruction. He then also attended the Gymnasium in Öhringen. — Particularly worth noting, in view of his later persistent financial troubles, is that the boy Friedrich Ludwig was required to keep his own accounts of daily expenditure out of his pocket money; a reprimand for lack of punctuality in this regard is documented.[^9] Unfortunately this educational measure had no success — financial troubles accompanied him throughout his life.

[p. 21]

### 2.2 Marriage and Family

FL married late — Marianne, Countess of Hoym zu Droyßig, seventeen years his junior. None other than Frederick II, King of Prussia, had drawn Friedrich Ludwig's attention to this very wealthy young lady (certainly already aware of the financial problems already besetting his protégé). The couple was regarded as glamorous. Frederick II then also became godfather to the first son, August.

Marianne, Countess of Hoym zu Droyssig, born in Mainz on 6 October 1763, died on 20 April 1840 at Marienhof in Mecklenburg. Her parents had moved temporarily to Mainz, and afterward also to Pyrmont, for political reasons. Her father died as early as 1769. The family's home was in Saxony.

The children of Friedrich Ludwig and his wife Marianne, née Countess Hoym, were:

- August, born on 27 November 1784 in Breslau, died on 15 February 1853 (godfather was Frederick II); married in 1811 to Louise of Württemberg (niece of the King).
- Adelheid (Adele), born on 20 January 1787 in Breslau, died in 1858; married in 1812 to Ludwig zu Hohenlohe-Kirchberg.
- Luise Amalie (Emilie), born on 20 November 1788 in Breslau, died in 1859; married in 1810 to Albrecht zu Erbach-Fürstenau.
- Wolfgang Ludwig, born on 18 December 1789 in Breslau, died on 9 February 1790.
- Auguste, born on 16 November 1793 in Walluf (Rheingau), died on 8 July 1821; married in 1816 to Karl von Hessen-Philippsthal-Barchfeld.
- Ludwig Karl, born on 16 November 1794 in Ingelfingen, died on 14 December 1794.

[p. 22]

- Adolf, born on 29 January 1797 in Breslau, died in 1873; married in 1819 to Luise zu Hohenlohe-Langenburg.
- Alexander, born on 3 July 1798 in Alt-Scheitnig (Breslau), died in 1829.

The wedding of Friedrich Ludwig zu Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen to Amalie Charlotte Marianne Luise Christiane, Countess of Hoym zu Droyßig, took place on 8 April 1782 at the castle in Glaina, Thuringia. As early as 9 October 1781, extensive contracts concerning the financial side of the union had been concluded in Leipzig. — The firstborn, August, became FL's successor. All three daughters married in a manner befitting their rank.

After the separation from FL in 1799 (see p. 77f.), Marianne entered into a union with Friedrich, Count of Osten-Sacken, a relative of her stepfather, and had two daughters with him: Angelica, born 1802, and Auguste, born 1804. These daughters, too, married in a manner befitting their rank in Mecklenburg at the ages of 18 and 20 respectively. — The formal judicial divorce of the princely couple did not take place until 1804. A so-called Transact of 19 November 1799, which contained arrangements between mother and daughter, plays a major role here.

From the marriage contracts:[^10] Of primary importance are first the proceeds from the inheritance of her father, Count Hoym, which was administered by Marianne's mother. Without going into detail here, suffice it to say that mother and daughter each received about 8,000 thalers annually from it. In the marriage contract between Marianne and Friedrich of 1781, no concessions exceeding the usual measure were made to the husband regarding his share. The Hereditary Prince undertook to leave his mother-in-law a free hand in the administration of the dominions, and "not to burden the same with debts, so that they may be preserved together for the flourishing of both future lines of descent." Of those 8,000 thalers of annual income, the Prince received 4,600 into his own hands, and left 3,400 to his wife as so-called pin money. He likewise promised to claim for himself only a quarter of any future income accruing to her by inheritance, but to leave the remaining three-quarters at her free disposal.

[p. 23]

Marianne's mother was Princess Christiane Charlotte Sophie von der Osten-Sacken (1733–1811), née Baroness of Dieskau, widow of Count Hoym. Her first husband, Julius Gebhard, Count of Hoym (1721–1769, Privy Councillor of Electoral Saxony), was the father of her only child. The castle at Slaventzitz in Upper Silesia, which FL inhabited in the last years of his life, had come into the possession of the later Princess through the Counts of Hoym, and was eventually made over entirely to FL by the daughter. After the Count's death, the mother Christiane married the Chief Chamberlain of Frederick II, Carl von der Osten-Sacken, Prince from 1786, later Privy Minister of State and War, who died in 1794 in Berlin. From 1777 the couple lived in a palace on the Wilhelmstraße in Berlin. The daughter often visited them there, and after her separation from FL, from her residence at Bellin Castle. The Princess died in Berlin — she had long been cared for by the Councillor of Medicine and Justice Cosmar, whom she also named executor in her 100-page will.[^11] The daughter Marianne, too, had dealings with him in his capacity as guardian of her children. As administrator of the estate, the Princess had appointed the Minister of State and Justice von Kircheisen.

As mentioned at the outset, Marianne, after whom the Mariannenvorstadt (Marianne's Suburb) of Ingelfingen is named, separated from her husband and children in 1799, after eighteen years of marriage and the birth of eight children together. This will be discussed again on several occasions. She left the family residence at Breslau. The children left behind were then fifteen, twelve, eleven, and six years old, and the two youngest (Adolf and Alexander) were only two and one year old! The father, FL, had spent the first months of the year in Hohenlohe. He asked his sister Sophie Christiane for help. She was already somewhat acquainted with the older children from their occasional stays in Ingelfingen. Very probably she traveled to Breslau together with FL at once. Sophie also taught the younger children

[p. 24]

and remained (presumably) three years in Breslau. Even before the separation, the three eldest had occasionally lived with their mother in Berlin, in the palace of their grandmother, Princess Osten-Sacken. They stayed there for the time being. Also present in Berlin, as on their travels, was the tutor and private instructor Kahlert.

What had moved the mother Marianne to take this step? The Hohenlohe biographer Adolf Fischer, who is well acquainted with the letters in the archive at Neuenstein, reports that she had "observed, with the deepest silence, her entire relationship to her former husband" (but see "Ein Wort des Vertrauens", p. 83f.). She did not, however, remain silent about her hatred of the French Revolution, which had allegedly led to the separation from her children, nor about the "fatal King of France."[^12]

This statement, with its high degree of abstraction, does not really get us anywhere. The usual grounds for separation today were not relevant for the nobility of that time. Childlessness in a marriage was often a reason. Extramarital affairs, even ones resulting in a child, were not unusual. Above all, when claims arose from them, they were kept quiet or simply passed over — though sometimes, too, they were treated quite openly. In any case, quite apart from whether affairs gave Marianne grounds for a separation (probably not), there is evidence of illegitimate children of FL's, which is presented in an Excursus beginning on p. 27.

### 2.3 Who was Marianne, Countess of Hoym?

Might an anonymously published epistolary novel from 1792, titled "Ferdinand Count of Falkenstein and Marianne of Ingelfingen. A Marriage Story of Recent Times," offer some help here?[^13]

That there was a second Marianne of Ingelfingen is extremely improbable. How did the author arrive at her name? And who is Ferdinand Count of Falkenstein? Behind this pseudonym lies concealed the son of Maria Theresa (1717–1780), the later Emperor Joseph II (1741–1790). For decades he traveled

[p. 25]

through Europe under this name, since the succession was indeed a very long time in coming. He got to know France and the lands of the Danube Monarchy, and eventually came to Silesia as well, where he visited his mother's (former) archenemy, Frederick II, with whom he got along well. As reported above, Frederick had played a role in bringing about the marriage between Marianne, Countess of Hoym, whom he knew, and FL. The two had married in 1782, and Frederick II will not have lost sight of them, having also become godfather to their first son August, born in 1784. He could therefore have told his visitor about her.

In the posthumously published novel, Marianne, mistress of a castle called "Breitenstein," is greatly admired by the principal narrator (letter-writer) Ferdinand von Falkenstein. He spends a few days at Breitenstein with some of Marianne's friends and acquaintances. She is a calm person who enjoys drawing and painting (like the "real" one). They talk with one another, go for walks, and plan a carriage excursion to nearby Breslau. Marianne is married to a man named "Ingelfingen," who is given no first name; he is frequently away. He is a womanizer, and one of his mistresses is also present. He is moreover a tyrant and a boor, snubbing and abusing his wife. He is a drinker and a gambler, so Count Falkenstein writes to a friend in one of the 39 letters that make up the book (all six adults gathered there write letters). He demands that she accompany him and his current favorite to Karlsbad. There, however, he falls ill and dies, apparently as a result of one of his excesses. Falkenstein now regards Marianne as free and wrestles with himself over whether he may bind himself to her. After much deliberation, the book ends with him falling to his knees before her to make his proposal. — Any further comparison between the novel and the reality to be described here is pure speculation. The novel's Marianne and the real one of Ingelfingen surely have things in common; but the husband probably does not. FL a womanizer, gambler, and drunkard? It would have been kept quiet in every text written about him

[p. 26]

— scarcely conceivable. — It is surely advisable to return to serious historical sources. I again cite Adolf Fischer:

> *Amalie Louise Marianne Charlotte, Countess von Hoym, was regarded as one of the most spirited and charming young ladies of her time. She knew how to move through society with an elegant yet natural ease, and not only that — as the future and sole heiress of the extensive estates in Saxony and Silesia, she was one of the wealthiest women in the Duchy of Saxe-Weimar-Eisenach and in Prussian Silesia. Her financial circumstances did not, however, allow her free choice of her future husband. [...] Unlike her future husband, to whom the young woman had even been recommended by Frederick the Great, she, as a well-bred daughter of good family, at nineteen years of age could hardly have had the opportunity to make inquiries about the Hereditary Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, who was eighteen years her senior.[^14]*

The first children of this marriage were born in Breslau; only one son, who died at the age of one month, was born in Ingelfingen in 1794. The Prince once grandiloquently called Ingelfingen the "principal seat of his family"; but this can hardly be said to have been true. Neither he nor Marianne with the children were regularly or for any length of time there. Whenever she was there, she indulged her "free-thinking" (her own expression), enjoyed painting miniatures, arranged to have to attend church only once on Sundays, went hunting with enthusiasm, meeting for it with the new relatives in Schrozberg, and paid visits to Öhringen, Kirchberg, Langenburg, and Bartenstein.

[p. 27]

### 2.4 Excursus I: Further Descendants

The following records,[^15] which extend to the year 1818, came to light only recently in the Neuenstein Hohenlohe Archive. The author Fischer either did not know of them in 1871 or deliberately did not include them in his account. They are presented here as thoroughly characteristic of the period:

*First*: A letter concerning the existence of an illegitimate son of FL, born in December 1782 in Berlin, following a tryst in Leipzig at the beginning of the year (Marianne and FL married in April 1782!). The child's mother had married a Herr Heinrichs, and he, a painter by profession, wrote the Prince a lengthy letter in 1798 from his residence in Potsdam. Whether FL or his wife had known of the son beforehand is unknown. The Heinrichs couple had in any case received no support (maintenance payments); the mother's husband now requested it, since expenses (for example for clothing) were now increasing. Unfortunately it is not documented whether or how the Prince responded. Nor is it known whether Marianne had any knowledge of the letter. In the first half of 1799 she was in Breslau with the children, and then, indeed, quickly gone.

*Second*: FL already had one illegitimate child; the girl Caroline was presumably born in 1775, that is, seven years before the marriage to Marianne. The mother was a "schoolmistress" in Hohebach. The girl grew up with her grandfather, the Ingelfingen "Tafeldecker" Siller (a "Tafeldecker" is a court servant responsible for the table silver, or for everything connected with the dining table apart from the food itself). He had apparently received assurance that the Prince would provide for this child. The pastor in Kupferzell knew of the child's existence and origins. What follows is a memorable administrative episode:

After little happened for years, Siller in December 1790 turned to the princely administration, where his report

[p. 28]

was recorded. He, Siller, stated that as grandfather he had raised the now 15-year-old girl the entire time, without receiving anything beyond a contribution toward clothing. This record was submitted to FL, who responded, under the date of December 1791, with his own handwritten instruction, which his administrative officer then put into proper bureaucratic form: "We Friedrich Ludwig, by the Grace of God Hereditary Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, do hereby certify and acknowledge [...]." Under "A" there follows a "deed of gift" for a manorial dwelling in the (Ingelfingen) suburb worth 3,000 florins, which is given to the grandparents as "property" with a lifelong "rent-free right of residence." As "B" an "obligation" is listed: 1,000 florins are earmarked by the Prince for the Weißbach saline, which are to yield 4% interest. This sum is to flow to the girl. FL thus appears to have acted. The matter of the housing presumably also worked out. But the financial support through an annuity that FL had devised failed, as did so much else connected with the Weißbach saline.

The story continues some years later, in 1806: Caroline is now 31 years old. In that year, the year of the takeover of Hohenlohe, she turns to the King of Württemberg with a petition to the King, evidently formulated in official German and certainly composed by a professional scribe. It then lands in Öhringen. In a kind of letterhead, the sender names "Siller, née 'Hohenlohe'" as her surname.

The text contains a new fact: the model of the annuity as a cash payment from a deposit in Weißbach has since 1795 been changed to an annual 200 florins from the treasury of the Chamber in Ingelfingen. Responsible for this arrangement, acting in the name of his brother-in-law Prince Friedrich Carl, is Princess Marianne. He himself actually has nothing to do with the matter, but was brought by Marianne to the point of ceding this sum to the princely Chamber and forgoing 200 florins of his apanage. The Chamber records it under a special heading as well. — That Marianne

[p. 29]

and her husband's brother, Friedrich Carl, know each other at all is new information. As a descendant of Heinrich August and as a general in the service of the imperial army, he had little to do with the other Hohenlohe houses. Until 1802 the above-mentioned arrangement held. After that Caroline received no further payments and, incomprehensibly, did nothing until 1806.

1806 is a singularly unfavorable moment for the petition. Since the summer of 1806, the Prince of Hohenlohe-Öhringen (and simultaneously of Ingelfingen) is FL's son August, himself now a subject of the King of Württemberg. FL will only take up residence, quite at the end of the year, as a prisoner of the French, entirely without authority, at the castle in Öhringen. The King begins to align the administration of his newly acquired territory of Hohenlohe with that of Württemberg. The Ingelfingen Chamber will shortly be merely an outpost of Öhringen.

In Öhringen as well as in Ingelfingen, an administrative process arises in response to the petition to the King, confirming that Caroline had received a cash payment for years; the vouchers are found. Why nothing more was paid out after 1802 remains unclear. The statement of position for Prince August reads:

> *May Your Most Serene Highness graciously be pleased to observe from the enclosed documents what Caroline Siller of Ingelfingen has represented to His Royal Majesty at Stuttgart concerning a pension of 200 Hohenlohe florins annually, and what the local Court Chamber has, as is its duty, noted thereon by way of explanation. Since it now appears to depend chiefly on the fact that His Highness — since the most gracious command given in the year 1802, which provided only for the case in which Siller might produce an instruction from His Highness Prince Friedrich Carl concerning his apanage, to pay her the same, was assured verbally — should most graciously set down in writing the resolution taken or*

[p. 30]

> *to be taken, it has therefore been humbly requested herein.*

On closer examination it emerges here that the claim was valid and that no grounds were found for discontinuing the payment. The "Sillerin" herself is to produce documentary proof. This is presumably not possible for her – that is sometimes how reluctant authorities proceed. August accordingly notes on the first page of the statement:

> *In the matter of Caroline Siller's affair, I have consulted with my Lord Father and have been authorized by him to repeat here in writing his repeatedly made verbal declaration, namely: that if the said Caroline should produce the consent of Prince Friedrich Carl to the deduction from his annual apanage, the Court Chamber will resume payment of the said pension of 200 florins to her.*

At any rate, he has discussed it with FL. That this was possible was almost a matter of chance: as already noted, FL had only very recently arrived on the scene. Whether the written consent of Prince Friedrich Karl ever actually existed is another question.

Another document surfaces, signed in Berlin by Marianne on 12 December 1795:

> *For and in the name of my brother-in-law, His Lordship Prince Friedrich Carl of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, I hereby assure Demoiselle Caroline Hohenlohe, for her maintenance for life, an annual pension of 200 [...] say two hundred florins [...] on the condition and in such manner that she may only spend it within the Hohenlohe territories, and under the presently specific condition that she depart at once from [...] and travel into the Hohenlohe lands. In witness whereof, my*

[p. 31]

> *own hand and affixed seal. Berlin, 12 December 1795. L.S. Marianne, Hereditary Princess of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, née Countess Hoym*

With this it did in the end go in the plaintiff's favor: the document was recognized in Stuttgart and it was decided to resume the payments. In Öhringen this instruction was accepted accordingly and signed by the Land Commissioner Count von Wenigerode on 15 November 1806. – The affair had come to an end; the workings of the administration had functioned!

*Thirdly*: further children of FL, raised by the "foster mother" (Pflanzmutter) Beata Kreiselmayer. – This story chronologically belongs at the end of the book, since it was only brought before the House of Hohenlohe-Öhringen after FL's death in Upper Silesia in 1818; on account of its substantive connection it is included here.

The document dates from September 1818, according to which the aforementioned woman, bearing the memorable designation "Pflanzmutter" (i.e., a foster mother who raises children entrusted to her), appears before the Court Chamber in Öhringen with a girl now 17 years old. She now comes, as expressly noted, from the Rothenburg region. She reports having raised three children of His Serene Highness FL, besides the daughter two sons, who (at some unspecified earlier point) had already been handed over to the Cadet House in Berlin. The mother of the girl, she says, had been "a distinguished lady whose name she could no longer recall." This woman had at first also occasionally visited the child. His Serene Highness, as the father, had sent money; she names – without further particulars – six ducats. She was also supposed to come to Slaventzitz to settle further matters. Now she had learned with dismay of his death. The girl now needed protection and provision, and lodging too would have to be purchased.

Her statements are not doubted, and the petition is evidently treated seriously. This matter must again be dealt with

[p. 32]

by Prince August. "Travel money is to be given to the Kreiselmayer woman." An answer from the Chamber regarding the girl's future is not given immediately, and only in part. Another plan is under consideration, "in order to provide for the girl."

In all three cases – are they perhaps merely examples? – it long remained open how the respective solution actually turned out in concrete terms. This indeterminacy is surely, in the main, also a matter of method, and to that extent these are also relevant contemporary testimonies (and it is only for this reason that they are presented here immediately one after another). The problem is smallest when no inheritance claims result, when boys, for instance, can be "handed over" to a cadet school.

FL's own conduct, and that of the Court Chamber, by no means corresponds to our own understanding, but it was in the end tolerated in that era. Punishments for unwilling fathers and considerable disadvantages for the "bastards" did, however, exist. Precisely in the *Ingelfingen Heimatbuch*[^16] an example from the court is included, from the year 1783 (thus before FL's reign): a high and noble overnight guest, His Serene Ducal Highness of Saxe-Meiningen, while himself still lying in bed in the morning, according to the testimony of the daughter of the court cook Gerster, had "pulled her with both hands and [...] fully accomplished the carnal commingling." She bore a child and – was punished by the Church, by the Ingelfingen parson! Although the place, time, and perpetrator were known, no – documented – judicial prosecution or punishment of the perpetrator followed.

For Marianne, all this was presumably of no real relevance. What perhaps did affect her was the case of Heinrich, since she, then just 18 years old, was at that time celebrating her wedding with FL, when he… – But let us leave that aside! The reasons for her decision to separate presumably remain her own secret. Or did she after all leave something behind concerning it?

Yes, she herself deposited a letter in 1811 which Fischer could not have known of, since he had already written his book in 1871

[p. 33]

and the document was only found by Gerlinde Kraus and made known through her publication[^17] in 2001. It is addressed to her son August, by then already grown, and to the guardians of the children. For that long, that is, 12 years after the separation which she had there justified, Marianne had kept silent. Since it builds upon knowledge of the documents produced immediately after the separation (the "Transact," the divorce proceedings, and the will of the Princess Sacken), it will be presented after the corresponding chapter "Separation and Divorce" (p. 77f.). – The "excursus" just made concerning the illegitimate offspring has, in substance, probably little or nothing to do with what is set out there.

[p. 34]

## 3 Friedrich Ludwig as Urban Renewer

### 3.1 Settlement of Trade and Construction of the Mariannenvorstadt

How did it actually come about that, in dreamy Hohenlohe, a local ruler hit upon the idea of resolutely pursuing economic progress as a path of development for his small realm? How did FL arrive at the idea of settling trade and industry — comparable to the founding of an industrial park today? He had, quite simply, seen a great deal, out there in the world, where his military service to the Prussian rulers had taken him. His idol was Frederick II, who, when he was not waging war, concerned himself a great deal with improving the economic condition and living circumstances of his subjects. To this belongs his acquaintance with Karl August von Hardenberg, and also his marriage to Marianne, Countess von Hoym zu Droyßig, who was native to Saxony. More will be said of this later. — It does not diminish his merit to point out that his uncle in Öhringen, Prince Ludwig Friedrich Karl, had undertaken something similar there with the Karlsvorstadt.

Friedrich Ludwig announced his intentions in a "Proclamation" in the year 1782:

> *It is the principal object of this most gracious Prince to make the new inhabitants of this suburb happy, to which end His Highness shall most graciously endeavor to bring about all that is possible, whereby the branch of trade and industry may be brought to the fullest flourishing.[^18]*

[p. 35]

*[illustration: photograph of the Hofapotheke, a building on a street corner]*

The Hofapotheke, the first new building outside the (demolished) Steubertor.

*[illustration: reproduction of a handwritten 18th-century document with seal]*

The deed, drafted personally by FL in his nearly illegible hand. Later he would almost always employ a scribe. The weaver Simon Böhm named here would settle in Ingelfingen. Erbprinz FL guarantees him the advantages (named below).

[p. 36]

He further added "that every enthusiast should receive free and unencumbered land on which to build, that he should be exempt from military billeting, that he need fear no monopoly, but rather that free trade and commerce should be exercised." Whoever could not build himself could purchase newly built houses, cheaply and ready to move into.[^19] The founding of the suburb represents an admirable testament to industrial settlement.

The first task was to win applicants for settlement, and this initially stalled. The bailiff (Amtmann) Taurinus and Court Councillor L. Braun had made precise calculations as to which occupational fields would be needed, after they had divided "the local circumstances here, in thought, [...] into classes." Active in the town were: "a red-tanner, a fustian-weaver, a stocking-weaver, a hatter, a soap-boiler, a sculptor, a bell-founder, a clockmaker, a stocking-knitter, a wool-spinner, a gunsmith, a coppersmith, a rope-maker, a potter, a brick-maker, two millers, a paver, two nail-smiths, a bookbinder, a button-maker, three shopkeepers, a brewer, a confectioner." It was precisely this "class" that needed to be increased, since it depended least on the town and its immediate surroundings.

Applicants were carefully selected; thus drinkers were not accepted (FL had informants sitting in the inns!). One craftsman who was indeed "a good worker, but who had shown himself to be a drunkard and had already gotten wildly and thoroughly drunk several times in Ingelfingen" was shown the door (of the town) without leniency. In 1787 a quite different decision had to be made: a Catholic, subject of the Comburg, had applied as the first non-Protestant for permission to settle. He was accepted; the Prince had expressed himself on the matter as follows: "[...] if his character is good and honest, then the Catholic religion is not at all offensive to me, and I am certainly as much for tolerance as anyone can be."[^20]

For persons judged suitable there were, in quite modern fashion, financial and other incentives. Thus no

[p. 37]

costs were charged for the building lot; further, there was a construction subsidy and a tax concession: a ten-year exemption from levies (presumably meaning the famous "tithe"). Complete exemption from corvée labor and military billeting. The costs of construction were advanced by the princely house or the building fund; on moving in, a quarter of the sum had to be paid, the remainder in annual installments over three years. The building funds were administered by the Government Councillor and Ingelfingen bailiff (Amtmann) Taurinus. Interested parties could also take over half-finished buildings and complete them with their own money, which apparently occurred chiefly toward the end of the project's overall twenty-four years.

The overall planning of the many new buildings here and in the actual suburb lay in the hands of the Director of Buildings and Salines, Court Councillor J.G. Glenk, engaged by Friedrich Ludwig in 1781, surely in close coordination with FL himself, who at this time was still Erbprinz. However, as an officer in Prussian service he was often not on site.

The various house types in the suburb, including the larger houses for officials or the ruler, were reviewed by FL. He himself designed one type of house entrance: an entryway reached by a few steps, from which apartment entrances led into the interior of the house to the right and to the left. These are thus semi-detached houses.

The first construction phase of the overall project began in 1782, after the Prince's proclamation, with the planning of 19 dwellings in five buildings and a projected cost estimate of 16,000 florins. The building material had to be brought in: the stones came from quarries within the Ingelfingen district itself; the timber, by contrast, was hauled in from Schrozberg. In 1785 it became apparent that the initial plan had been too small. Previously the Steubertor (the former eastern town gate, on the town side of the pharmacy) had already been demolished and a new gate structure erected about 400 meters to the east — this was now torn down again in order to carry the expansion further.

[p. 38]

*[illustration: photograph of a street corner building, the former "Alte Kelter" in Ingelfingen]*

The "Old Winepress" (though historically the second one, since a first press had stood opposite the church) at the beginning of Mariannenstraße was rebuilt after the "Outer Winepress" was completed in 1787. This produced an inn and, under the same roof adjoining it, six craftsmen's workshops with their associated dwellings.

*[illustration: photograph of a two-story house with a steep tiled roof, a typical building of the Mariannenvorstadt]*

A typical building of the Mariannenvorstadt

[p. 39]

This second building phase began in 1786 and lasted until 1804 or 1806. In between, admittedly, the building fund — managed by the bailiff (Amtmann) Taurinus — ran dry. Then in 1792 came a good wine year, and the winegrowers' dues replenished the fund somewhat. Taurinus lived in the "Old Bailiff's House" (Altes Amtshaus, on today's Bühlhofstrasse).

Johann Georg Taurinus served the lord of Ingelfingen here. J.G.T. had owned the rear building, purchased the front building to add to it, and brought the whole into good repair. "1743" and a monogram "J.G.T." can be read on the side of the house wall. – In the cemetery there still stand two monumental, though somewhat weathered, grave memorials commemorating him and a predecessor of the same name.

In 1789 there must have been occasion for a new decree:

> *In order not to draw only strangers into the trade, We admonish the parents of such young people who have the desire and aptitude to devote themselves to those crafts which are either not to be found here at all or not in sufficient number [...] We shall, where necessary, have capable young people supported out of the charitable foundations available. Should, however, one of the scholarship recipients settle abroad, We find it equitable that he repay what has been expended upon him [Everything, in some form, has already happened before!]. But in order to do away with the bunglers among the craftsmen, henceforth no one shall be accepted as a subject who cannot produce a certificate of craft or a trial piece.[^21]*

Father and son Braun, both Court Councillors, evidently found the undertaking so remarkable at the time that they had a monument made as a founding stone for the Mariannenvorstadt and set it up between their own building and the apothecary. The two Brauns

[p. 40]

*[illustration: photograph of a half-timbered gabled house with an oriel window, the "alte Amtshaus"]*

The Old Bailiff's House

*[illustration: photograph of a carved stone monument bearing a coat of arms with bee-like emblems]*

The monument erected by father and son Braun for the woman who gave her name to the Mariannenvorstadt.

[p. 41]

provided for a short text on the back, no longer legible today; it is composed in Latin and reads, translated:

> *This suburb was founded, with a generous hand, by Friedrich Ludwig, Hereditary Prince of Hohenlohe, pious and fortunate and great in war and peace, for the good of his native town and its citizens; and Marianne, his noble consort, ornament and adornment of our century, of her line and of her sex, gave it her name in 1782.*

On the front side, four-part coats of arms are carved into the two elliptical arches; unfortunately only the left one is clearly recognizable. With its quartering, the two "leopard" lions, the double eagle, and the three lilies at upper right, it corresponds to a Hohenlohe coat of arms. It would fittingly be the case, without doubt, if the coat of arms on the right represented that of Countess Marianne von Hoym zu Droyßig. And indeed it could be identified as such! Three of the four fields — an armored arm with a golden mace, a palm tree, and a crown — are still recognizable; at upper left (presumably) a bishop's mitre or a bishop's headband. A Hoym coat of arms with nine fields contains all these elements. That only four appear here is possibly an adaptation to match the Hohenlohe coat of arms.

Concerning the individual houses of Mariannenstraße, the architect Eduard Krüger wrote the following in 1923:

> *The rear of the houses stands open and adjoins meadows and orchard grounds, so that modest farming can be carried on. [...] The houses are arranged so that, besides the living rooms on the ground floor, there remains room for a workshop. [...] The attic spaces are fitted out for residential use.*
>
> *[...] In [house] type B, where there is only a single story, [...] the grouping of the rooms is in principle the same [...].[^22]*

[p. 42]

Already by 1783, according to Himmelheber in his "Description of the Künzelsau District Office"

> *there live in the suburb, alongside the customary local craftsmen, a ropemaker, a wigmaker, and a potter. In 1784 the sculptor Philipp Christoph Sommer is granted credit so that he can build in the suburb. In the same year the houses of the tapestry-weaver and the hatmaker are completed. In 1786 a house each is erected for a stocking-weaver, a goldsmith, and a nail-smith. The goldsmith Nicolaus Berger of Mannheim acquires a house in 1787.[^23]*

Farther out from the old town core stand, or once stood, simpler single-story cottages. Mariannenstr. 42 (demolished long ago) was, for example, the family home of the Court Carpenter J.G. Schillinger, whose descendants will still prove important. The trades of the craftsmen living here and further within the town are named above (p. 36).

### 3.2 Further Establishments

Even before the planning of the suburb, the Princely House had brought about the settlement of trades. At the founding of a gold-goods factory, the Prince gave 10,000 florins, and a further 10,000 were added through the issuing of shares (!). A director, Lartigue, was brought in from Strasbourg; his successor was the deputy director Köhler. In Adolf Fischer, the former Öhringen pastor and author of the "History of the House of Hohenlohe" of 1871, this is recorded thus, along with the claim that the enterprise never became profitable.[^24] In Bundschuh's "Lexicon of Franconia" of 1800, by contrast, it states:

> *On the whole, the processing of goods is not indeed the principal object of [Ingelfingen's] industry, yet there is found*

[p. 43]

> *here, besides the Lantiquian and Köhlerian jewelry factories, which on account of their tasteful work always have enough to do, good custom too in Frankfurt, where they attend the fairs.[^25]*

Which is correct? (Whether the gold and silver goods factory, established as a branch from Schwäbisch Gmünd after the takeover of the Adler inn in 1872 in its building, has anything to do with this seems unclear.)

In 1788, at the suggestion of Wilhelmine Leonore, Princess of Hohenlohe-Neuenstein-Öhringen and wife of Prince Heinrich August, a bathing establishment was planned on or in the Kocher river. The undertaking, a pontoon construction by the Court Carpenter Schillinger, proved, however, too difficult and was not pursued further.

Nor were there any lack of further plans: a grain market was to be established, a supplementary grain storehouse (probably not the one in the attic of the Inner Press House), a leather manufactory, a wax-candle factory, a wax bleachery, a large oil mill on the stream above the town. For the workers in the factory and the trade establishments, a consumers' cooperative was to be brought into being on the model of the Moravian Brethren. Which of these succeeded and lasted for any length of time remains an open question.

Equally unsuccessful was the effort to improve the roads and the postal and communications system. Thurn und Taxis was unwilling to establish a newly accessible post and travel station, so that FL, even as a senior Prussian officer, continued to have to make do with couriers. How he and his family managed the great distances between Ingelfingen and Breslau, and also Berlin, one may well wonder. By coach, of course.

Plans, too, for the construction of a representative mineral fountain in arched form beside the Mariannenstraße had to be abandoned. Likewise the work, pursued by the energetic Director of Buildings and Salines Karl C. F. Glenck (son of J. G. Glenk),

[p. 44]

for the extraction of coal near the Hermersberg, proved to be a misjudgment. Correspondence on the matter with FL continued until shortly before his death in 1818 (see p. 129f.).

In the field of education, plans could be realized.[^26] A Latin school had existed since as early as 1710, evidence of the aspiration attaching to the residence, which FL carried forward. It was attended by the children of clergymen, including from the surrounding area, as well as of administrative officials and employees of the court administration. The principal aims were linguistic knowledge, with an emphasis on Latin, ultimately in preparation for the so-called Land Examination. With interruptions, the school continued to exist into the twentieth century.

FL himself brought into being, in 1792, an "Industrial School." It was intended to familiarize the children with agricultural and craft knowledge and to prepare them for practical life. For girls, this concerned learning sewing, embroidery, and lace-making, as well as cooking and washing; for boys, viticulture, gardening and field cultivation, tree cultivation, wood carving, and basket-weaving. Instruction took place as a supplement to the main school on two weekdays.

FL also founded a "Drawing School" (presumably in the sense of technical drawing), with the Court Painter Wagner as master. Instruction and materials were free of charge for the pupils. This institution served predominantly, it seems, adult education. Instruction accordingly took place on weekends. Its purpose was to promote "the industriousness, skill, and taste of the artists and tradesmen" in the Mariannenvorstadt.[^27]

[p. 45]

*[illustration: photograph of a half-timbered house in the Mariannenvorstadt with a carved doorstone above the double entrance]*

House in the "Mariannenvorstadt" with a doorstone above the double entrance.

*[illustration: close-up photograph of the carved doorstone, showing a coat of arms with a monogram "FL" beneath a sunburst, flanked by grapevines, with an inscribed text panel below]*

Doorstone with a text of homage beneath an intertwined "FL": "Thy name shall not perish. Fame, fortune, and salvation to the best of princes; just is He and good, for what He thinks and does works the weal of His land." — Evidence of good acceptance of the overall project.

[p. 46]

## 4 Friedrich Ludwig's Career as a Prussian General

### 4.1 Stations in the Military up to the Peace of Basel in 1795

Even early on FL was drawn to a military career. At the age of 13 he received the promise of a captaincy in the Franconian Circle Regiment, part of the Imperial troops. He took up the post at 17. After that he was rarely at Ingelfingen Castle (as would also later be the case when he was reigning Prince). Military duties came first, and there were armed conflicts in abundance. He made his first campaign with this regiment. With the Imperial troops under General Kleefeld, FL rose to the rank of senior major (Obristwachtmeister). He received the necessary military knowledge from him and also acquired it himself.

One episode concerned the capture of a Prussian officer by ordinary soldiers, who intended to shoot this prisoner — it was Major von Zastrow. FL intervened, gave each of the soldiers involved a small sum of money, and von Zastrow was set free — and he was never to forget this of FL. He made a career directly at the court in Berlin and years later was able to assist FL with various wishes. For example, when it came to the promotion of officers, for which FL had petitioned the King. In that same year, 1766, at the age of 20, he entered the service of the King of Prussia, Frederick II, as a Major.

After the end of the Seven Years' War in 1763, FL was sent traveling, and among other places stayed for a long time in Paris. Frederick II was his first patron, and it was him he wished to emulate. It is reported that he came to Potsdam

> *to offer his services. Since his handsome bearing spoke well for the policy of the Prussian court with regard to the Imperial princes,*

[p. 47]

> *his appointment presented no difficulty. [...] his income amounted [not counting a major's salary] to no more and no less than 600 imperial florins. To satisfy his needs with this [...] presented all the more difficulty since the prince's needs were very costly. Above all things he wished to be regarded as an excellent rider; and since such ambition can only be satisfied through excellent horses, it was chiefly this item that plunged the prince into debt and ruined all his affairs in advance.*[^28]

The quotation comes from the often somewhat tendentious author Friedrich Buchholz (see p. 102f.). Frederick II also expressed himself about the young officer: "Croyez moi, le prince vaut par les qualites militaires tout le destin de l'empire, et je l'aime veritablement par ses excellentes qualites de l'esprit et du coer. Il fera une carriere brillante" — "Believe me, the prince is worth, in his military qualities, the whole destiny of the empire, and I truly love him for his excellent qualities of mind and heart. He will have a brilliant career."[^29]

The sequence of his military ranks is impressive; see the chronological table in the appendix, p. 235f. Frederick William II, King of Prussia after the death of Frederick II in 1786, retained him. He took him along as his military expert in August 1791 to the Convention of Pillnitz, where the foundations for the War of the First Coalition against France were laid. After the King's death in 1797, FL wrote the following:

> *It is a misfortune, especially for me, that this good lord [Frederick II] died too soon for me. But I cannot complain of the young lord [Frederick William III]. He treats me with great respect, passes over me in nothing that falls within my sphere of activity, but given our great distance, much occurs in other matters where I either fail to grasp the necessary connection [sic — clause defective in the original] or learn of it too late.*[^30]

[p. 48]

*[illustration: silhouette (scherenschnitt) of a man riding a horse]*

FL, likely as he liked to see himself: as a fine rider. (Ink drawing, 18th century; artist unknown)

*[illustration: ink drawing of a mounted officer in the foreground, with two onlookers in the background]*

In the background the young Friedrich Ludwig, in front on the horse his idol Frederick II. (Artist and year unknown)

[p. 49]

At any rate, when he was once summoned by him in September of the fateful year 1806, Frederick William III put him up in the rooms of Frederick II in the palace. FL would proudly report this on several occasions. Von Marwitz, FL's former aide-de-camp, writes in his memoirs that these were "now" occupied by the Crown Princess, and further: "Everyone crowded around the man who had sacrificed his princely holdings in order to follow the call of honor and the wishes of the Prussian Army."[^31]

FL had previously been Governor of Ansbach-Bayreuth, and from 1791 of Breslau. Silesia had become the center of his life; see p. 64f. on this.

He took part in the War of the First Coalition against France. At the so-called Cannonade of Valmy (13 September 1792), where the commander-in-chief, the Duke of Brunswick, had decided upon a retreat, he was not directly involved. He had been given the task of forming an advance guard with a cavalry troop and holding a corresponding French formation in check, driving it back if possible. He succeeded in this, but the Duke of Brunswick had made the above-mentioned decision with the main army. FL won numerous smaller battles in the course of this campaign. In particular, he defeated the French at Kaiserslautern, although he actually had no clear orders for a battle. From then on he was regarded as "the Victor of Kaiserslautern." Details of this campaign, which Fischer (1871) describes precisely, are otherwise without significance. Goethe took part in it as companion to Duke Charles Augustus and later wrote "The Campaign in France"[^32] about it.

From May 1794, FL was Inspector-General of the Lower Silesian Infantry in Breslau, then again head of an infantry regiment. In 1795 he received the superior inspection over the Prussian troops in the Franconian Margraviates and took up headquarters in Ansbach. He had been given this position by the King after the King had commissioned him to present his thoughts

[p. 50]

on the matter. FL did so in a detailed letter of 24 May 1795:

> *[...] In my conviction, the royal service can only gain from all this, and certainly no impure intention on my part, nor any inclination to vanity, still less any desire for convenience, shall arise from it. [...] Any craving for gain is in any case far removed from me. I seek nothing, but since I am at present anything but wealthy, I am glad to owe every proof of royal grace on this account as well.*[^33]

The King had understood, and granted a salary increase of 5000 florins. FL's letter is one of the very few, and a very early, documents in which he himself addresses his financial situation.

In Bayreuth he met the Prussian reformer Karl August von Hardenberg, who served there in a civil capacity at the court of the Prussian province of Brandenburg-Ansbach-Bayreuth for some years from 1791. The two remained on friendly terms in the further course of political business as well, and met, for instance, also in Ingelfingen.

### 4.2 Duties after the Peace of Basel

The Peace of Basel had come about in early 1795 with von Hardenberg as Prussia's chief negotiator, without the participation of Austria (that is, of the imperial army and the troops of the south German imperial estates allied with it under General Clairfait – note, only his name is French)!

A demarcation line (also called a neutralization line) through southern Germany had been agreed upon, intended as far as possible to prevent warlike undertakings. Austria was not in agreement with this development and regarded the whole matter with great suspicion. FL, as commander of the portion of the Prussian troops

[p. 51]

stationed in southern Germany, had his headquarters in Frankfurt, while the Austrians under Clairfait were in Groß-Gerau. Prussia hoped to be able to reduce its troops in Frankfurt (for reasons of cost) and replaced FL with another general. When the situation, however, again threatened to become critical, FL was immediately ordered back there by the King.

FL's task was to monitor the demarcation line. In addition, he was to reduce, as far as possible, the suspicion of the Austrians, who had been prepared to agree only to a supplementary accord, and not to a peace treaty, with France. He also had to maintain contact with the French under their commander-in-chief Jourdan.[^34] In retrospect FL described this task as a Herculean task. A separate peace such as that between Prussia and France did not come about between the Empire, represented by the imperial Austrian troops together with the allied imperial estates, and post-revolutionary France. The French side, moreover, called the neutralization line fundamentally into question. It even threatened to occupy Frankfurt and there levy "requisitions" (as it had once already done years before). FL was called upon as a diplomat. In confidential talks he was able to assure Clairfait that he would never permit the French to march through Frankfurt. And they then indeed held still. For this the city of Frankfurt rewarded him with the first-ever conferral of the dignity of honorary citizen (the corresponding list is headed by his name!). He had declined a gift of money.

Jourdan was prepared, in the event of a renegotiation of the demarcation line between Prussia and France, to accommodate the Hohenlohe principalities in the preservation of their neutrality. For this, however, he had to await the order of the Revolutionary Directory in Paris. FL immediately sent a courier there as well. A provisional agreement was also reached: French troops were to be permitted to enter Hohenlohe only in the most urgent necessity. – All of this is somewhat obscure, yet it does show rather well, for our purposes – a biography of Prince Friedrich Ludwig –

[p. 52]

how actively engaged he was, even in the field of diplomacy. It was, incidentally, precisely during this period that Karl August von Hardenberg visited him in Ingelfingen.

*[illustration: obverse and reverse of a commemorative medal, with a portrait bust and Latin inscription]*

Commemorative medal on the accession to government of Prince Friedrich Ludwig zu Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen in the year 1795.

*[illustration: obverse and reverse of a second commemorative medal, with a portrait bust and Latin inscription, and a scene of ships on the reverse]*

Commemorative medal on FL's appointment as Governor of Breslau in 1791.[^35]

[p. 53]

## 5 Family Letters from the Archive as Sources

### 5.1 Preliminary Remark

A brief note on the letters as a whole: the Neuenstein archive contains numerous letters written by FL and Marianne to members of the family. The majority of those composed by FL are addressed to the firstborn son August, in the period between 1798 and 1817.

What is found in an archive is often a mixture of chance and the documentary interest of the persons concerned, or of the archivists themselves. In our case there is the peculiarity that "occupation-related" documents of FL, the high-ranking military officer, were sorted out and taken over by the Prussian state after his death in Upper Silesia. Occasionally there are copies or preliminary drafts of these in the archives of the Kalliope network (a union catalogue of German archival finding aids) that could be used. – On the whole, however, the state of the sources is quite excellent and also extensive. A picture of the two persons, FL and Marianne, can indeed be gained from the letters. (I hope, in any case, that readers of my selection will find this to be so.) Where a letter yields quite essential elements for the outward progress of the biography, the information has been integrated into the running text. The selected "family letters" are reproduced in five bundles (Chapters Five, Eight, 17, and 19), with attention paid to their chronological context.

### 5.2 Family Letters I (Marianne to FL)

Several letters survive from Marianne, which she wrote in French to FL, her "*Cher Ami*", in 1792.[^36] By this time they had been married for ten years, had three living children, and their residence was

[p. 54]

Breslau. Marianne writes from Berlin, where she is staying — presumably with the children and the tutor and private teacher Kahlert — in her parents' palace. The first letter, of 8 February, is reproduced here in translation with its first page:

> *My dear Friend. The letter I received yesterday, which testifies to your remembrance, your friendship, and your well-being, has overwhelmed me with joy, and I offer you my heartfelt thanks. I longed for news of you — my health remains good — and it would hardly be fitting to be ill in this constant whirl in which I must live here — I am treated very well in the city, as at court — so that I have nothing left to wish for. Nevertheless, I assure you that at your first sign I shall leave this region without regret, and shall be glad to follow you wherever it seems fitting to you. As for the rest, it is not on account of the French quintessence [la Quinte essence francoise] that you will have with you that I regret having left your country. And however courteous and honorable your proposal is, to make small purchases here and there in passing [en passant], I confess to you that I hold too low an opinion of my talent for buying to boast of it. And on the other hand, I possess too much patriotism to venture beyond the German border. — So there, my dear Friend. Do with your French colony as seems good to you — but be assured that my heart feels the loss of being far from you, your parents, and your excellent sister, the latter in despair over her renewed affliction of the arm and grieved that the suffering has increased, so that I reproach myself — I fear that the purchases she made for me, in order to accompany me, may have contributed to it. I beg you to convey my thousand greetings [...].*

[p. 55]

Not a great deal emerges from this small piece: FL has written to her and encouraged her to spend money. She evidently holds FL's sister, Sophie Christiane, in great affection. In Berlin, including at court, she is treated well. She is invited out, goes dancing, and has many acquaintances.

All this seems to have little to do with high politics. But perhaps further reports were of significance to FL nonetheless — perhaps he had even asked her to pay particular attention to certain matters. For Marianne reported not only society gossip but also, in part, on persons and circumstances of particular interest to him, especially now that he had taken part in the meeting at Pillnitz as a member of the King's council of advisers. There the subject had been revolutionary France and how to deal with it — out of which came the Wars of the Coalition.

The royal house was always of interest. Marianne repeatedly mentioned the Duke of B. (sic, meaning the Duke of Brunswick). He would, according to Marianne, meet with the King in Potsdam and afterward travel to Vienna (there he was to discuss with allied Austria the campaign against France). At the same time, however, it seemed that in Berlin the power of another man in military matters was increasing. The Duke was far too entangled in all manner of business! Numerous other names were mentioned, among them that of Prince Louis Ferdinand, with whom she got on well.

Shortly afterward, on 3 March 1792, she wrote another letter. The opening of the letter is exceedingly formal, as, in my view, is still very apparent even in Monika Radermacher's translation (Marianne has now been staying in the Osten-Sacken household in Berlin for four weeks):

> *My dear Friend!*  
> *Your two letters have given me great joy, and the proofs of your trust, your friendship, and the kindness with which you wish to tell me everything that concerns you flatter me and touch me*

[p. 56]

> *most deeply. You know well, my Friend, that my heart knows how to value such conduct — so mine rejoices, in a burning wish to be able to be of use to you, to prove all its gratitude, its feelings, and its desire to be good for something to you.*

The letter then became quite concrete after all: she found the plans he had submitted very well considered, but did not know to whom she should turn:

> *I await your commands in this matter. Your plan for your return is the one that comes closest to my wishes [...]. May God keep you in your good resolve to return in the course of this month. I confess to you honestly that I believe you would do well to hold firm on every point of this plan.*

Where was FL? Presumably in Ansbach-Bayreuth, or even Ingelfingen. Marianne was

> *convinced that people wish to see you pressed toward your own rule. Persons who occupy themselves with envying you and doing you harm seize upon this motive to prove that you are little satisfied with what you have. [...] For now everyone is saying that you are aspiring to the government of Ansbach; it is even claimed in Silesia [...].*

One may conclude from this that his further career was a topic of discussion in Berlin. What plans, then, was he himself pursuing? In the end this proved of no consequence, however, for he was very soon obliged to march against France in the War of the Coalition under the supreme command of the Duke of Brunswick. And he eventually became Governor of Ansbach, and later of Breslau (Silesia) as well — the latter being a residence that Marianne, too, wished for. — Further on in the

[p. 57]

letter, she again reported on a theatrical performance, sent greetings from the relatives, and was quite excited, since her parents were giving a ball that evening.

The children were cared for, not only during these Berlin weeks, by Herr Kahlert, who would later work as a private scholar (or professor) with his own family in Breslau. When Marianne once made a journey from Silesia via Prague to Ingelfingen in 1791, the children were already there with him at their grandmother Osten-Sacken's. In this connection Marianne expressed herself on the rigid dress regulations and condemned the wearing of a corset by the girls. When the Coalition Wars against France then broke out in 1792, FL sent Marianne with the children from Breslau to Ingelfingen, to his father Prince Heinrich August, who was still reigning there, and gave him 1200 florins as a contribution toward their board.
