It was thought that they would soon be victorious in Paris — "Paris would be splendid," Marianne wrote. Yet in the spring of 1793 FL was still in a field camp near Kaiserslautern. Marianne was able to visit him. In a letter to the tutor Kahlert she wrote:

> *I found the military circle heroically healthy. Nowhere does one hear less talk of war than in the army. The military sojourn here is truly most delightful. The camp on the height makes a splendid effect.*[^37]

Nowadays one is astonished at such remarks. The campaign was still continuing in the year 1794. FL suffered in Wiesbaden, or rather in the camp at Pfeddersheim, from a protracted throat inflammation; Marianne traveled there and stayed with him until his recovery. The children are in Wiesbaden with the tutor Kahlert. Walluf, the birthplace of the daughter Auguste, born on 16 November 1793, is near Wiesbaden. This place otherwise never plays a role again.

[p. 58]

### 5.3 Family Letters II (FL to his young children, 1798–1806)

Letters from Marianne to the children perhaps did not exist at all before 1810. She was constantly together with them until the year of separation, 1799. Only more than ten years later does a correspondence begin with August, who is by then an adult and himself Prince of Hohenlohe-Öhringen (see p. 88f.).

With the letters from FL that follow, I also attempt to narrow down somewhat the date of the mother's departure from Breslau. FL wrote a short but informative letter to his twelve-year-old daughter Adele on 13 February 1799 from Öhringen to Breslau, in his own hand.[^38] He thanks her for the good wishes and also for the drawings, "with which you gladdened me on my birthday [31 January]." He asks her to tell Mama that he has not yet received any further letters from Breslau

> *than the ones I have answered by the packet addressed to her. I shall remain here until the 15th of this [month], then go to Ingelfingen and stay there. I am here overwhelmed with grace and kindness. In Ingelfingen several visits await me, among which the young gentleman son will probably also be. V. Stetten is still in Asbach, will probably return these days. If the weather with you is as dry and moderately cold as it is here, do not forget to take vigorous exercise. As to the fulfillment of your assurance that you will conduct yourself well, I remain the more confident of it, since you yourself are now able to see the benefit of it. Of my true love you may be assured [...]. Keep your love for your faithful father [signature of Friedrich Ludwig].*

From Ingelfingen FL still had to deal with the purchase of the Mainz share in the town of Niedernhall. That was to take up time.

[p. 59]

"Mama" states later in the divorce record that she left "during an absence of her husband." No exact date for this step can be found in the documents. It probably took place in the summer of 1799, since the so-called Transact, an extensive agreement on the separation that had to satisfy legal requirements, was finished in November. As late as the end of March FL was writing a letter to his son, meaning the mother must still have been together with the children at that time.

The theme of health and the exhortation to good conduct belong from the outset to the repertoire of such letters, as we shall see from those he sent to the fourteen-year-old August:

Of the letters, the first dates from early 1798, from Ansbach (where FL had his headquarters for a time), and probably went to Breslau; the second, of 26 March 1799, went from Öhringen to Berlin or perhaps to Breslau.[^39] He dictated both to his aide-de-camp von Stetten; from FL's own hand comes a brief postscript in each case. Von Stetten has, not only here, a legible hand, but occasionally an idiosyncratic orthography and word order, which I have, as with the other letters, largely retained. — In the letter from Ansbach (1798), the subject is first of all news of the improvement in the boy's health:

> *My dear son. However much the news I had received of your health had until now worried me, all the more it pleases me now to be assured, both by Mr. Kahlert and by yourself, of your complete recovery. Without becoming soft on that account, do nevertheless take care of your health; it is all the more necessary since I hear that you are growing strongly, and nature requires additional strength for this development. That you ride diligently pleases me, it will be beneficial to your health. That GrosMama gives you proofs of her gracious benevolence on every occasion pleases me; seek always to deserve [sic] this disposition through diligence and good conduct. Greet all your siblings*

[p. 60]

> *from me, I look forward to seeing you again soon. Emily's [the sister four years younger] Unfortunately I heard should also now shortly be lifted [i.e., cured], since the cause of it has been recognized. To Mr. Kahlert I myself give thanks today for the care he has had for you, which you will certainly recognize with gratitude as I do. Farewell, dear son, with a faithful heart loves you your faithful father [signature of Friedrich Ludwig]./Postscript: embrace dear Mama and Aunt for me. To the latter I give thanks most humbly for her two letters, which I received here yesterday together, through Schulenburg.*

To be noted here is the concern for the health of the boy, about which he had previously received less good news. But from whom? There must therefore also have been some other channel of correspondence. The generosity of the grandmother, Princess Sacken, was to be maintained by her throughout her life. Something similar held for the contact with the tutor Kahlert, which lasted until FL's death, thus long beyond his period as tutor.

The further letter to August bears the date 26 March 1799:

> *My dear son. I have just now received your last letter and am very grateful to you for it. It pleases me that your health is good again and that the little feverish attacks have had no consequences. I trust that you will contribute your own share to the maintenance of your health. Give Mama and Aunt many regards from me, and I am pleased that the former is quite well, and the latter is again feeling better. Embrace all your siblings in my name. To the Countess [Massancourt], to Mr. Kahlert, Bally, Glenck many compliments; to Miss Stetten and Count Schulenburg I wish from my heart good and speedy recovery. If you see Mr. von Triebenfeld, give him much [...] from me. For the Hohenlohe lands I have for some time been very worried, since*

[p. 61]

> *a visit from the French has threatened us more closely than ever before. But now, thank God, the danger seems for the moment to have passed again, since I received yesterday the definite news that the French army under General Jourdan has been totally defeated and will probably be forced to retreat across the Rhine. — Farewell, dear son, and continue by your conduct and diligence to give your parents joy; proud am I then with a faithful heart, your [signature of Friedrich Ludwig] faithful father./P.S. send the enclosure at once to War Councillor v. Triebenfeld*

Again, health stood at the beginning. The list of persons mentioned was long: Mama and Aunt (could this possibly mean the Countess M. as Mama's companion?). Does Fräulein Stetten have anything to do with the aide-de-camp? Kahlert has been clarified, Bally is the equerry, responsible for horses and carriages and "always" (and for many more years to come) with FL; Glenck is new. The spelling with "ck" suggests it must be the son Glenck (see p. 129f.), who is said elsewhere to have still been studying in Freiberg in 1799. He later became FL's personal secretary and could therefore already have been in Breslau. That the letter went there thus seems clear. They are all there together, and Mama is also (still) there at the end of March. – Triebenfeld is to be handed an "enclosure." He is FL's finance and property manager (see p. 143f.).

Since "Mama" Marianne is still there, and the power of attorney for drafting the Transact for Princess Sacken mentioned above bears the date of 19 April, it may be assumed that she made her decision to leave, at least, in April as well. When she actually left Breslau we do not know. It must have been at a time when the Prince was absent.

It appears noteworthy that FL apparently began here to inform his Hereditary Prince of news from the war then in progress between the Austrians and France (the War of the Second Coalition):

[p. 62]

Indeed, a few days before the date of the letter, on Maundy Thursday, 21 March 1799, the vastly superior Austrians under the command of the Archduke of Austria had defeated the French army under Jourdan at Ostrach near Sigmaringen, and then again four days later at Stockach. – In the long run this could do nothing to harm France's, or rather Napoleon's, ambitions.

Inserted here now is a letter in FL's own hand, written only a few years later, in September of the year 1806. It is a mixture of private matters and high politics (which will not become a topic here until after several further chapters). The son is already his successor and resides in Öhringen:

> *Dresden, 18 Sept. 1806/Dearest Son!/I thank you for your letter [written] soon after this arrival home from Bayreuth. This is the only one I have received from [her] up to now. Adele finds here my thanks for her dear letter from Kirchberg of the 8th, which I received the day before yesterday. From my sister I have likewise received a letter, for which I am most grateful. So too the first letters from Count v. Stetten of the 8th and 10th of this [month], which were agreeable and instructive to me. Do not leave me without frequent news in my love and cares for you all, make yourselves worthy of this effort. You will by now have been in Stuttgart, and I hope to be informed about what has unfortunately now come to pass regarding the Württemberg annexation. From the King of Württemberg I received the enclosure; I sent it to the King in Berlin and asked him for orders and advice as to how I should reply. I sent this by courier, this very night. I am sending you the letter itself in the original; both are to be kept among the files. My reply to the King of Württemberg you will receive in copy; seal it and see that it is sent off safely. Now act further according to circumstances, with good counsel. We have indeed foreseen the*

[p. 63]

> *most of the cases. The wheel is turning mightily. God grant that I may act happily and effectively toward the liberation of good people. I stand at the head of the Prussian-Saxon army. Of the latter the Elector entrusted me yesterday with the supreme command. All the troops are full of courage, of the best will, and the Prussians are excellently equipped with horses for this. Bally has arrived with my equipage and everything is in good order and is only being completed still. Here the court treats me well. The King honors me with full confidence. The Queen and the Princesses in Berlin have shown me a rare degree of esteem. The King honored me with distinctions and many tokens of favor. I lodged in the Palace, in Frederick II's own rooms, had my own royal attendance, and equipage with Kurnen [...]. I was distinguished more than ever, and more than I expected. I was consulted above all on political and military matters, au fait and with great candor; my advice was heeded, and the present arrangements are to my liking. Princess Solms is here, and goes tomorrow to Berlin. At the latest, I shall on the 24th [...] take up my quarters in Freiberg. You had best address your letters directly by hand to the valet Tanesi in Bayreuth. Tauentzien is and remains, as I wish, in Hof. Greet everyone from me. God keep you all in health and blessing. I hope, and wish, for a swift reunion. Your loyal Father [signature of Friedrich Ludwig]*[^40]

This letter is the last one available before numerous discussions among the political and military leaders of the allied Prussian and Saxon army in Jena and other places, and indeed also before the defeat at Jena and Auerstädt. Private matters nevertheless still occur. Hohenlohe is by now already part of the Kingdom of Württemberg. But before all this comes to pass, the account must once more turn back to "the Ingelfingen years."

[p. 64]

## 6 From Ingelfingen to Öhringen and Breslau

### 6.1 Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen

When FL had become reigning Prince in 1796, following his father's death, he introduced himself with the following declaration of government:

> *We, Friedrich Ludwig, reigning Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, Count of Gleichen, Lord of Langenburg and Kranichfeld etc., General of Cavalry of His Imperial Majesty and of the Holy Roman Empire; His Royal Majesty of Prussia's most graciously appointed Lieutenant-General of His Armies; Inspector-General of the Lower Silesian Infantry, likewise Inspector-General of the royal troops in the Franconian Principalities, Governor of the royal capital and residence city and fortress of Breslau, Colonel-Proprietor of an infantry regiment and Knight of the Great Royal Prussian Order of the Black and Red Eagle and of other Orders, do hereby extend to Our dear and loyal subjects Our greeting.*[^41]

He had no greater or more heartfelt wish than "to further the welfare of Our dear and loyal subjects and to bring it to the highest degree of perfection possible given their circumstances and Our powers." By "their circumstances" he presumably means the rather poor winegrowers, so dependent on the annual course of the weather, and by "Our powers" his own finances, which — beyond his salary as a general — likewise depended in large part on the winegrowers' dues, that is, on the grape harvest.

[p. 65]

In his new official function as "head of state," FL also occupied himself — at least a little — with the ideas of the French Revolution:

> *The various new theories are mostly ideals of a constitution as it ought to be in its highest perfection, and as a norm for legislation for man in the social condition, wholly unusable!*[^42]

His sovereign laws were meant to be "fulfilled and affirmed in their lawfulness and their intended benefit." This purpose was served, among other things, by a printed official gazette distributed free of charge. In this programmatic text he also addresses his frequent absence as a Prussian general and calls upon his administration, the "Councillors," to cooperate with the Prince of Hohenlohe-Öhringen, his uncle. It continues:

> *To Our government We commend justice, equity, loving treatment of the subjects, and honest zeal for the common good. We are pleased to be convinced that Our loyal servants, in the exercise of these duties, have regard for the rights of man, while at the same time watching strictly over the laws.*

This declaration of government concludes with three main points:

> *In all Our ordinances We shall always chiefly have regard to this: that 1. agriculture be promoted, 2. Industry be increased, 3. Commerce be enlarged and multiplied, because We recognize these as the most productive sources.*

[p. 66]

In later times there were few such texts from him addressing matters of fundamental importance. In this he was quite unlike his companion in the Prussian army, Colonel von Massenbach (see Chapter 22 on this). At most, among the surviving texts, one might place here his remarks and thoughts on the politico-military balance in Europe, more a strategic memorandum. He set these out in a letter to the publicist Böttiger, with whom he was evidently on friendly terms, in 1805.[^43]

Every residence had a court household attached to it; it could be lavish or modest — in the case of Count Christian Kraft and FL's father, Heinrich August, probably rather modest. For 1738, that is, during the father's reign, a register of the court personnel survives listing 102 persons. When FL moved his (military) headquarters from Ansbach in 1796 to his seat of government (which was, at the time, Ingelfingen), this required a considerable expansion of the court household there. In these times of relative economic hardship, this also had an effect on the prices of foodstuffs, especially meat; every week, slaughter cattle had to be bought up from the Margraviate of Ansbach. There was actually an export ban on this in that territory, but the government (under Hardenberg) lifted it. Karl August von Hardenberg visited FL in Ingelfingen. The two men presumably conversed about high politics, but also about the plans, already under way, for the economic development of the residence town — Alexander von Humboldt, too, was there once.

But already in 1796 FL received a new command, again in Breslau. He moved his court household there as well. Many of the Ingelfingen servants were left behind without employment; they received a temporary continuation of their wages.

### 6.2 Friedrich Ludwig's Move to Öhringen

When Friedrich Ludwig gave up — or was compelled to give up — the residence in Ingelfingen in 1805/06 for dynastic reasons (the princely uncle in Öhringen had died without

[p. 67]

descendants after the early death of his son), and the administration moved to Öhringen, where the Feudal Court (Lehnhof) was also located, Ingelfingen fell into great anxiety. The citizenry drew up a petition to the "Most Serene Imperial Prince, most gracious Prince and Lord!"[^44] The letter is long and detailed, full of the stereotyped phrases customary at the time. It concerns the Prince's past services to his residence town, a reverent thanks

> *for the most exalted arrangements [...] whereby His Highness had laudably striven to raise the prosperity of the local town through the establishment of various trades and the reception of artists and craftsmen [...] most respectful attachment, the continuance of His good and most gracious sentiments, most humbly and obediently [...].*

It is not entirely easy to infer, from documents of this kind, genuinely collective veneration on the part of the Ingelfingen subjects. Again and again the sources point out how satisfied they were said to have been with their lord (the "Much-Beloved"), how affable the Prince was said to have been — but whether corresponding feelings are honestly expressed in letters whose vocabulary was, after all, ultimately prescribed, whether the formulas express a genuine sentiment? Perhaps it is indeed so! — This same problem applies equally to the ornate homage texts that adorn the house inscriptions in Ingelfingen (p. 41).

Be that as it may, the Prince answered the citizenry's petition in January 1806 — from his headquarters at Gera: his letter is of course composed in a similar style:

> *[...] deeply moved, proof of the attachment of My loyal subjects, tireless endeavor, [...] I shall certainly continue to take care that My preference for My paternal and*

[p. 68]

> *enduring residence town remains perceptible to the good people of Ingelfingen. [...] I continue to bear my name of Ingelfingen, since it is under this name that I have become particularly well known in the world.*[^45]

In substance he neither wishes nor, presumably, is able to reverse the matter; it remains at "affording security, [...] preserving prosperity, [...] and also improving it."

For the former residence, the facts remain: everything will now proceed from Öhringen. On one point the Prince can meet the wishes of the people of Ingelfingen. In their reply they had asked that the Prince release the rooms in the town hall that Count Christian Kraft had occupied when he moved to Ingelfingen (about 100 years earlier). These rooms, they said, were needed for the many tasks now to be handled in the town hall. FL agrees to the repurchase at the price then paid, 380 florins.

In the summer of 1806 the moment came when the obligation to take over Öhringen was solemnly carried out before the public. His son August had fetched him from Gera, and together they traveled from Ingelfingen via Künzelsau to Öhringen. There the reception had been festively prepared. FL received the key to the town from the magistrate.

Only a short time later, everything changed. He had his firstborn, August, declared of age and transferred the princely title to him. The latter was carried out on 23 August. He thereby resigned as sovereign lord. The dissolution of the small states called "mediatization," through the "Confederation of the Rhine Act" dated 12 July 1806, then also affected Hohenlohe. Hereditary Prince August had already foreseen this in January 1806: "My opinion is that sooner or later it will certainly happen. — If only lasting peace could be bought for our poor German fatherland through the abolition of the small states!"[^46] How reasonable that sounds! — Incidentally: along with the princely title, August also took over his father's considerable household debts and private liabilities!

[p. 69]

Could it be that FL's strength lay not in battles but in the leading of armies; in the display of military strength, not its deployment; in negotiation, as at Frankfurt and in achieving Hohenlohe's neutrality? Or was it that his dreams, which he could make credible as his intentions, were able to convince many people? Did he possess persuasive power and organizational talent? His positions of leadership cannot have been conferred on him without reason.

And even when he disagreed with orders or particular courses of action — for example, those of the Duke of Brunswick or even orders of the King (as happened repeatedly in 1805/06) — he behaved correctly. This was probably not always easy for him. For he once formulated (1805), quite as a complaint, that he was

> *systematically kept in complete uncertainty regarding all the major measures for the protection of the Prussian state and regarding knowledge of the theaters of war. We are dealing with an enemy whom I do not love, but whom I must respect, because he understands war. I neither wish nor can appear, in the eyes of my contemporaries and of posterity, as a general who presumes to wage war without any fixed points, who does not understand the art of incorporating fortresses into his operations, of giving his army strength and his operations solidity. It is a great error that the strategic importance of Erfurt has not long since been taken to heart.*[^47]

V. Marwitz reported that at such a moment (upon receiving an order that went against him) he "struck his calf with a small stick that he was accustomed to carry, which he did only in the most violent agitation."[^48] By his complaint FL meant chiefly the Duke of Brunswick, about whose strategies almost all commentators on the events of the

[p. 70]

following years have written little that is good — see on this also Kahlert's letters to FL (chap. 16).

### 6.3 Excursus II: Ingelfingen Castle[^49]

What, then, became of the Ingelfingen residence? It continued to be used until the middle of the century as an outpost of the princely administration at Öhringen. Only a few servants still lived here, such as the housekeeper Louise Breitschwert and the castle gatekeeper, the widow Wirth — the castle was thus rather empty. In 1843 a Cavalry Captain (Rittmeister) von Schlumbach died, "departed from the residency of the castle." It may be that he was the last representative of the administration.

For a few years beginning in 1843, at the wish of his father Prince August II, the latter's firstborn son Friedrich Ludwig Eugen lived in the castle with his wife Mathilde von Brauneck. There the couple's two sons were born, Kraft August (1844) and Alexander (1846). They were the last two "genuine" Ingelfingers among the members of the various Hohenlohe lines. The family soon moved back to Stuttgart. (The King had long since established a duty of residence there for the mediatized nobility.) The castle (and the castle garden, which was used as a nursery) were let out from Öhringen until the middle of the twentieth century. It was then taken over by the town of Ingelfingen, renovated, and converted into the town hall of Ingelfingen.

### 6.4 Breslau as Center of Life until 1806

In Breslau the first son, August, was born in 1784. This makes the place the center of his life, for five further children, including the two youngest, Adolph (1797) and Alexander (1798), were born there as well (Wolfgang Ludwig, the second son, died at not quite two months of age).

[p. 71]

*[illustration: reproduction of a handwritten letter, salutation "Durchlauchtigster Herzog, Gnädiger Herr!"]*

*[illustration: reproduction of a handwritten letter page, closing with signature]*

The letter of 17 July requesting that he stand godfather. As very often, a dictated draft is the basis; in FL's own hand are the greeting and the closing signature.

*[illustration: reproduction of an entry in the Ingelfingen church register, pages numbered 360–361]*

The entry discovered by archivist Andreas Volk in the Ingelfingen church register (illegible to laypersons in reproduction). The date given results from the page number.

[p. 72]

FL was, after all, frequently traveling, and so it happened — somewhat surprisingly — that the baptism of Alexander, born on 3 July 1798, was entered in the baptismal register of the parish of Ingelfingen during the period 14–21 July. Did the baptismal act actually take place there? Hardly. The newborn and the woman in confinement, together with attendants, would then have had to travel from Breslau with great speed! FL himself, however, was there at that time, for a letter survives, signed by him in Ingelfingen and dated 17 July, in which he informed the addressee of the child's birth and asked him to stand godfather.[^50]

In the entry, the Duke of Brunswick is named as an absent godfather (he was the addressee of the letter) — alongside other high-ranking gentlemen, e.g. Duke Peter of Courland,[^51] who had lent Friedrich Ludwig 300,000 thalers in 1790. Registered as present godparents were: the Princess Sacken (that is, the grandmother), Princess Sophie Christiane von Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen (the aunt), and Prince Heinrich of Anhalt. — There is no evidence of a visit by the Princess Sacken to Ingelfingen. It is possible that Pastor Knapp, who had held this office since 1768 (that is, even before FL's father's accession to government), allowed himself to be pressed by his princely employer! (assuming he was indeed the one who made the entry). A personal familiarity may be assumed.

Whether the family lived from the outset in the Schlegelberg house in Breslau, which his idol Frederick II had also used for a time (which naturally pleased him particularly), is doubtful; he purchased this town palace on the Albrechtstraße in the year 1794. He had not forgotten Ingelfingen and retained the name Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, incidentally also after he succeeded his childless uncle at Öhringen in 1805. In Prussia this played no role; there he was "Prince Hohenlohe", a regiment bore his name, and he was so styled in official documents. The palace, rebuilt in 1801, later received the designation "Haus Fürst Hohenlohe."

[p. 73]

In addition he purchased an estate at Alt-Scheitnig, in order to develop it as a residence and summer house outside the town, probably as early as 1783. The grounds comprised the properties on the eastern bank of the Old Oder. The connection to the town was made via the newly laid-out Fürstenstraße and the Fürstenbrücke. There he established himself, one might say, in feudal style, and added to the building complex the princely dwelling house, stables, coach houses, a billiard house, an orangery, and a riding ground.[^52] To this place he regularly withdrew. He had the Öhringen Court Painter J. G. Schillinger, who had also worked in Ingelfingen, paint rooms there as late as 1805. The design for the so-called Fürstengarten (Prince's Garden) was probably developed by FL himself with the help of the already renowned Breslau architect Carl Gottfried Langhans, the builder of the Brandenburg Gate in Berlin. The grounds contained a French garden and a landscape garden (the so-called "wild garden"), which merged into the adjoining municipal forest. He laid out paths and had an avenue of trees planted. In the "wild garden" the princely green spaces were not fenced in; thus they were accessible to the broad public as the town's first public park. On the occasion of one of the regular royal visits, FL had a statue of Frederick II erected, as well as antique figures. Here invitations, dancing, and musical performances alternated with one another. There is documented evidence of the performance of a play, in which he had to play the heroically conceived leading role (and thereby presumably, indirectly, himself). The Prussian King Frederick William II was also his guest. In August 1790, following a great dinner at Breslau castle, FL was visited by him with his entire entourage, which also included Goethe and Charles Augustus of Weimar[^53] — they were soon to meet again during the Coalition Wars against France.

Frederick William III was also there on several occasions, when he was in Silesia, usually in August, on his inspection tours ("reviews"); FL surprised him there in 1788, 1798, 1800, and 1804 with a particular display of splendor, lantern illumination, even with statues of the royal couple. In 1788 none other than Ditters von

[p. 74]

Dittersdorf provided the musical entertainment with a concert. One finds this in the annals of the Prussian monarchy, "Das Leben der Königin von Preußen Luise Auguste Wilhelmine Amalie"[^54] as well as in K. F. Klöden's "Lebens- und Regierungsgeschichte Friedrich Wilhelms des Dritten, Königs von Preußen"[^55]. For the "review" in the year 1800:

> *In the evening the Prince of Hohenlohe gave them a brilliant festivity in his park at Scheitnig, in which the public too could take part. A free masked ball was held in the park, and it was illuminated in the most tasteful manner, with many surprising features.*[^56]

In an extensive work, "Wanderungen durch Breslau und dessen Umgebungen nebst weiteren Ausflügen", 1836, its author Julius Krebs writes:

> *Breslau must give thanks to the Prince of Hohenlohe, who, beyond his means, supplied what the commune, given the circumstances of the time, could not yet provide — that he laid out this park with its lofty, beautiful leafy halls, and, full of humanity, granted admission to everyone.*[^57]

Mentioned are an equestrian statue of Frederick II, a column of Trajan and reproductions of antique figures, a temple at the end of the main path with a bust of Frederick William II, as well as busts of the royal couple Frederick William III and Queen Louise. The author also expresses himself critically: he considers the park "tastelessly overladen". It is to be surmised that with "beyond his means" he was alluding to FL's financial distress, which had certainly become known.

It is unknown what his personal circumstances looked like during this outwardly so splendid period. His wife had, after all, unexpectedly left him and Breslau in 1799.

[p. 75]

From Breslau, FL continued his predominantly preventive military-diplomatic duties. He came to Ingelfingen and Öhringen — rarely enough — only to attend to matters of government. The construction of the Mariannenvorstadt (Marianne's Suburb) became a success even without his presence. He had much to do with Öhringen, as the intensive administrative correspondence shows.[^58] The seniority of the Hohenlohe princes, the so-called Lehenhof (feudal court), had passed to the Kirchberg prince, while the office itself resided in Öhringen. The matters at hand were coordination among the various Hohenlohe princely houses, costs for road construction, and similar affairs.

Ingelfingen went through a grievous period between 1799 and 1801, with quite considerable burdens from the contribution demands of the army of the Franconian Circle. This encompassed the whole of northern southern Germany, under the military leadership of the Habsburg Archduke Charles, in the joint struggle against Napoleon. FL was indeed "principally" a general in Prussian service, but within his own territory of rule, that is, in Ingelfingen and its associated communities, he had to help enforce these demands.

FL had, as described earlier, worked hard for the neutrality of the Hohenlohe principalities and had even achieved it, under Prussian protection. This neutrality applied only to armed conflict in the form of combat operations, not to marches through or billeting of troops. When in 1800 levies — this time owed to France — fell due and Hohenlohe was to be drawn into the payments, the Hohenlohe princes invoked their neutrality. Indeed, with success: the French general of the Army of the Rhine, Moreau, had heard of the matter and communicated "that the land of Hohenlohe has been declared neutral by the French government [...] and is under no circumstances obliged to furnish any war contributions for the French army".[^59]

Behind this success stands, quite certainly, FL as well. He had already come to Napoleon Bonaparte's attention before this, probably on account of his

[p. 76]

military abilities and his skill in negotiation (see, for example, Frankfurt). Napoleon, who had already been his military opponent during the Wars of the Coalition, had inquired after him. When the French and German army commanders once met in Öhringen in 1805 — at that time the unofficial capital of Hohenlohe — toasts were also proposed at the banquet. FL, in his own toast, emphasized Napoleon's achievements; in substance he said, in the French language, that he hoped to make his personal acquaintance (well and good, but then followed) — in order to defeat him in battle! This was, admittedly, hardly diplomatic — and things did indeed very soon turn out quite differently.

Regarding Breslau, it may further be noted that from 1781 Joseph Christian Franz von Hohenlohe-Bartenstein (1740–1817) was active there at the episcopal cathedral church, at first as Coadjutor, that is, as representative of the bishop. The Bartenstein (also Waldenburg) line of the overall House of Hohenlohe was Catholic. This distant "cousin" of FL became Prince-Bishop of Breslau in 1775. No personal contacts are documented.

*[illustration: engraving, view of Scheitnig with river, footbridge, and boat, 1804]*

View of Scheitnig. (Copper engraving, 1804; artist unknown)[^60]

[p. 77]

## 7 Friedrich Ludwig, the Deserted Husband

### 7.1 Separation and Divorce, the Transact (1799–1804)

The reason underlying the separation of the married couple FL and Marianne remained a mystery for a long time. That it was intended to be permanent emerges from the contract drawn up by Fürstin Sacken for her daughter, the Transact. The daughter had granted her mother power of attorney for its drafting. By 9 and 19 November 1799 respectively, everything was complete. It had gone quite quickly.

This document[^61] is an extensive text — 33 densely written pages, divided into 25 paragraphs, in which everything is addressed and settled that is regarded as requiring settlement, both immediately and in the more distant future (a judicial divorce would not follow until 1804.[^62] Its documents refer repeatedly to the Transact).

Without doubt the Transact is the work of the circumspect Fürstin Sacken, though the text does state that she discussed its contents with the daughter and the son-in-law. Paragraph 25 reads:

> *Since, in the absence of Her Serene Highness the Princess of Hohenlohe, Her Serene Highness the mother, acting on behalf of Her beloved daughter, has concluded this Transact — and indeed out of considered maternal solicitude, in order to secure for the future the happiness and honor of a beloved daughter, and the happiness and honor of a beloved princely House descended from her — Her Most Serene Highness the Princess of Sacken hereby undertakes to obtain from Her beloved daughter*

[p. 78]

> *full, unconditional, and now expressly judicial adherence, in every formality entirely lawful, and according to the laws of the place of execution, to this entire Transact in the best legal form, and is convinced that the clear judgment, together now with the good dispositions of Her beloved daughter, will fully coincide with this draft prepared through maternal representation. Should, however, contrary to expectation, the Princess of Hohenlohe be unwilling to make the present Transact her own, and should she instead withhold the consent which Her Serene Highness the mother has thus undertaken to obtain, then, for this unforeseen case, Her Serene Highness the mother, Princess of Sacken, hereby establishes the necessary consequences. [...]*

Two paragraphs follow. It is not necessary to reproduce these; the document is possibly a copy, or perhaps a draft, which bears the following closing line: "In witness whereof. Berlin, 9 Nov. 1799." No signature. It is unlikely that there was a new version or revision; this is suggested by the later, frequent references — for example in the divorce documents — to the Transact under the double date of 9/19 November. The 19th of November may be the date on which the copy signed by the three persons concerned, that is, including FL, was executed. It was with him, as mentioned in the preface, that the Princess of Sacken "spoke." On this point, the excerpt reads:

> *Thus, between the Serene Lady, the Lady Christiane Charlotte Sophie, Princess of Sacken, acting for herself and on behalf of Her beloved daughter, on the one part, and the Serene Lord, Lord Friedrich Ludwig, reigning Prince of Hohenlohe, Count of Gleichen etc., on the other part, the following Transact was discussed and concluded.*

A brief summary of the contents shall suffice here. The first paragraph is especially short and reads:

[p. 79]

> *His Most Serene Highness and His Most Serene Consort, Their Serene Highnesses, both renounce, under the present circumstances, any claim to personal reunion, and mutually consent to live apart from one another, insofar as, in the course of time, the free and joint will of both should not bring this now-necessary separation to an end or annul it.*

Paragraph Two settles the daughter's private allowance. She is to receive 7,000 reichsthalers annually, of which 400 come from the revenues of the numerous estates, and 3,000 directly from the mother.

In Paragraph Three: the daughter receives Schloss Oppurg and its appurtenances.

In the fourth paragraph the Prince leaves to his wife, "for her free disposal and ownership," her jewelry and toilette, library, engravings and materials, a team of horses, a riding horse together with a cob, and two good "closed" carriages, namely one single-seat and two double-seat carriages. Reference is made to the case of inheritance. The young Princess has not yet decided "to make Oppurg her permanent residence." Nevertheless it is stipulated "that, for the formation of her household, the Princess's two chambermaids, her Chamberlain Günther, and the remaining personnel required for service, kitchen, and stable shall depart with her."

Further paragraphs settle the financial provision for the children and grandchildren (of Fürstin Sacken), directed first at the eldest (August), who by right of primogeniture is to become his father's successor, but then also at his brothers and sisters. The costs and revenues of the Silesian and Saxon possessions (though not the South Prussian ones) are set out. Slaventzitz ("consisting of eight villages and 2 colonies, as well as 6 outlying farms"), originally the property of the daughter's father, Count von Hoym, is ceded to FL. It had "already been held in ownership by virtue of marital usufruct."

[p. 80]

Paragraph 13 refers to the marriage contract of 1781 in Leipzig and to two further agreements made in Ingelfingen in 1782 and 1784, the "so-called Ingelfingen Final Testamentary Ordinances." Both parties undertake, "once this Transact has been fully substantiated, to annul the latter."

Further paragraphs again concern provisions for the children and grandchildren; the three princesses, for instance, are each to receive 25,000 reichsthalers as a marriage portion upon their marriage. As regards the sons, FL retains the right to observe the Hohenlohe house laws and, importantly, to provide for "natural and rank-appropriate maintenance and the flourishing of the House." This also includes an inventory of his "own immovable allodial estate [...] at present, insofar as it may be devoted by His Highness to the entail."

This inventory of the year 1799 can readily be compared with later ones, which Triebenfeld, FL's General Plenipotentiary, repeatedly submitted. The mere listing of assets, of course, says nothing as to whether loans still remained to be repaid for their acquisition — and it is from this that FL's greatest problem would soon arise.

*I. In the house at Breslau. II. In the park laid out near Breslau and a house and garden recently purchased in addition to it. III. In the South Prussian estates and manors, namely the Conin estates, the Cujavian and Meseritz estates, consisting of three towns, one hundred and thirty villages, twenty-one manorial farms,[^63] as well as land and twenty sheepfolds, twenty-three mills and ironworks, six brickworks, and thirteen [...] and breweries.*

"The flourishing of the House" remains the guiding phrase of the following sections as well. The "family happiness" thereby defined is invoked repeatedly, as is the welfare of the descendants. The text contains neither the concept of "guilt" nor any

[p. 81]

trace of a resentful attitude. Not a syllable addresses a reason for the separation. That would not have accorded with the style of the whole, nor perhaps with the fundamental behavioral pattern of this social class. What mattered was honor, and also pride.

Marianne's marriage, soon after the separation, to Count Friedrich von Osten-Sacken (later a colonel in Grand Ducal Mecklenburg service) and the birth of their first child together, Angelica, in 1802, do not warrant any speculation about a pre-existing relationship with the new partner, who, as a distant relative, presumably came and went at her mother's house. The marriage to the man fifteen years her junior presumably took place in 1801. Why the formalities of the divorce from FL were not notarized until 1804 cannot be established.

Only at the end of the twentieth century was the already-mentioned statement on the separation from the year 1811 discovered (see p. 83f.). A look at the divorce documents is significant in this connection. They bear the date of 6 August 1804. Two documents, each bearing a seal and officially countersigned, in the hand of a scribe ("I" refers throughout to Marianne), stated the following:

> *Doc. 1: Since I well understand that, through my departure — undertaken of my own accord in the year 1799, during my husband's absence from Breslau, without any occasion given by him for it, and with the intention of not returning to him — and through the separation now continued for five years, I have made any reunion and restoration of good relations with him impossible, and have given the most well-founded occasion for a suit for divorce to be brought against me [...] [pending]; I hereby declare, after due deliberation, that in the event my husband shall bring, before the court whose jurisdiction I acknowledge, a suit directed toward the complete dissolution of our marriage as it has existed, and shall not only consent to a judicial separation of our marriage but shall also wish to declare me the party solely at fault by reason of the aforesaid willful departure, I shall likewise readily submit to the statutory penalty of divorce with respect to property, and shall abide by whatever may be determined concerning the upbringing and provision for our children —

[p. 82]

> unless a settlement (Transact) has already been made concerning this, which I hereby not only repeatedly declare to be legally valid and binding, but shall also acknowledge once more before the court when answering the suit. In particular I bind myself once more hereby that, after the judicial dissolution of the marriage has taken effect, I shall leave to my husband, without any interference on my part, the right of upbringing of the princes and princesses, solely and exclusively. I likewise promise, in accordance with my husband's wish, in general not to reside in the same place as he, especially not in Berlin when he is present there, nor in Breslau and the Hohenlohe lands, save with his express permission.*

In another hand, presumably Marianne's own, but on the same document: "Done at Rheinsberg, 6 August 1804 / I approve the contents / Amalie Marianne Louise Charlotte Christiane / of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, born Countess Hoym." Here, then, is mention of guilt. This, however, refers exclusively to "the willful departure," not to its grounds. There must, after all, have been some cause for the departure.

> *Doc. 2: Since, with regard to the provisions contained in the settlement (Transact) of 19 November 1799 concerning the Saxon estates belonging to me, certain formal requirements remain still to be met, which have not yet been attended to; I therefore [...] [request] by this judicially executed [...] either*

[p. 83]

> *to repeat and confirm the provisions merely referred to, contained in the settlement of 19 November 1799 concerning the Saxon estates, in a separate instrument to be drawn up by Electoral Saxon jurists in accordance with a specific prescription, and also that a draft form of such an instrument to be communicated to me — insofar as it agrees with the settlement of 19 November 1799 — be executed and communicated without refusal or delay in the prescribed binding form. Done at Rheinsberg, 6 August 1804 / I approve the contents / Amalie Marianne Louise Charlotte Christiane / Princess of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, born Countess Hoym*

### 7.2 "A Word of Trust"

This concerns a document of central importance to Marianne, which she composed on 12 October 1811 for particular purposes and wished to have kept separately. Since it deals with the separation, the Transact, and the divorce papers, it is reproduced here in full — this document was found by Gerlinde Kraus in the archive in Upper Silesia.[^64]

> *A word of trust to my two sons, Prince August von Hohenlohe Oehringen and Prince Adolph von Hohenlohe, and for the present in his stead to His Excellency, the Lord Minister of Justice von Kircheisen, Lord Privy Justice Councillor von Sack, and Lord Chief Councillor of Medicine Cosmar. To my son, Prince August von Hohenlohe, and to the aforementioned gentlemen guardians of my remaining children, I take the liberty, together with the enclosed declaration concerning the will of my late, dear mother, to speak yet a word of trust. Until these named persons, and my children who are still minors, have reached the years of their majority, these

[p. 84]

> *confidential lines apply to no one. I ask, therefore, that they not be placed among the papers that might fall into the hands of any third party outside this small circle./It is now twelve years since I left Prince Hohenlohe — in my view I had to take this step in order to save my children's fortune. Already on several occasions, to save the Prince, I had been **compelled** to sign, against my mother's wishes, sums of enormous amount, and yet the mass of his debts grew again, like a rooster's crop. Had I remained in relations with him for these twelve years, then — especially in the most recent period, in which every household, even one economically managed, was in any case falling into financial straits — the dissipation of my fortune would **certainly** have followed./I renounced, then, the splendor of my rank, I renounced the advantage of my station — but at the same time I also renounced the fame and the greatest happiness of my life, in that I tore myself from the midst of my children, and fled into a foreign land, exposed to the vengeance of the world, to the momentary displeasure of my mother, so beloved above all things, so deeply revered and worthy — who at that time was less informed of the various causes of my step than she later became. In the first surge of her displeasure she demanded of me — perhaps stirred by other impulses — a great many sacrifices, all of which I willingly brought her for her sake. I myself am astonished now, when I read through the Transact of 19 November 1799 and the reversals and declarations that followed it, which I was made to sign within a short span of time, at the demands that were made of me. I, mistress and rightful heir of the fine estates in Saxony and in Silesia, was not left even a small corner of my paternal ancestors' land where I might have lived quietly, separated from a world that misjudged me, but was instead shown out, with a cold hand, beyond the borders of my fathers' possessions./I know, and I knew it then just as well as I know it now, that

[p. 85]

> *no power on earth could have compelled me to sign all these documents, which were meant to be the legal detriment of my temporal well-being; I know that it would have been an easy matter for me to obtain far more favorable terms — but I signed all these papers in silent obedience. Good, transfigured mother! They did not flow from your heart, these cold demands of those documents — and so they shall not rest upon your coffin; I alone will bear all their consequences for myself! I signed all those papers, partly to reconcile my kind mother through my unconditional submission, partly because I recognized, with gratitude, that the welfare of my beloved children was thereby secured. Against this great pledge, my own welfare was cheap to me./Whatever ought to have been sacrificed **to me** — to the Princess Hohenlohe, to the heiress of the Hoym line — my mother gave away with a loving heart, for she gave it, after all, to what was dearest to her in the world, her children. The feast of reconciliation that I celebrated with my kind mother in a quiet corner soon led me back again to her warm mother's heart, and I then went, reconciled with myself, into a foreign land, in order there — as convenience demanded — separated from my loved ones, to live out my life, if possible, in peace./My mother departed into that realm which opens not to etiquette and calculation but to love and esteem — heaven gave me two children — the care of their upbringing, the untiring diligence devoted to their education, was the sole compensation — the greatest joy to a mother's heart, scorched by separation from her children — and I doubled this devotion to their education, since it is the only treasure I could gather for these poor creatures. Yet my duty as a mother commands me to uphold the peace of these my younger children with the very same warmth with which I sacrificed myself for the welfare of the children of my first marriage. All of you! have lain upon my heart, all of you! are equally dear, equally precious to my heart!/The welfare of my

[p. 86]

*youngest children suffers through the provisions of the maternal testament; should I die sooner, my husband will scarcely be able to raise them according to their rank./My son August knows his sisters – he received them with love and that brotherly benevolence – which only further marks his good, noble, and sensitive heart; and I am likewise convinced of my son Adolph – they are both too good, too noble, for a stepbrotherly trait to be feared from them toward their stepsisters, who are in any case harshly disadvantaged in every respect, and surely you cannot wish for the misfortune of your poor innocent sisters. I demand no generosity – and I shall not beg for my children – not even from their siblings – I therefore merely commend to my beloved sons my two youngest daughters, and repeat, to the satisfaction of all parties concerned, those fair proposals for settlement regarding the purchase of Bellin, which I set out to date under 22 September in my letter to Mr. O.M.R. Cosmar and detailed there more closely, to which I refer in all points./Should this proposal be approved, then I declare – this time not according to prescribed formulas, but – wholly with a free and joyful heart, that I shall then consider myself and my children entirely satisfied, and shall preserve the love of my sons for their sisters and for me in tender maternal feeling – and shall acknowledge the shared responsibility of the gentlemen curators with warm gratitude./Berlin, 12 October 1811/Marianne, Countess von der Osten-Sacken/née Countess Hoym*

Does this text fill the gap that the previous absence of an explanation for the separation has undoubtedly been? Marianne tried thereby to leave behind something that, at the chosen moment, at least the eldest could take note of. She feels the need to present her motives to a small circle of confidants and to the family.

[p. 87]

Much appears understandable. With the shared responsibility for all manner of contracts made by FL, which she too signed, and their foreseeable financial consequences for the future, she describes a critical point in the marriage. The harshness of some provisions in the estate documents appears striking (to me as well), particularly when one's own mother is responsible for them. Thus the Transact and the testament take little account of Marianne, at least in comparison with the children who are to carry forward "the flourishing of the House of Hohenlohe." Her daughters from the second marriage really do come up short – but that is an assessment of the circumstances that arose after the separation, not a justification for her step 12 years earlier. Is it really only the finances, FL's weakest point? It has, unfortunately, become apparent that she herself, in her new financial situation, for which she was responsible together with Princess Sacken, does not manage well (Bellin Castle and her complaint about Count Hardenberg, see p. 174f.). – And FL's infidelities will not have been the cause either.

From today's perspective, one may certainly state that the abandonment of the two small children Adolph and Alexander – that is, the "how" of the separation – appears unacceptable. On this point, the letter says nothing.

[p. 88]

## 8 Family Letters III (FL to August, 1800–1805)[^65]

A single letter has a letterhead. It was written, in French, in Breslau on 10 January 1803. The writer (probably not FL himself, who mostly dictated and probably did not hold the pencil) has drawn a building at the top right of the letter sheet. It is — most likely — the House of the Double Eagle at the north-east corner of the Rynek (market square); no connection to FL emerges from a historical explanation posted in the entrance there.

*[illustration: facsimile of a handwritten letter with a small sketch of a building at the top]*

[p. 89]

The next illustration shows, from another document — also a letter dictated to a scribe — FL's signature and an additional note in his own hand:

*[illustration: facsimile of a handwritten letter closing, dated Berlin 19 May 1800, with FL's signature and a handwritten postscript]*

Here he has signed "treuer Vatter" (faithful father); other formulations are "Dich treu liebender Vatter" (your faithfully loving father) or, in French, "Votre tendre et fidele Pere" (tendre: loving; fidele: faithful).

For the years 1798/99 to 1805, in which FL had no military operations to conduct — there were no armed conflicts — we nonetheless learn a good deal from the large number of letters he wrote in this private context (they are not reproduced here in their complete original text).

His domestic center was the Governor's House in Breslau. The summer house at Alt-Scheitnig (spelled "Scheidnich" in the letter) was rebuilt and extended during these years. The painter Schillinger from Öhringen had just finished his work. One room is referred to, in writing to August, as "your room." It was mainly there, and in the large garden that went with it, that he received guests — and would continue to do so only for a short time longer.

Most of the letters went to the firstborn, August. In 1800, that is, directly after the separation, he was sixteen years old and was, most probably along with his three- and four-years-younger sisters Adelheid and Emilie, in Berlin in the care of

[p. 90]

the tutor Kahlert. FL regularly asked August to give his greetings to his siblings, as well as to Herr Kahlert and Countess Massencourt, who was presumably to be regarded as Marianne's companion and lived there too. On one occasion FL asked August to show his letter to "Mama" as well.

August had no reluctance to report about Mama. In his reply, FL expressed sympathy for the rheumatic complaints reported to him. But he did not express any regret about the overall situation (the separation). When FL himself later mentioned Marianne, he spoke of "Madame Hoym" (or "Comtesse Hoym," since French was primarily the language used). In 1804 he also mentioned the fact that she had had a child — he meant the first of her two little daughters, who were born in 1802 and 1804.

FL's sister Sophie Christiane, who had come to Breslau immediately after 1799, looked after the two small boys, Adolph and Alexander, there. A "nursemaid" of Alexander's named Eleonore Born is documented for Breslau. FL regularly referred to Sophie as "votre tante" (your aunt).

In May 1803, as emerges from a letter of Privy Councillor Hermann from Öhringen to FL,[^66] she was now in Ingelfingen with the children. In 1804 FL traveled to Öhringen to see his uncle, whose health was already failing (he would die in 1805). All together ("notre famille": FL, his sister, his uncle, and the two children — the staff being additional) they made a carriage excursion to the hunting lodge of Friedrichsruhe, which in summer was also used, from Öhringen, as a residence.

FL was delighted by the children's behavior; they were six and five years old, respectively. It is unlikely, however, that this whole party also accompanied him on his visits to relatives in Langenburg and Kirchberg. His route also took him to Kupferzell and Ingelfingen, where he was, after all, still "the Prince."

Naturally, he told August about his professional duties as a high-ranking military man. He did so, this reader's impression is, also with

[p. 91]

"votre carriere" (your career) in mind, which he — unsurprisingly — envisioned as a military career. To this end it made sense to keep his son up to date on this subject, to let him know who was now serving where, in what capacity. An immense number of names came up, some of which are only partly familiar to us today: Haugwitz, Strachwitz, Treuenfels, Kockeritz, Hardenberg, Hatzfeld, Wolzogen, Wedell, Wartensleben, Zitzewitz, Tauenzien, Reitzenstein — FL knew them all.

As General and Governor of Breslau, he traveled a great deal: to Berlin and Potsdam, the center of Prussian power and administration; he thought it worth mentioning that he had lodged at Sanssouci palace. Bielefeld, Magdeburg, Erfurt, Bautzen, Chemnitz, Zwickau, Glogau — he made his inspection tours everywhere. In the south there were, in addition, Hof, Fürth, and other places in the (Prussian) Margraviate of Bayreuth (including Ansbach and Erlangen).

As a father he also took an interest in his son's studies at the University of Erlangen. He asked about the subjects Latin, mathematics, philosophy, and law. Regarding the last of these he also names a professor: Gründler (see p. 203f.). Whether August had told him this name, or whether FL had heard of him himself, is unclear — Gründler was a professor specializing in Prussian law. Elsewhere[^67] English, Latin, optics, physics, and philosophy are named as his subjects. — It remains open whether August told FL about his fellow student Eugen, Prince of Württemberg. FL may have known of him, for there had already been contacts between the two houses before.[^68] FL, for his part, repeatedly mentioned the Margravine (Caroline of Bayreuth), who resided at Erlangen Castle and was much appreciated by August as a hostess. Since, as noted, most of these letters are composed in French, she appears as "Madame le Margrawe."

[p. 92]

*[illustration: painting of the Jagdschloss Friedrichsruhe, a manor house with figures and a dog in front]*

The hunting lodge of Friedrichsruhe, today a hotel and conference center. (Painter and year unknown)

[p. 93]

## 9 After the Defeat against Napoleon in 1806

Now the good fortune that had once accompanied him, so successful before, deserted him. Two weeks after the great battle of Jena and Auerstädt was lost, he capitulated on 28 October at Prenzlau, together with the remainder of the troops he commanded, to the French. In a letter to the King — unfortunately not preserved — he resigned from his command duties.

The news of the defeat evidently did not spread so very quickly after all. The Weimar Privy Councillor Voigt informed Goethe, in a letter dated as late as 2 November 1806, that Duke Charles Augustus of Saxe-Weimar had marched with his troops as far as Havelberg and intended to hand over his command to FL! In "early November," Voigt referred, likewise in a letter to Goethe, to a French army report according to which FL had been taken prisoner with 10,000 men after the capitulation of Prenzlau. Duke Charles Augustus, with his corps, had been cut off from the Elbe.[^69]

### 9.1 Jena and Auerstädt and the Capitulation of Prenzlau

For his soldiers FL had negotiated favorable terms, but he himself and his staff were of course taken prisoner. On 3 November they were staying at Lithin. From there he himself writes (that is, in his hard-to-read hand) the following letter to his son August in Öhringen, which shall be reproduced in full and verbatim. It is of interest what all he considers worth reporting and important:

> *Lithin, 3 Nov. 1806/near Potsdam/Dearest Son! Since the Master of the Horse Bally has informed me that the two*

[p. 94]

> *grooms [G.] and [K.] wish to be discharged, I do not wish to withhold from you [...] that I must reduce the number of my horses. I have therefore given each of these grooms 5 ducats as a gift and had their monthly pay calculated through November, so that they leave quite content. Their request is that you might use your influence so that these men remain exempt from Württemberg military conscription. This is my great wish, and I hope that citing it will protect them. For the time being I am taking up residence at Liegnitz. N.S. Loncey, Pirch, Röder, Blumenstein, [Kriechenbach] send you and all of you their greetings. They share their fate with mine. Marwitz has gone to Mecklenburg to his mother-in-law. I still have with me the Cavalry Captain von [D.] as aide-de-camp. He is a fine man. Then also the engineer von Möldendorff, son of the Government President at Bayreuth. This is my entire suite. I have lost several riding horses, yet I am still well mounted. From the graze wound I received on the right arm on 14 Oct. I feel no inconvenience, apart from [...] still some swelling remaining from it. – Murat has remembered you favorably. Since I am not permitted to cross the Oder, else I would have betaken myself to Slaventzitz, where I could still have occupied myself usefully. I do not wish to leave the Prussian cavalry now, else I would have gone too soon, or I would have betaken myself to Ohrdruff. That I am a prisoner you already know from Berlin, from where Count Kapper was to inform you. I hope to receive news yet today from my mother-in-law, to whom I had sent [Ströbel]. Farewell all of you, and God keep you from all harm. To my sister, to you, to yours, to your brothers, cousins, all our lords and ladies, let there be here the assurance of my love. Ever with the greatest devotion. Your! Faithful Father [...] Hohenlohe.[^70]*

[p. 95]

It is altogether remarkable that he turns first to the concerns of the grooms, then reports on the aides-de-camp, and only last speaks of himself.

FL had at first received the order to betake himself with his aides-de-camp to Silesia, his destination being Slaventzitz. On the way, however, he was ordered to go to Spandau. The aides-de-camp, except for the one, traveled on. Major Röder and Captain Blumenstein then found lodging in the Breslau residence, the former seat of the Governor. – Incidentally: Prince Jerome, Napoleon's brother, took up quarters in the summer house at Alt-Scheitnig, which had suffered only slight damage from the fighting.

### 9.2 The Situation after the Defeat at Jena and Auerstädt. – In Magdeburg and Afterward, as Described by Massenbach

The remaining army continued to march under the Prince's command toward the Oder. Its first objective was Magdeburg, where the intention was to regroup, to properly provision men and animals at last, and to establish a new order. The Duke of Württemberg laid down his command there and departed, because he could not serve under the orders of Prince Hohenlohe. This is reported by Massenbach in his "Historical Memorabilia"[^71], who adds: "Duke Eugene is said to have made this remark. I cite it as a tradition." This already illustrates something of the confusion, which is described in detail. The Prussian General Rüchel likewise simply departed in the direction of Stettin.

In the days that followed, too, there were, as before, false reports and misunderstandings of orders. The reporting system between the units had broken down. "Complete disorganization had already set in." This included the fact that Blücher had orders to hasten forward with his soldiers. He did not do so, since he could no longer subject them to further night marches.

[p. 96]

On the supply situation: "Our men suffered such a want of provisions that they plundered several villages." Count Schlippenbach

> *begged with wringing hands that the mischief be checked. Was it checked? Or rather, could it be checked? Hunger hurts! the men cried, when one wished to hold them back. And the officers felt only too keenly that hunger hurt.*

Fischer then writes of this situation:

> *The disorder spread ever further. On the night of the 21st to the 22nd [of October] a hussar had committed an assault upon his cavalry captain and wounded him with his sabre. Hard as it was for the Prince, he had to have him shot, and seized the occasion to speak out most emphatically at the parole against the spreading indiscipline. Yet the example no longer served as a deterrent; the sharpest words, as the officers' conduct immediately afterward showed, no longer bore any fruit.[^72]*

At or near Prenzlau, Massenbach committed an error that was later cited variously. After lengthy remarks on the purity of his convictions and his intentions, he himself reports on a reconnaissance:

> *I was convinced that I had encountered Marshal Lannes at b [a point on the map appended to the work, as also below], because I was convinced that I had ridden across the Seehausen bridge. I was mistaken; and I did not recognize this error until the month of March [1807], when a friend, Captain von Schöler, drew my attention to the fact that in so short a time it would have been impossible for me to have covered so*

[p. 97]

> *long a march. – I had ridden past the Tresort mill, and had encountered Marshal Lannes at d. The enemy forces drawn up were not at c.c., but at c'.c'. Yet the enemy cavalry was in the vicinity of St Georg, as is now reliably known. I therefore saw wrongly and committed an error. [...] But the enemy was in Prenzlau; he was master of all the gates [...]. I do not excuse my error; but I maintain that it had no essential influence on our unfortunate fate.*

In this he is probably also right. – He concluded the publication in question – he wrote unceasingly – with markings of the positions of troop units on terrain maps (as above) and a designation of Prussia's fundamental errors in the face of the French Revolution.

A portrait of Christian von Massenbach, who was not only Quartermaster-General but also FL's correspondent, will be sketched in Chapter 22.

### 9.3 Prisoner in Öhringen

FL was brought to Öhringen and was held there under house arrest as a French prisoner of war, a condition he observed. This does not mean that in the following two years he remained cut off from all communication. There was an exchange of letters and also communications carried by disguised messengers. He conducted a large part of his correspondence with Glenck from here (see pp. 129f.).

His son Prince August II had taken over his former duties in Öhringen in the summer of 1806; FL remained a prisoner of the French occupation (under house arrest in Öhringen Castle) until the end of 1808. The ruler of Württemberg, Frederick I, King by Napoleon's grace since 8 February 1806, who had already taken over Hohenlohe at the beginning of 1806, compelled him (allegedly at the insistence of

[p. 98]

Napoleon) to leave his Hohenlohe homeland forthwith. He withdrew, resigned in a double sense, to Silesia (Slaventzitz). He arrived there on 4 January 1809.

The number of catastrophes was great enough: the end of his marriage; the debts; the military defeats without any prospect of rehabilitation; the certainty of no longer being needed; the quasi-banishment from Hohenlohe. This did not mean, however, that he had nothing to do in Silesia. Slaventzitz, Koschentin, and Landsberg were the estates there that still needed to be developed and that were managed by his staff. In addition there were the previously mentioned acquisitions in South Prussia, which in retrospect must clearly be classed as highly risky, and for which — along with their final financing, which was never successfully achieved — he relied on his General Plenipotentiary von Triebenfeld.

### 9.4 Interim Summary I

Friedrich Ludwig made his career in the military, where his name was "eminently well known"; he could look back on a 40-year career with many honors, and had been richly furnished with decorations. He took the liberty of enumerating much of this in his Regierungserklärung of 1796 (p. 64). In Ingelfingen he was most certainly open to advancement and improvement of economic conditions, and he also did something toward it. He was evidently loved by the citizens there and is described as affable. They were all no doubt proud of the Mariannenstraße, surely his most enduring achievement for the residential town. That, too, had cost a great deal of money. The building fund, for which Amtmann Taurinus was responsible, was often empty, even though the buildings were, for the most part, only advance-financed and then sold to their users.

The Free Imperial City of Frankfurt made him its first honorary citizen in 1795; a gift of money that had been offered to him there he declined. As commander of a Prussian

[p. 99]

troop contingent, he had brought it about—without any fighting—that the city was not occupied (and plundered) a second time during the war.

From this point on he would forever remain a co-loser of Jena and Auerstädt, and the one who, through the capitulation of Prenzlau, contributed to France's victory over Prussia. His capitulation had had a contagious effect on other units and their commanders.

It is the coming-together of dramatic events—the separation of 1799, the years from 1806 to 1808 with the withdrawal from the princely office in Öhringen and the military defeat, the quasi-banishment to Silesia—that lends this whole the character of a watershed. How, in the face of all this, could the flourishing of the House endure!

[p. 100]

## 10 Statements by Contemporaries on the Prince and the Events of His Time

To gain further insight into FL's personality, we now turn to a number of assessments by contemporaries and companions. These often, though not exclusively, concern the struggles of Prussia and Saxony against Napoleon, with the greatest significance attaching to the Battle of Jena and Auerstedt and the capitulation of the remaining troops under FL's command at Prenzlau shortly thereafter. — The authors drawn upon here as reporters write in particular on questions of his character, often only a few years after the events, and with quite varying degrees of concern for accuracy.

### 10.1 Friedrich von Cölln (1767-1820)

Friedrich von Cölln was a jurist and Prussian state official who, among other things, served after the Polish partitions as a War Councillor and administrator in Posen and in South Prussian districts from 1793 to 1803. After a checkered career, he ultimately worked as a writer.

Relevant here is his work written in the form of letters from Stettin: "Confidential Letters on the Internal Conditions of the Prussian Court since the Death of Frederick II,"[^73] which appeared as early as 1807. Incidentally, he also wrote defamatory tracts. He knew "him (FL) and his circumstances well." "He has never avoided the appearance of wrongdoing, that is true." "Hohenloh was esteemed and favored by Frederick, and this brought him renown. He has broad vision, fire, energy, talent, knowledge, and character. [...]" We know from Kahlert (chap. 16) that von Cölln knew the estate at Scheitnig. Kahlert had shown him, when he was once a guest there, the garden layout and the park. Whether this was before or after Marianne's departure is unfortunately unknown.

[p. 101]

There follows a lengthy account by von Cölln of how the defeat in the last battle is likely to have come about.

> *His enemies say that the prince has never shown character; this is proven by the story of his wife, who ran away from him and heaped shame upon him; this is proven by his so frequently circulating promissory notes, his ever-empty pockets. This is proven by those around him, whose reputation is in part decidedly bad, in part ambiguous. A Triebenfeld, a Grattauer, and others are his advisers and agents, the Jew Goldstecker his money-broker [...] How often has he proposed exchange schemes to the King between his own and the royal estates, in which he naturally sought to profit? How has he not profited from the South Prussian squandering of estates? How could the King entrust an army to a foreign prince sunk in debt, greedy for money, whose lands are in the power of the enemy?*

Then again another topic: "That the Princess Hohenlohe was a Messalina was not the prince's fault." (A "Messaline" — the expression is meant, more or less polemically, to point to a particularly dissolute life, as is attributed to Messalina, wife of the Roman emperor Claudius; in any case, a foreign-word dictionary of the 19th century does not distinguish between Messalina and Messaline. Alternatively: Messaline is also a term for a lustrous artificial silk fabric.)

> *That Hohenlohe is not a good manager of his affairs, cannot reckon accounts, and keeps no budget — this is true, but it is no proof of a bad character. That Triebenfeld is his friend, that is true; but whether this man is as bad as he is portrayed, that is not proven. He serves the prince faithfully, that I know. Whether out of ambition, or out of pure devotion? That is immaterial.*

[p. 102]

Regarding Triebenfeld, Forestry Councillor Friedrich August von Triebenfeld, we learn still more from von Cölln; he is introduced as a Major and "War Councillor" and is himself also entangled in all manner of "dealings." This relates to his activity as a supervisory official in the granting of estates in the part of Poland that had already come to Prussia after the second Polish partition. In this capacity, he is said to have received, between 1795 and 1798, eight estates with an assessed value of 51,000 thalers as a gift, which he then sold in 1802 for 750,000 thalers — he had thus provided most successfully for himself. Triebenfeld was FL's General Plenipotentiary in his difficult financial affairs — more precisely, the burden of FL's debts, which had arisen to a scarcely conceivable extent, in part because of the estates in former Poland (South Prussia).

For FL had also concerned himself with the purchase and leasing, and perhaps also speculative sale, of such estates — though without the success enjoyed by Triebenfeld. Near Zagorow in the Warta valley, he acquired an extensive manor as early as 1796 and renamed the surrounding hamlets, among others as Ingelfingen (Anielwo), and also after the first names of his children (see p. 144).

Triebenfeld was closely connected with FL for more than 20 years and concluded hereditary lease contracts there with German colonists on his behalf. He was above all FL's manager of debts. It would be a wonder if he or FL had not also had contacts with banks and bankers in these dealings; the banking house of Oppenheimer is mentioned. With the aforementioned Jew Goldstecker, apparently an art dealer or jeweler, one of FL's administrative officials had contact in Breslau around 1810. He will resurface right after FL's death in Slaventzitz. — The entire complex of the "squandering of estates" is devoted its own section (chap. 13).

### 10.2 Friedrich Buchholz (1768 – 1843)

In his book *Gallerie Preussischer Charaktere* (Gallery of Prussian Characters), published in 1808, in which the first part presented twelve "military persons,"

[p. 103]

FL came in third place.[^74] He was described in an article of ten printed pages, brief in comparison to the other persons. The overall tenor is — unfortunately — related to that of Friedrich von Cölln. Alongside rather little information of genuinely reportorial character, one notices, as with von Cölln, how often the author touches on matters that are only sparsely documented, or cannot be documented at all: "[...] we will therefore attribute it to his youth, that in the earliest period of his military career he distinguished himself only by his advantageous figure and by his virtuosity in riding." Of the same quality of communication was the statement about the marriage to the very young Countess von Hoym:

> *It is said that the Countess von Hoym did not sink into the arms of her husband as innocently as a large part of the public believed. [...] Both pursued divergent paths; and the natural consequence of this was that the prince's economic circumstances not only failed to improve, but even worsened further, and that the husband's debts, under which he nearly succumbed, in turn worked to worsen his domestic circumstances.*

How did the author know this in 1808 — do financial misery and marriage have anything to do with one another? We readers know it now!

Buchholz's portrayal of the already-mentioned War and Forestry Councillor von Triebenfeld, of whom more will be said in detail, was accurate.

Who could have advised FL to take his discharge? Buchholz mentioned this — does he mean Müffling? As a military man, FL is supposed to have lacked all strategic insight, and von Massenbach is said to have been placed at his side as the brains of the operation (Massenbach is likewise presented in Buchholz's work, on twenty-four printed pages, and in a wholly different tone). One wonders how Frederick William II, and also III, could have let FL rise so high in the military hierarchy, if he had indeed corresponded to this portrait

[p. 104]

at all. Frederick William II had him at his side at the Pillnitz talks in 1791 (the assembly of princes preceding the decision on the wars of the Coalition against France). — The author said nothing about FL's activities in Hohenlohe, who was, after all, not merely a "Prussian character," and evidently could not come to terms at all with this — admittedly — not very intellectual person. He ought to have left out his article.

### 10.3 An Unnamed Prussian Officer (1831)

There are then also quite different voices to be heard:
