> *Above all we must remember the noble, truly princely bearing and that proud composure of the warrior which never left him for a single moment - The only question that remained was: whether he was not equal to the greater upheavals of the later age, or whether he merely succumbed to circumstances, since fortune had abandoned him. Thinking too greatly ever to have regard for his own advantage, and too proud to justify errors he had committed by pointing to those of his subordinates, the much-misjudged and much-maligned Prince of Hohenlohe belongs, in any case, among the noblest natures who ever held a command staff.[^75]*

### 10.4 Friedrich August Ludwig von Marwitz

In the following extract from the "Lebensbeschreibungen" (Biographical Accounts),[^76] a number of matters are mentioned that have in part also been discussed in other sections. I have not abridged it here, in order to preserve to some degree the flow of the text. Fontane published a biographical text on him in 1863.[^77]

Von Marwitz (1777–1837) had been an officer already at a very young age and had then managed his estate. When Prussia found itself threatened by Napoleon, he decided in 1805

[p. 105]

to enter the army again, after having paid his respects to FL in Berlin in September. FL asked him whether he did not wish to march with him again (!) — there would be war.

In October he received the commission: he was assigned to the Prince as Cavalry Captain (Rittmeister) and aide-de-camp. The Prince was to arrive in Dresden on 3 November (1805).

> *Prince Hohenlohe commanded in Dresden the army, which consisted of 38 Prussian and 19 Saxon battalions and 50 Prussian and 20 Saxon squadrons, with 92 guns, the battalion numbering 700 men, the squadron 120 men, together at least 48,000 men. [...] In Dresden everything was ready for war and had the highest confidence in Prince Hohenlohe, who was known from the campaigns on the Rhine as the most enterprising of commanders. When I arrived in Dresden, our army was already fully underway on its march to Erfurt, to Hohenlohe's great vexation, since he had wished to march straight into Bohemia [...]. The Emperor [Tsar Alexander] remained in Berlin until the 10th [of November] and arrived in Dresden on the 11th. On the 12th he had a lengthy conference with Prince Hohenlohe, at the close of which we aides-de-camp were presented to him. The Emperor said: "The best compliment one could pay them is that they stand so close to Your Highness."[^78] Altogether, I have never seen a reigning sovereign who knew so well how to win over everyone as Emperor Alexander did. On the 13th he left Dresden in order to go straight through Bohemia to Olmütz [...].*

Von Marwitz did not mention that on this occasion in Dresden Tsar Alexander presented FL with the Order of St. Andrew.

> *In my post as aide-de-camp I had absolutely no duties whatsoever. There were two older aides-de-camp who had long been with the Prince, Major v. Pirch and Major v. Röder.*

[p. 106]

> *Besides these, the Prince had taken two other officers into his headquarters, Major v. Loucey, an émigré and an older man, and Captain von Blumenstein, likewise an émigré and a former engineer officer. He had been quite young at the outbreak of the Revolution, for he was now only my own age. One can imagine how I must have felt, given this total lack of occupation, after three years of a very busy life, and while my actual affairs, to my greatest pecuniary detriment, lay entirely idle. Already on the third day of my presence I went to the Prince and asked him to give me something to do. He replied that his affairs were all very well taken care of and that he had none for me. At the same time he entered into a lengthy conversation with me about our situation, in the course of which, although he did not conceal that the march to Erfurt was contrary to purpose and most distasteful to him, he nevertheless expressed his conviction that we would still come to the aid of the Austrians in time. [...] What this flanking march of 30 (German) miles was meant to signify, and where it was meant to lead, I have never been able to comprehend. [...] The Prince went on leave to his territories, and the rest of us dispersed again just as we had come. We did not part without tears, for we had all come to love him, and many of us to hold him in the highest esteem. The Prince had known how to awaken, sustain, and animate a quiet, earnest, and noble spirit at headquarters, and he was truly loved as a child loves, and held in the highest esteem, by all who surrounded him. [...] In general, for as long as I knew him, he showed a visible effort to conceal the failings of our Cabinet and to keep secret the bad news that constantly arrived — evidently in order to preserve the spirit of the army. This is why we at the army always had good — false — news. Later I saw through this; but for the time being I was completely deceived. — At last he said, in a pathetic*

[p. 107]

> *tone: "You may well imagine that the aides-de-camp I choose for myself are dearer to me than those the King sends me! But whoever has a purpose such as yours will always be welcome to me!" Only through this did it become clear to me that my arrival had been most unwelcome, and now I understood why the Prince's old aides-de-camp had treated me with such extreme coldness. Perhaps they even feared that I might be a Berlin spy sent to headquarters. But all this lasted only a short while; the Prince became very well disposed toward me, and the aides-de-camp became my friends. [...]. This [Prince Hohenlohe], [...] immediately after the dissolution of his army, had gone from Gera to his principality of Oehringen. This had been occupied by the French, who heaped flatteries upon him, which he manfully rejected. He had more or less concluded his inheritance affairs when the Act of the Confederation of the Rhine appeared, by which he and his entire House were reduced to the status of subjects of his hitherto neighbor and fellow Imperial Estate, the Duke of Württemberg, who then also did not delay, with the help of the very same French who had just been flattering him so, in taking possession of the land. Now there was no remaining for him in the Empire any longer. He declared his still-underage son to be of age, ceded the government to him, and, since he was now nothing more than a Prussian general, he departed. How many men in that time would have been capable of acting thus? I ask: is there a second example of such self-denial? The grave of Prussia already stood open! How deeply the King must have felt this devotion of his commander! — He had, moreover, already corresponded with him by means of disguised mounted messengers sent to Oehringen [...].*

Concerning a conversation held with the King in Memel, at the conclusion of peace with England (January 1807) — after Jena and Auerstädt, and after Prenzlau, and after an adventurous flight in civilian dress

[p. 108]

via Stralsund and Malmö to Königsberg — reported the following remark by the King there, as related by von Marwitz: "Sadly true enough that some did not do their duty, - but the Prince Hohenlohe, so distinguished a gentleman — I cannot comprehend how he came to that capitulation!"

From this it may be inferred that "Prenzlau" had impressed itself deeply upon the King's memory. FL was never again to be free of it. With this capitulation, and after a stubborn negotiation with the commander of the superior French forces, he had acted rightly, and had thereby saved the lives of many soldiers. It had, however, been preceded by a misjudgment of the situation on the part of von Massenbach, one that soon became known and that Massenbach himself did not gloss over even in a writing of his own. FL, for his part, had taken the responsibility upon himself before the King.[^79] — The relationship between FL and von Massenbach will be further discussed in Chapter 22.

And one further point ought to be clarified: what does Marwitz mean by the French "flatteries"? It is documented that the French, as the leading force behind the "Confederation of the Rhine", were interested in winning over as many "states" as possible for the alliance of South German territorial rulers. Hohenlohe did not belong to the Confederation of the Rhine. As a means to this end — the creation of a larger confederation of states under Napoleon's influence — the forging of dynastic ties also served this purpose, as in the marriage of Napoleon's brother Jerome to Catherine of Württemberg and that of Stephanie de Beauharnais to Charles Louis of Baden. And there is a document,[^80] according to which FL's daughters Adelheid and Louise Amalie were drawn into such considerations, with prospective marriage candidates being named! That, however, had already taken place before the defeat and the captivity.

On 13 July 1806 the "Act of the Confederation of the Rhine" had been signed in Paris. FL, who ten years before had secretly hoped for a Duchy of Franconia, had now refused — despite being offered a large territorial lordship — to join in with his Hohenlohe domain

[p. 109]

. This was evidently reckoned very much to his credit by Prussia, that is, by Marwitz. The refusal had no consequence whatsoever, since Hohenlohe was in any case annexed in 1806 by the King of Württemberg, within the framework of the reorganization (mediatization) forced through by Napoleon.

It should not be concealed that von Marwitz, in his memoir, includes a page-long characterization of Massenbach that leaves not a good hair on him.

*[illustration: portrait of von Marwitz in military uniform wearing decorations]*

Von Marwitz, in the glow of later honors. (Lithograph by F. Krüger, 1827)

[p. 110]

### 10.5 Goethe and Friedrich Ludwig

The two men lived at the same time. Goethe was born in 1749, FL in 1746, and was thus three years older than the Poet Prince. Both had become prominent, though to different degrees and in different spheres of life. Yet this latter statement does not quite hold true: both stood in close connection with the respective state leadership.

Goethe had left his native city of Frankfurt and become, in the small state of Saxe-Weimar-Eisenach, a state official close to the ruling house, even a member of the Privy Council. Duke Charles Augustus's mother had brought him there. He was closely bound to the future territorial lord. FL had already, as Hereditary Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, joined the Prussian military, had risen almost to its very top, and had a lively exchange with the governmental apparatus, including with the kings, from Frederick II through Frederick William II to Frederick William III. In their rank and their access to the politically powerful, Goethe and FL were at least similar. A Hessian and a Hohenloher had each made a career, respectively, in Saxe-Weimar-Eisenach and in Prussia.

And they did in fact meet: in the War of the First Coalition against revolutionary France (1792–1794), planned as a punitive action by the powers of Central Europe, both were present. Goethe had been asked by Duke Charles Augustus to accompany him. The Duke, though not a Prussian, served as a general of cavalry in the Prussian army, and Prussia led the coalition. Goethe thus went along with him, but as a civilian remained largely uninvolved in the fighting. In the Ardennes he was chiefly interested in questions of geology and wished to make progress on the development of his theory of colors. Even on the day of the Cannonade of Valmy (20 September 1792) he was occupied with this. He kept a diary but, out of caution, destroyed it upon returning to the Empire. He wrote his account "The Campaign in France" only in 1819, from memory.[^81] This small work has repeatedly

[p. 111]

been the subject of analysis by historians and literary scholars. It is regarded as a model for the literary use of one's own experiences, of the perspective on them, and of their significance for a reality however conceived.[^82]

One might have expected that Goethe, besides his Weimar Duke and the commander-in-chief, the Duke of Brunswick, would also mention other participants, perhaps FL as well. But of him there is no mention anywhere. And yet they must have met, for instance at the evening conversation circles in the respective field camps that Goethe describes. Charles Augustus probably introduced the two to one another; there is, however, no document in which either Goethe or FL reports on a meeting. Nevertheless, the two of them probably retained a memory of this time when they did indeed meet again later:

It was in 1806, in Jena, during a few days at the beginning of October. Napoleon's army was advancing. Some confusion prevailed in the Prussian army command over its plans of action. Things did not go, contrary to what he himself probably expected, according to FL's own conceptions, but he had to follow the orders of the now 72-year-old Duke of Brunswick, behind whom the King had placed himself. FL arrived in Jena on 2 October. On 3 October he at once gave the great banquet described below. On 4 October a council of war was held at headquarters in Erfurt, and it was resolved that the right wing of the army should be led by General von Rüchel from Eisenach, the main army by the King and the Duke of Brunswick from Erfurt, and the left wing, with the Prussian and Electoral Saxon troops, by FL from Gera. On 8 October FL was again present in Jena.

FL lodged in the castle. Duke Charles Augustus had had quarters made ready for him there. Voigt, the head of the cabinet in Weimar, had pointed out to Charles Augustus by letter that Goethe was already lodged in the castle.[^83] Goethe had only just returned in August from Karlsbad and was now there in his capacity as Weimar Commissary of Provisions. Had he concerned himself with the

[p. 112]

danger of war then prevailing? There was in any case cause to reprimand the so-called Magazine Intendant of Jena: for the next three weeks there was a shortage not only of ammunition but also of food. — Goethe met FL there regularly for several days at the midday table, in a large company. In his "Annalen" Goethe wrote of these meetings, or at least of one of them:[^84]

> *In the meantime I had moved into the side wing of the castle, in order to make room for Prince Hohenlohe, who, advancing reluctantly with his detachment of troops, would rather have wished to march against the enemy along the road to Hof. — With Colonel von Massenbach, the hothead, I had a strange scene. In him too the inclination to write got in the way of political prudence and military activity. He had composed a curious opus, nothing less than a moral manifesto against Napoleon. Everyone sensed, everyone feared, the overwhelming power of the French, and so it happened that the printer, accompanied by several members of the council, approached me, and all of them urgently begged me to refuse the printing of the manuscript submitted, which, upon the entry of the French army, would necessarily bring ruin upon the town. I had it handed over to me and found a sequence of periods, the first of which began with the words, 'Napoleon, I loved you!' The last, however, 'I hate you!' In between were expressed all the hopes and expectations that one had at first entertained of the greatness of Napoleon's character, in which one had fancied one must suppose morally human purposes in the extraordinary man, and at the end all the evil that one had had to suffer from him in recent times was reproached in the sharpest terms. With few changes one might have translated it into the vexation of a deceived lover over his unfaithful beloved, and so this essay appeared as ridiculous as it was dangerous.*

[p. 113]

*By the urging of the good people of Jena, with whom I had for so many years stood on good terms, I overstepped the law I had set myself, not to meddle in public affairs; I took the pamphlet and found the author in the spacious old rooms of the Wilhelmian Pharmacy. After renewing our acquaintance, I came forward with my protest, and had, as was to be expected, to deal with a persistent author. But I remained just as persistent a citizen, and expressed the arguments, which indeed carried sufficient weight, with eloquent vehemence, so that he finally gave way. I still remember that a tall, sturdy Prussian, apparently an aide-de-camp, stood there motionless in expression and posture, and probably wondered inwardly at the boldness of a mere citizen. Suffice it to say, I parted from the Colonel on the best of terms, weaving into my thanks all the persuasive arguments that would actually have sufficed on their own, but which now brought about a gentle reconciliation. I also waited upon further excellent men; it was on Friday, 3 October. I found Prince Louis Ferdinand vigorous and friendly, after his manner; Lieutenant-General von Grawert, Colonel von Massow, Captain Blumenstein, the latter young, half-French, friendly and trusting. At noon, dined with all of them at the table of Prince Hohenlohe.*

FL had taken a seat opposite Goethe that day. The latter was flanked by the two aides-de-camp Marwitz and Blumenstein. Blumenstein later reported on the difficulty of finding suitable topics of conversation with the famous man. Finally he asked him about the play "The Bride of Messina" — by Schiller, of course!

Blumenstein did not forget this episode. In a letter to Goethe dated 26 March 1808, he mentions that he had written a description of the

[p. 114]

Prince, which a certain von Rühle wished to have printed.[^85] Unfortunately, this work cannot be found.

Through such contemporary reports we gain an impression of the Prince's standing and his personal conduct within the military sphere, which of course always included the political sphere as well. His personality was also revealed in his activity as territorial ruler, or — sadly, quite badly — in his handling of finances (despite his training in this in early youth, see p. 20) and in his acquisitions in South Prussia, which will be discussed further below.

[p. 115]

## 11 The Resigned Prince in Upper Silesia

### 11.1 Conditions in Saxony and Silesia

Property (manor estates) and money were abundantly present in Saxony as also later in Upper Silesia (so-called seats of lordship; Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen too had property there, even if no money). Marianne's mother, Princess Christiane zu Osten-Sacken, née von Dieskau, widow of Hoym zu Droyßig, administered a large fortune there, and was, in the assessment of the historian Gerlinde Kraus, an "early-capitalist entrepreneur of the 18th/19th century".[^86] The property and the income derived from it were precisely fixed between mother and daughter as her only child; after the latter's marriage, the share of the princely husband was likewise fixed. The extensive "Transact" ultimately secured further possessions in Silesia for the House of Hohenlohe. We do not know to what extent the wealthy ladies' funds had previously also played a role in the building activities at Ingelfingen – probably not.

The mother was the dominant personality, and had indeed taken note that her daughter was married to a Hereditary Prince who was enterprising, or also reckless, in economic matters – and how he handled money. When she heard of the separation in 1799 and the subsequent intention to divorce, she was "not amused". Nevertheless, Marianne went first to her, to the parental estate or to Berlin. Mother and grandmother kept in contact with the children and grandchildren respectively. Marianne then lived on the manor estate of Bellin, and after her second marriage bore the two daughters Angelica (1802) and Auguste (1804). From Bellin or also from Berlin, both women were in lively correspondence with the children and grandchildren, above all with the grandson August in Öhringen. Marianne died in 1840 in Mecklenburg, at the neighboring Marienhof near Güstrow.

[p. 116]

### 11.2 Move to Upper Silesia

On 4 January 1809, FL, having been expelled by the King of Württemberg, arrived from Öhringen in Slaventzitz, where he was to live for about another 9 years. The palace had formerly belonged to the family of Count Hoym, that is, to the early-deceased father of his ex-wife Marianne. FL had received it, transferred to him as his own property; it was presumably free of debt — a highly important consideration for the further life of the great debtor. He maintained a small household there. He was still a Prince, at least by way of this designation as a title of address, and still a Serene Highness, but he no longer possessed any sovereign rights — only property.

His mother-in-law had taken note of the exceedingly difficult financial situation. In 1808 she wrote him a letter on the matter (thus still while he was at Öhringen):

> *We would both now have a great deal less worry if you had earlier granted me your confidence, which I so wholly deserve. Through lack of confidence you have damaged the good cause, and it can no longer now be carried out as well as it could have been before. Nevertheless I shall do what I can, for I love your honor as I love my own.[^87]*

As grandmother of his children, she evidently still regarded herself as an entrepreneur and as a responsible family member acting in his favor. In the immediate term she was indeed able to help. But the misery continued. The biographer Fischer speaks, for the years 1810/11, of "total economic ruin."

[p. 117]

*[illustration: Photograph of a restored open pavilion with columns, remnant of the destroyed Slaventzitz palace]*

Restored remnant of the destroyed palace of Slaventzitz.

*[illustration: Historical print of Schloss Slaventzitz showing the palace building with a horse-drawn carriage and figures on the drive in front]*

Schloss Slaventzitz, rebuilt by Prince August in 1837 after a fire caused by a lightning strike destroyed the original Piast castle, in which FL had lived from 1808 until his death in 1818.

[p. 118]

### 11.3 The Castle at Slaventzitz as a Retirement Seat

The castle, originally built by the Piast dynasty, FL had taken over from his wife's (Hoym) family holdings. His mother-in-law, Princess Sacken, had made attempts to secure parts of the inheritance (for example, the attempt to convert it into a so-called Fideikommiss, which made it impossible for creditors to seize it). This was the case with Slaventzitz. It was here that the letters now went, here that he received visits from former staff members and from his children. – Marianne, by then married and become Countess von der Osten-Sacken and living at Bellin Castle, was always kept informed about their visits to FL and events there, through the lively exchange of letters and through visits to her. Slaventzitz was thus his residence for old age and retirement. This says nothing — an unfortunately recurring theme — about the financial situation, which gave rise to manifold deliberations at Öhringen.[^88]

### 11.4 Friedrich Ludwig's Dreams Before and After His Time as a Military Commander

Before attempting to grasp his everyday life there, it seems appropriate to look once more at his life thus far and his dreams. He had achieved a good deal: one need only recall his Ingelfingen, with its settlement of crafts and its educational opportunities for the population — this was a lasting success. His military career and his diplomatic activity between the Peace of Basel of 1795 and 1805, a period without many armed conflicts, was likewise a success. As Governor of Breslau he had found recognition even from the Prussian King. He had had no success, so far and unfortunately not thereafter either, in promoting salt production and still less coal in his Hohenlohe — this will be discussed further in the correspondence with the Director of Salines Glenck (pp. 129f.), but ore mining in Upper Silesia was nevertheless still to become

[p. 119]

a success story, even if only for his descendants.

After the conclusion of peace at Rastatt in 1798, he had been keen to enlarge "his" Hohenlohe decisively, through the lands of Schöntal Abbey, the Teutonic Order's territory with Mergentheim, parts of the Palatinate, and of the Archbishopric of Mainz (both territories in which Hohenlohe had once held possessions in earlier times). He even, as mentioned, entertained hopes of being put forward as Duke of a newly created land of "Franconia" within the framework of the reorganization of the Empire. His emissary, or agent, in this matter was the Privy Councillor Mayer, who went as far as Karlsruhe in order to be closer at hand in Rastatt. Yet nothing came of it in writing, apart from promises; "Berlin" did nothing here. He was not a fixed quantity in the great powers' marathon of negotiations; not then, and no longer now either.

FL had not been in military service as a commander since shortly after the defeat of 1806. Precisely in this field, however, there was, at least briefly, once more serious consideration given to him:

Interestingly, in 1813, after he had already lived in Upper Silesia for a good many years, he was inwardly prepared to be called upon once again. The King had already, in 1807, entrusted Scharnhorst with reorganizing the army. What was now at stake was the King's project of creating an entirely new military order. Blücher was judged by other military men to be too old for this task, and FL was urged to inform the King of his readiness. He did so on 12 March 1813 in a long letter, whose respectful, indeed almost submissive tone strikes me as so characteristic that it shall be reproduced here word for word in its essential parts:[^89]

> *[...] I would make it a rule to await further orders, without first expecting a request for employment, nor*

[p. 120]

> *to bring myself to mind, were I not thereby exposed to the reproach of having, through my failure to report, failed to make my good will known. My duty therefore requires me to inform Your Royal Majesty that my health and strength will not hinder my activity, and that I have continually neglected nothing in order to keep myself fit for service. My sole and most eager wish is: to sacrifice my last strength in Your Majesty's service, and to prove my loyalty and usefulness with my blood. These are not empty words, but the expression of my true feeling. My intention is pure, I still feel myself capable, and my experience and example will surely be of use. It is neither pride nor greed that induces me to make this offer. — I lay claim to nothing, neither to rank nor to high salary. Employ me, Your Royal Majesty, wherever and however you wish. At any time Your Highness's commands will find me ready to obey, and I set my glory upon being able to increase, through obedience and resignation, the proofs of loyalty which I have always striven most eagerly to demonstrate. Do not, most gracious King, entirely reject my petition, and grant me the happiness of Your gracious attention, and take to heart my urgent situation, which without Your Highness's grace and support will utterly destroy me.*

A few days later he received the King's reply: "[...] the state of affairs does not permit me to give you an appointment in the army."[^90] In total it was a mere 8 lines.

"Berlin," then, again showed no inclination — and perhaps FL's final remark about his "urgent situation" had not been altogether fortunate either. That he had, at the urging of others, involved himself once more with military matters at all, turned out to be a mistake.

[p. 121]

But he still had other interests and obligations: for instance — not yet described thus far — his acquisitions in South Prussia, where, following a general offer from the King, he owned several estates, almost another domain of his own (see ch. 13). Then there was the further development of his existing possessions in Upper Silesia, where Slaventzitz had been taken over from the estate of Marianne's father, who had died young, and where Koschentin, and then also Landsberg, had additionally been acquired. Here he might perhaps be able to revive his "Ingelfingen dynamic of development and action." What, in fact, was happening there during his enforced absence?

### 11.5 On FL's Activities During His Time in Upper Silesia

His primary residence there was of course Breslau, with the summer house in Alt-Scheitnig. There FL had worked as Governor, as already described.

After 1806, as a prisoner in Öhringen, he had only limited opportunities to supervise or control his Upper Silesian estates, which he had not, after all, lost. He must therefore have had confidants "on the ground" there. In 1809 he went there again, more precisely: to Slaventzitz.

Here too, requests and information reached him from Öhringen, for instance through Privy Councillor Hermann[^91] — why, exactly? FL was, after all, responsible for nothing there. The Senior Collegii of the Hohenlohes was the Prince of Kirchberg. Nevertheless, the matters often concerned money, but also the road-building (!) from Künzelsau toward Mergentheim, and occasionally family news was included as well. The conditions in Hohenlohe therefore did not remain hidden from him. He also received reports from the Justice Chancellery of Öhringen on "the most important matters of the land"[^92] Might the administrative officials have felt much as the Salines Councillor Glenck did, who had likewise turned to FL in Slaventzitz

[p. 122]

after August, uninterested in the matter of the saline, had failed to give him a response?

Lombardini had been FL's Master of the Household in Upper Silesia since 1803.[^93] He worked in Breslau, or rather Scheitnig, and after 1809 was in Slaventzitz. In his function he was responsible "for everything" — for the staff, for guests, for the bookkeeping. In his reports appear the names of General von Grawert, von Triebenfeld, the financier/merchant/banker Goldstücker, and other persons. Among those who came to him with matters from outside were the Justice Commissioner Kippen and the works inspector König, and the forestry master Kaufmann. August had direct contact with Lombardini whenever he visited Slaventzitz.

His bookkeeping also formed the basis, in 1817, for the long list of employees with an indication of the salaries still outstanding (see appendix p. 249), naming 18 persons, headed by Bally, Hahn, and himself, who had the highest wage arrears. August would later pay them out.

Among these was also Court Councillor Hahn, who remained until at least 1815.[^94] Hahn traveled a great deal; on several occasions he reported by letter from Breslau. Among his tasks was the procurement of funds; he negotiated with the banker and jeweler Goldstücker (who, immediately after FL's death, would turn up in Slaventzitz).

The name Triebenfeld is likewise mentioned; no wonder, since the misery of the debts in South Prussia continued unabated (see the lists in the appendix, pp. 239/240).

"Bally" — the equerry and coachman — was present in Slaventzitz. He was mentioned in FL's letters of September 1806 in Berlin and then during the wait in Prussian captivity, and also on one occasion when he drove Triebenfeld far into South Prussia and back to the latter's estate at Kontopp.

[p. 123]

### 11.6 Friedrich Ludwig Again as Renewer of a Territory

We have additional knowledge of FL's activities through letters he wrote to his son and successor in Öhringen, August. These included, among other things, the difficulties arising from the restrictions imposed by the King of Württemberg upon the former rulers of the annexed territories.

One letter to August in particular seems well suited to illustrate his evidently continuing drive to create things around his new residence, the castle at Slaventzitz. It concerns agriculture and the exploitation of mineral resources (the further development of coal and ore mining). The letter bears the date 17 August — without a year. It dates from 1814, since his son Adolph is visiting FL that summer, being only 17 years old at the time. The letter, as stated, is addressed to August:[^95]

> *Dearest son! I dictate this letter from the bath, which suits me well; already half the cure is completed, and I [get] no other addition to the residues. [He evidently has health problems that are being treated with a bath cure at home]. Adolph goes today with the Forest Master by way of Lassowitz to Landsberg; from the former office J.B. Kunorsky and Court Councillor Hahn from Koschentin are coming today as well; they were there for 3 days, and Kunorsky made the journey there, accompanied by Baildon and Korb, by way of Bytkow and Tarnowitz; Bytkow is said to have made a great and lasting impression on him, as Korb told me. Everything now seems so well prepared for its acquisition that it is hoped the bidding date can be set for the 28th of this month. As the works overseer told me, Kunorsky intends to be present himself. I do not know whether he will find the time for it, since he still wanted to go on to Brieg. From my sister I receive good news from Carlsruhe, and all are well there; she will probably remain a few*

[p. 124]

> *weeks longer. Her [Ehrenpfort] she left behind here ill, but she is now better again; the local brewer's daughter accompanied her. I passed on to Sophie the good news you gave me of your state of health. From Auguste I had letters from Landshuth of the 4th of this month, and learned also that they had passed through to Schmiedeberg by evening. They must, if they still delay in order to look about the district of Warmbrunn, nevertheless have arrived in Herleshausen between the 10th and 12th. I hope, therefore, that they will have found her brother Wilhelm together with his wife, who traveled with him from Altona to [H.], he too having gone to Bückeburg at the time Prince Eugene was there; the birth of the Hereditary Prince will have caused great joy there, though the Elector of Hesse, who had long hoped for the reversion of the fief of the county of [S.], may not have wished for it quite so ardently. General von Welzien, formerly my adjutant and who recently paid me a visit, has been appointed the new Commandant at Cosel. His neighborhood is agreeable to me. His post as Inspector of the Landwehr is to go to Colonel Thiele, whom you know from Gera, where he served on my General Staff. The protocol of our last conference here, signed by you, I am filing away, and your will in this matter will be followed, as I have already directed. The harvest is still under way and is turning out excellently of every kind. These past days I inspected the buildings at Burawa and found the [tenancy] building, along with the other installations, nearly finished. The sawmill, which had to be rebuilt, was completed and is already cutting. The following day I was in Lechine, where I found the new granary floor finished, as well as the new sheepfold and shepherd's house, which are already occupied. The other, entirely masonry sheepfold has been very usefully converted into a stable for horses, oxen, and cows, and the old granary and the former cowshed, which threatened dangerous*

[p. 125]

> *collapse, are now to be torn down. There now remains for me only the new construction of a barn in Lechina, and then we would have the farm buildings in this department fairly well in order. In the matter of the works, this is already the case. For the new fitting-out of the Jakobswalde wire mill, the iron castings are now being formed in the local blast furnace. The whole is being carried out at little cost, just as you saw it done in model form at Korb's in Jakobswalde. The parsonage there will only be fully finished and occupied by winter. You will see from this, my dear son, that one has not been altogether idle. The clearing in the forests is proceeding well, and charcoal-burning of this cordwood will now begin. The damage from the last great storm, which felled over 500 cords in the forests, actually did no essential harm, since correspondingly less will now be cut in the ordinary felling. We are presently occupied here with, upon the reduction of the portion of field bordering the forest road that is to go to the peasants and is already partly measured out, establishing a regulation whereby the forest is given a natural boundary [...] and gains a useful increment. Mr. Sillg [...] is being accommodating in this matter, and since after the survey these peasants are given even more field than is their due, this good arrangement will probably begin without great difficulty. Korb let me have your letter [conveying] your opinion regarding the memoranda of J.K. Hofrichter. I see from it that I, and the matter, have been misunderstood on your side. Mr. Hofrichter is the commissioner for our Lassowitz estates as well as for Landsberg. He has, however, nothing whatever to do with the Slaventzitz estates. What he says regarding the gardens is his expert opinion. I am sending you the entire file of documents, as he expressly requested of me, but am having copies made of them and have communicated them to Estate Councillor König; he and his assistant, Mr. Councillor Bröcker, seem later*

[p. 126]

*with the F.P. Hofrichter, so that it now seems to me this affair is somewhat delayed. I hoped to see more clearly in the matter once Mr. Kunorsky and Forest Master [Mi] have returned, since Mr. Hofrichter will then have met with them and with Metallurgical Councillor König. This affair concerning Landsberg is to be finally settled on the spot, in the presence of your brother Adolph. Korb, who is acquainted with all of this, I advised to remove the misunderstanding with Metallurgical Councillor König and to accept his communication so modestly that no harmful jealousy [Eifersucht] should arise from it. I found it only necessary to note all this to you, as it serves your guidance [...] from your brother [...] the most earnest regards to you and your amiable consort, whose recovery gave us heartfelt joy. I hope these lines will find you both at Friedrichsruhe, where a stay at this time of year should still be quite pleasant. Farewell, dear son, continue to grant me your love and trust [...] in return for my affection and high regard.*  
*Your faithful father*

This letter—admittedly somewhat clumsy and perhaps too long for some readers (dictated in the bathtub!)—omits only a single subject: the existing heavy burden of debt, which will be addressed later in connection with Triebenfeld's duties. Everything else that comes up must be seen as a document of admirable activity. The 68-year-old was in poor health, but he concerned himself, comparably to Faust in *Faust II*, with the development, renovation, and expansion of the entire territory, with the forest and its use, with roads, with agricultural installations, with mills, and not least he took joyful part in the development of coal and ore mining (the aforementioned Korb was the manager there). In doing so he knew that ultimate authority lay with his now 30-year-old son August (his own father had, twenty years earlier,

[p. 127]

left responsibility to him, as his then Hereditary Prince, in Ingelfingen; now FL himself has already had to abdicate). Reports on former employees and family news are, as always, included in the letter.

FL, although the letter above conveys otherwise, was not free in his decisions. Sovereignty over finances and administration lay in the hands of his son and his officials in Öhringen.[^96]

Already years before, FL had attempted, in the vicinity of the estates there belonging to his long-divorced wife Countess Marianne, and of the manor of Bytkow which he himself had acquired in 1801 without the involvement of his mother-in-law, to gain a foothold in the long-established coal and ore mining industry and the emerging metal industry (zinc, brass). Bytkow and the neighboring places Josephsdorf, Jakobswalde, and Ignazdorf were later known collectively as "Hohenlohehütte." The Princess von Osten-Sacken accepted from him that he was doing this with a view to the future of his children, her grandchildren. He had concluded an advisory contract with an English mining expert named Baildon, who had already held corresponding Prussian government offices.

The felling of timber for the smelting of the ore proved at first to be a critical matter (as it also was in Hohenlohe). It was not to become a clear-cutting. Wood could also be used as building timber and thus brought quicker profit. Princess Sacken still had a supervisory role over the accounts at that time and had to establish that FL was aware of the immediate opportunity for profit and made use of it, for more timber was felled than was needed for smelting. This caused trouble; she tried to restrain him. Here too the Öhringen administration, at least after the death of Princess Sacken, had a strong hand in matters.[^97] The same, incidentally, applied to the smelting works as well.[^98]

It was only a descendant during the era of the German Empire who truly became successful and wealthy there in mining—naturally, a Hohenlohe. In the days of "Hohenlohehütte" he was considered the richest man in Prussia after the King! — Upper Silesia

[p. 128]

became part of Poland after the First World War. Almost nothing remains there of Slaventzitz Castle. The place is today called Welnowiec and is a district of Katowice.[^99]

*[illustration: reproduction of a share certificate (Aktie) of the Hohenlohe-Werke Aktiengesellschaft zu Hohenlohehütte in Oberschlesien, 1000 Mark, dated 20 May 1905, with various overprints and stamps]*

What became of it: share of the Hohenlohe-Werke (1918).

[p. 129]

## 12 Another Contemporary and Companion

### 12.1 Father and Son Glenck. The Salines and the Search for Coal[^100]

For this we must first return to Hohenlohe. In Weißbach in the Kocher valley, a few kilometers downstream from Niedernhall, there had been salines since the Middle Ages — that is, facilities for extracting salt as a preservative. The raw material is brine, that is, strongly saline groundwater, which is either pumped up or emerges as spring water at the surface. Salt was for centuries an important commodity, including as an article of trade. From the eighteenth century onward there was, in addition, the drinking of brine as a curative water and the taking of brine baths, particularly in Ingelfingen.

Brine springs suitable for salt extraction existed at Niedernhall. From there the brine was conveyed in wooden pipes to Weißbach, where two salt works were operated. The salt works functioned on the principle of evaporating the water content of the brine (boiling down salt). This required fuel — wood. In the middle of the eighteenth century wood became scarcer, the salt works had fallen into poor condition, and profitability was in question, particularly since pricing was subject to regulation. The town of Niedernhall was, in the period relevant here, two-thirds in the possession of the Archbishopric of Mainz and one-third Hohenlohe property.

Friedrich Ludwig, already as Hereditary Prince, recognized the necessity of intervention; his father, Prince Heinrich August, gave him a free hand, as presumably did his princely uncle in Öhringen, to whom the Hohenlohe third there belonged (FL would purchase the Mainz share in 1799, becoming thereby "majority owner" of Niedernhall).

[p. 130]

Father and son succeeded in recruiting Johann Georg Glenk of Schwäbisch Hall, who was employed there at the great saline ("Baugegenschreiber", a building and accounts clerk). Glenk's wish to be granted leave from Hall in order to take over the direction of the Weißbach saline was rejected by the councils of the Imperial City. He changed employment nonetheless and entered Ingelfingen service in early 1781 as Court Councillor (Hofrat). His annual salary of 600 florins was quite considerable, and he had, besides, a number of additional benefits (by comparison: the annual salary of the best-paid clergyman in Ingelfingen, the Court Preacher, amounted to 120 florins, to which were added a further 24 florins for wine as well as free provisions in kind). Supervision of the salt works lay with FL. — It was this same Glenck who gave concrete architectural form to the Hereditary Prince's plans for the settlement of craftsmen in Ingelfingen — the later Mariannenvorstadt — and who saw the project through until his death in 1801 (see p. 34f.).

Glenck worked on the rehabilitation of the salines. In 1787 he even began boring a further shaft at Niedernhall, which was given the name Friedrich Ludwig Brunnen. Truly profitable, however, they did not become in this period.

This deficiency was also rooted in the great quantity of wood required and its price. The surrounding forests — for instance up above at Hermersberg Castle — were already suffering. With coal as a heating material of higher calorific value, an improvement might perhaps have been achieved, and this was evident to FL as well. He himself had some acquaintance with the matter from his own possessions in Upper Silesia, where coal mining and ore mining were carried on. And Glenck had become familiar with coal deposits and mines there on study journeys.

Glenck had some understanding of geology and mineralogy. In test borings, it was believed that a coal seam had indeed been found. In order to be able to begin mining, capital was now also needed. FL called, in a printed promotional tract, for the formation of a company; it states there, among other things: "Friedrich Ludwig, Hereditary Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, grants to Court Councillor Glenck the establishment of coal mines in the lands of Hohenlohe." The idea

[p. 131]

*[illustration: oval engraved portrait, profile view, of a man in period dress]*

Director of Buildings and Salines J. G. Glenk (1751–1801).[^101]

*[illustration: photographic reproduction of a painted portrait of a seated man, with a facsimile signature "C. Glenck" beneath]*

Councillor of Salines and Privy Court Councillor Carl Christian Friedrich Glenck (1779–1845)

[p. 132]

was progressive: it was resolved to found a kind of joint-stock company! The coal was to be supplied to purchasers other than the salt works itself as well, for instance also to glassworks. FL's task was to convene the first assembly of the shareholders. Interest and confidence in such an undertaking were great; throughout the Empire shares ("Kuxe", these being securities certifying a share in a mine) were bought, and local lordships also took part.

After the death of Johann Georg Glenk, he was soon succeeded by his son Carl Christian Friedrich Glenck [sic]. He had received a good education for his father's profession: attendance at the Hohe Karlsschule in Stuttgart, study at the University of Erlangen (law, together with mineralogy and geology out of personal interest). He continued to pursue the natural sciences and, from 1799, studied at the Mining Academy at Freiberg in Saxony.

Glenck the younger then became first private secretary to Friedrich Ludwig, his father's employer, and from 1803 worked as Princely Hohenlohe Justice and Revenue Bailiff (Amtmann), presumably chiefly as Director of Salines, resident at Weißbach. He thus had charge of the salt works there — and, with the money from the share issue and the knowledge of mining he had acquired at Freiberg, he continued the search for coal, also committing his own money to the effort.

Coal pits were to be created, among other places at Hermersberg above Niedernhall and Weißbach. Six shafts were sunk there. What was extracted, however, was only inferior material, "which for the most part scarcely deserved the name of coal."[^102] Moreover, a great deal of water broke into the shafts. Glenck set about building a tunnel intended to run some 1.5 kilometers from the valley, meant to drain off the water — the "Ziegelei-Stollen" (Brickworks Adit). In a letter of 15 July 1804 he reports to FL on progress in this work, and he also mentions the name of the English mining expert Baildon. Unfortunately it is not clear whether the latter had by then also brought his knowledge to bear at Weißbach, or whether FL had merely mentioned him earlier on his own account. Baildon's collaboration FL had, as

[p. 133]

noted above, secured by contract for his projects in Upper Silesia.

We know today that nothing came of the entire enterprise. But it is a project from FL's box of ideas and belongs here. – The author Adolf Fischer did not distinguish, in his 1871 text, between Glenk sen. and Glenck jun., as indeed other authors also failed to do. The search for hard coal plays no role in Fischer's account.

There is, however, a substantial number of letters from Glenck to FL extant, as well as a few of the addressee's replies.[^103] The two remained in contact from 1802 to at least 1815, and the correspondence concerned almost exclusively the saltworks and the hard-coal project. In 1802 there are six letters, in 1804 seven. The content at first consisted of often very technically emphasized progress reports, figures on wood consumption together with its costs, and information about an accident. In 1804 complaints were reported from financial backers (shareholders from Schwäbisch Hall) who had committed their entire fortunes, could provide no new capital, and now had no further hope. On 7 August 1804, 3,000 florins arrived for Glenck from a Braunsbach address (that is, quite nearby), and he mentioned the purchase of estates (probably meadow lands) in order to improve the roads. He wrote further that part of the saltworks' income (the salt fund) had been put by him into the mining project. This could become dangerous, since he had to reckon with soon having to present the account books!

The Prince of (Hohenlohe-)Schillingsfürst (who is certainly also a shareholder) seemed to have grown especially uneasy and would, according to Glenck writing to FL, presumably visit him in Breslau "in the course of the month" (August 1804). He added a wish: "May God and princely wisdom avert all misfortunes to be feared." So things once again looked bad, but he hoped "to have to write no more laments." Glenck later mentioned the South Prussian estates (see p. 140f.), so he

[p. 134]

knew of this commitment of FL's — had Glenck also invested money there?

For the following years, 1805 and 1806, no letters to or from Glenck are extant, or none existed; the Prince was, precisely in these two years, very much occupied with Prussian deliberations and preparations for the confrontation with France. At the end of 1806 the Battle of Jena and Auerstedt was lost, followed by the capitulation of Prenzlau and his capture. For two years, as has been described, he had to endure as a French prisoner in the castle at Öhringen. Nevertheless, despite guards, he remained reachable via "disguised messengers" and wrote and received letters, among them also to and from Glenck. Such is this letter from Öhringen dated 27 July 1807 (Öhringen is not stated, but the date indicates it — he was still a prisoner there at this time):[^104]

> *I very much regret, Your Well-Born [he always writes "Wohlgeboren" with the additional "h", and it is moreover the form of address for persons of lower rank of bourgeois status, as Glenck indeed is], having become burdensome to you with my request. You ! [the "!" always follows this additional form of address] would oblige me if you would come here on Thursday and could stay this day and the following one, since I have pressing correspondence to attend to for Berlin and Breslau, or perhaps for Silesia; if possible, the needs of Weißbach should also be seen to. It cannot, however, be arranged as quickly as you would like. I am glad to have found a means whereby the Hofräthin Hahn shall at once receive at least 1,500 thalers. This you may tell her from me. My women's [intercession] contributed nothing to it; it was my own eager good will alone that made it possible. From Hahn I have a report. It will also work out concerning Bitbus, and for that too I need your pen. My stay here will likely not be much longer

[p. 135]

> *though I have no news of this as yet. Then you will no longer be plagued by me. This as a consolation. [signature of Friedrich Ludwig]*

This letter shows, for one thing, some linguistic peculiarities that are also found elsewhere. Unfortunately not everything can be clarified either: for example, what is meant by Bitbus (perhaps he means Bytkow)? For what purpose does FL need "your pen"? If the addressee is indeed Glenck, it must be a request for writing assistance from his former private secretary. For his own handwriting was and indeed still is not very easy to read, and he probably knew it. For which addressees did he — or rather the writer, who was apparently to place a whole day at his (FL's) disposal — take such trouble? – That he was glad actually to have raised money that he wished to pay out, or from which he wished to pay something, certainly pleased the Hofräthin Hahn — though whether it was all that he owed her is another matter. A (partial) resolution of what was at first puzzling suggests itself: Glenck is asked by FL to come to him because he, the head of the saltworks and former secretary, will shortly be traveling to Silesia, since there he can consult with the mining expert Baildon. In Silesia, after all, the coal-mining venture continued, which FL as a prisoner in Öhringen could not himself direct. He remained, as before, the owner in Silesia and had people on the spot, for example the Hofrath Hahn ("From Hahn I have a report"). His wife, with or without this title, was the Hofräthin Hahn. Hahn's activities have already been mentioned (see p. 122). Which women ("Weiber") surrounded FL in Öhringen — his daughters? He was mistaken in assuming he would soon get away from Öhringen; he was not to receive the corresponding news until 1808.

In 1810 there was particular "urgency" regarding decisions on continuing the Weißbach undertaking. To a letter in May Glenck enclosed an accounting, and this time sealed the whole thing. He reported the arrival of threatening letters from the Princes of Langenburg and of Kirchberg. The letter was a cry for help; if

[p. 136]

free timber were not delivered soon, the saltworks would be lost. He received no answer; no reaction from FL can be inferred from the following letters that year. FL received in all twelve letters from Glenck that year, and the correspondence continued at a somewhat lower frequency until 1817, first from the address Breslau, thereafter Slaventzitz.

In 1810 there was finally some good news for once: in the Friedrich-Ludwig shaft a new salt spring was tapped. Glenck reported this to the Privy Councillor von Braun at Öhringen. He replied:

> *However pleasing this event was to me, Your Right Honourable Excellency will readily believe that, as a grateful and loving son of my late father, I hoped with such longing that I might yet be able to save his honour after all. This has now, thank God, come to pass, and the joy of my heart is especially great on this account. But also in the main matter, with respect to the gracious Lordship, this event is most gratifying, particularly at the present moment. For the saline works will now certainly become, within a short time, a profitable enterprise, and will also be able to contribute its share toward the discharge of the general burden.*

Father and son Braun had already been councillors in the princely administration at Ingelfingen. The positive assessments of the news itself unfortunately had no consequences: von Braun found no support in the Öhringen administration for his enthusiastic estimate. There was no wish to invest any further.

Glenck's letters to FL, always in the same devoted yet trusting tone, were addressed to the wrong man, since FL had long since abdicated and his son August was now the ruling lord of Hohenlohe-Öhringen. Glenck also informed FL that he had turned to him but had received no answer:

[p. 137]

> *the Prince, to whom I have in the meantime written twice, did not answer me with so much as a syllable and abandons me entirely, as he does his saline works [...] It is now exactly 30 years since the first work [by the Glenck family] was done on the works here. All exertions were in vain, all costs appear to have been fruitlessly expended.*

The present-day reader of these letters may gain the impression that Glenck needed someone to whom he could communicate his worries. August placed little trust in the whole undertaking and was "unwilling to contribute even 1 florin."

Glenck had discovered a gypsum deposit near Marbach from which he could draw profit. He continued the operation at Weißbach only briefly, for private investors. In Wimpfen he was then more successful. For the technique of boring, he had invented what was later named after him, the Glenck grab-bucket (a pear-shaped gripping device that was lowered into the shaft open and then closed, in order to bring up the material at the bottom — stones and the like). Glenck eventually became a wealthy man.

### 12.2 Excursus III: Glenck and Goethe

In Thuringia, where Glenck had lived since 1823, he also searched for salt deposits and acquired a prospecting concession from Goethe, who was responsible for such matters. He struck it lucky near Stotternheim, north of Erfurt. Goethe himself was interested in minerals. Systematic extraction began in 1828. Glenck informed Goethe that he had "at a depth of 1170 feet in a borehole [...] encountered, in wholly pure form, according to the fragment, partly granular and partly foliated crystalline salt."[^105] Goethe wrote about this in June 1828 to a certain Kaspar von Sternberg, saying that Glenck was "just now about to erect the necessary buildings." Goethe and Glenck met at the saline "Louisenhall" – as the Stotternheim works were called – and presumably also nearby in Buffleben.

[p. 138]

For 30 January 1828, the birthday celebration of Grand Duchess Louise Auguste, to which he was invited, Glenck asked Goethe for an occasional poem, which he also received (it was not, however, to be given out of hand, as Goethe told a publicist – yet it did appear in 1830 in a Musen-Almanach). The birthday greetings, with a dedication to the Duchess, to which Glenck presented, among other things, freshly mined salt from Stotternheim in a precious glass vessel, have been preserved:

> *The first products of the Stotternheim saline, accompanied by a poetic dialogue between gnomes, Geognosy, and Technology, presented on 30 January 1828 with a comforting Glückauf! C. Glenck, Director of Salines, most humbly.*

In Goethe's little work, a gnome speaks out against the exploitation of treasures hidden in the earth, but can do nothing to prevent it and joins the festive company. "Geognosy" (i.e., the science of the earth's structure, properly the doctrine of the structure and composition of the solid crust of the earth; here personified) regards the successful borehole as the outcome of a firm belief in the universally identical laws of seam formation. "Technology" boasts of having, with the aid of physics and geometry, first developed the means for exploiting the salt deposit.

The supposition has been advanced that Goethe, impressed by Glenck's work, "made use of" these conversations in *Faust II*.[^106] There, in the first act, "Imperial Palace," the subject is economy and mineral wealth, mostly however the precious metals found there, though brine as the raw material for salt is also mentioned. Glenck, for his part, also understood a great deal about mining. And gnomes likewise appear in *Faust II*.

The reader may well ask what all this has to do with FL. It is speculation that Goethe and Glenck also spoke with one another about the past. After all, Goethe had known FL from the conferences at Jena in 1806, and Glenck had, in his first

[p. 139]

professional post, been FL's private secretary and then for many years his Court Councillor (Hofrat) and Director of Salines at Weißbach. Glenck had once written to FL that he had also put private money into the Weißbach saline. That was not all: in the last schedules of debts that Triebenfeld had drawn up for FL (see appendix, p. 239), a loan from Glenck to FL also appears! And, one can scarcely believe it, between 1823 and 1828 Glenck himself once fell into a financial bind, so that he had to ask Goethe for 2000 thalers in order to be able to continue the boreholes! – Who, faced with this, does not think – as with FL – of entrepreneurial spirit and the making of debts!

A great breakthrough was represented by Glenck's discoveries of rock salt in Basel-Land. They became a foundation of the chemical industry now established there – and also of the wealth he ultimately achieved. Such wealth was attained only by FL's grandson, in Upper Silesia.

[p. 140]

## 13 The "Squandering of Estates" after the Third Partition of Poland

### 13.1 Excursus IV: "South Prussia". The "Founders' Consortium" and its Allocation Practices

The second (1793) and third Polish partition (1795, among Russia, the Danubian Monarchy, and Prussia) led to a considerable expansion of the territory under Prussian rule (with an almost exclusively Polish population). The designation for the relevant part of Poland here was "South Prussia". Central places were Posen, Kalisch, and Warsaw; the "Chamber Departments" were also named after these towns, and these in turn were divided into roughly ten districts each. Politically, South Prussia was subordinate to a General Directorate in Berlin.

After 1806 (the Peace of Posen) and 1807 (the Peace of Tilsit), and then again in 1815 (the Congress of Vienna), substantial changes occurred. Thus, for example, in 1807 large parts of South Prussia became the Duchy of Warsaw, in union with Saxony. In 1815 a large part of it became Prussian once again: the Province of the Grand Duchy of Posen. This was of considerable relevance for the owners of the estates granted by the Prussian King in South Prussia. – The Elector of Saxony had become King in 1806 and, from 1815, ruled in personal union over the remaining parts of West Poland, where his ancestors had themselves once been kings.

Prussia's King Frederick William II had already, before 1795, promoted immigration from many German lands into South Prussia. From September 1798 dates an "Energetic Order for the Continuation of Colonization in the Newly Acquired Provinces of South Prussia and New East Prussia" issued by his successor Frederick William III. Newly offered were ecclesiastical estates—monasteries and the like—that had come into state possession through royal seizure, as well as other

[p. 141]

Polish institutions previously in public ownership ("Starost" holdings). One example is the locality of Betsche (where FL, too, later held property):[^107] Betsche was, until 1793, the property of the Bishopric of Posen, which forbade any settlement of non-Catholics there (there were 522 inhabitants). Then a rapid development set in: by 1843 the population numbered 1,329, and alongside the Catholics there were now also 179 Protestants and 173 Jews. With Prussian thoroughness, documentation on the new state territories was available as early as 1798: K.J. Heubner's "Historical-Statistical-Geographical Description of South Prussia and New East Prussia, or of the Royal Prussian Seizures from Poland in the Years 1798 and 1795".[^108] In this work all localities are recorded, and one finds the locations of the estates referred to when Friedrich von Cölln wrote of the squandering of estates.

There were regulations for the colonists: no service duties at court, only monetary interest payments. The purchase contract stipulated: "Hereditary leaseholders and their descendants shall forever remain wholly free of all hereditary bondage, and shall likewise render the manorial lordship not the least court service nor deliver any produce in kind." Interest was to be paid after the expiry of four rent-free years. Some of these arrangements are strikingly reminiscent of the provisions made for the settlement of craftsmen in Ingelfingen!

In Berlin, the War and Forestry Councillor Triebenfeld was to direct and oversee allocation practice through a General Directorate established by the King. Administration of the new territorial parts was subordinate to Minister Georg von Hoym. A "Founders' Consistory" consisted of him, General Bischofswerder, Triebenfeld, and the "Royal Chamber Servant" Ritz.

Mainly noble military men were interested in acquiring estates. Many of them, as younger siblings of the firstborn heirs, will have had no career prospects other than military service (and administration); owning an estate of one's own would secure a new livelihood, and speculative profits (and tax evasion) might also be possible. This is no mere insinuation:

[p. 142]

A "financial official" named Hans von Held uncovered abuses very early on, first in a poem:

> *To all the knaves their just reward*  
> *who cheat the state,*  
> *and out of rapacious lust around the throne*  
> *coil themselves like serpents.*

His book, bearing the cumbersome title "The True Jacobins in the Prussian State, or a Documentary Account of the Wicked Intrigues and Fraudulent Conduct in Office of Two Prussian State Ministers"[^109] (1800), was first largely redacted and then withdrawn from circulation by the authorities (owing to the extensive redactions, it is considered the first "black book"!). The author, who sought through this publication to force an investigation and who denounced above all the practices of Count Hoym, was imprisoned and convicted! Held had, however, also hit the mark: Count Hoym had been removed from office on 26 April 1798. As was usual at the time, rumors preceded such events, and, as if in proof of his own vigilance, Triebenfeld reported this to FL as well.[^110]

Distinctly critical voices concerning the events in South Prussia could already be read shortly after the reign of Frederick William III (Klöden, 1840)—though coupled with complaints about the ingratitude of the Poles toward the Prussian state. After 150 years it was then also established that Prussia had performed good work of development and administration in the thinly settled region, especially in the field of schooling.[^111] One should not simply assume that the new owners of the estates had thoroughly exploited the Poles who worked there (for the most part) for their livelihood. They, too, had been obliged to render dues to the previous owners, the monasteries and other public institutions. After 1807, following the peace settlements resulting from Napoleon's victories, the French authorities informed the inhabitants that

[p. 143]

they now no longer needed to render any dues at all! This had, among other consequences, a great impact on the Prussian purchasers, who generally had an interest burden to pay. FL, whose financial difficulties will be discussed at length later, lamented bitterly this temporary loss of his income.

### 13.2 Triebenfeld, FL, and Massenbach as His Beneficiaries

First a quotation from Friedrich Buchholz's article on FL. The author is not only emotional here, indeed polemical, but nonetheless:

> *His need for money [that of FL is meant] rose, namely, from year to year, and a certain Herr von Triebenfeld, in whose hands his affairs lay, was by far better suited to exploit the Prince's embarrassments to his own advantage than to bring him relief. This Triebenfeld, who through his intrigues worked his way up from a servant to a nobleman, plays so important a role in the Prince's history that the future biographer of the latter (should such a one be found) can scarcely give him too much attention.[^112]*

Triebenfeld had also seen to himself in the founding consistory mentioned above: he had, as already noted, received a donation of eight estates at an appraised value of 51,000 thalers and, some time afterward, sold them for a total of 750,000.

It is he — whom the author Friedrich von Cölln has named — on whom the discussion here must turn, for he became an important helper in FL's acquisition of estates in South Prussia and later, as his General Plenipotentiary, oversaw his disastrous finances right up to the Congress of Vienna. FL had knowledge of the opportunities on offer in Berlin.

[p. 144]

Worthy of particular mention are his acquisitions in the Warthe valley. He went so far as to introduce German place names there as well! Some can be made out on the map (Emilienheim, Friedrichsfeld, Adelshof, Sophiental, Ludwigslust), others exist only under their Polish place names.

He corresponded successfully with Triebenfeld from 1796 onward. In that year he received

> *the ecclesiastical estates of the lordship of Zagorow and, from the crown domain [Starost], the lordship of Trombczyn, which consisted of nine granges and two villages, extensive forest and meadow lands in the Warthe valley. The management of these may have yielded little profit, since as early as 18 May 1805 Triebenfeld concluded a hereditary lease contract for their dismemberment [distribution] with eighteen German colonists — with subsequent authorization from the Royal South Prussian War and Domains Chamber at Kalisch dated 28 January 1806. Thus arose in the Warthebruch […] the following German villages: Sophiental [Olchowo], Ludwigslust [Maly Las], Friedrichsfelde [Tarzewo], Adelshof [Chrosniki], the grange of Adolfsberg, Ingelfingen [Anielewo], Emilienheim [Michalinow], Augustinhof [Augustinow], Schwentz [Swiecie], Kolonweiler [Wrombczyn], Klein-Öring [Grozdzikowo].[^113]*

Anyone to whom the German names sound familiar is right. They are first names of FL's children and Hohenlohe place names! A contemporary and somewhat expanded list by Triebenfeld is to be found in the appendix on p. 238.

FL, then, if the date 1796 is correct, profited very early from this grant of estates. He remained engaged in the matter, and in early 1798 even conducted correspondence on it with the King. Whether he was pursuing further applications for himself, or was interceding for a

[p. 145]

*[illustration: historical map of the Warthetal east of Posen, showing the Polish villages of the mixed-Polish Warthebruch settlements (Handzeichnung von A. Breyer, 1938)]*

The Warthe valley east of Posen. The place name Zagorow can be made out on the map at upper left. (Hand-drawn by A. Breyer, 1938)

[p. 146]

particular application (conceivably on Massenbach's behalf), does not emerge from the King's surviving reply letter of 18 January 1798. The matter concerned a lordship of Krotoszyn, of which the King wrote that it might yield an annual return of 60,000 reichsthalers. In any case he informed him that the matter lay with his Minister of State, von Struensee, and had not yet been decided.[^114] Evidently the King, in his reply, had in mind a proper procedure into which he did not wish to intervene.

It could indeed have been an application by Massenbach! He was interested in the estates. He stood close to the court, having given the King's son instruction in mathematics. In March 1798 he reported in a letter to FL that "His Royal Majesty has most graciously deigned to enfeoff me with South Prussian estates." He enclosed a list of these with the letter and thanked FL "for your gracious efforts." FL, then, must have interceded on his behalf. Massenbach even asked for advice

> *whether I should keep all these estates and lease them out […] or bring them into better condition […] so that they would represent double the value […] this would at a stroke make me a rich man, and my wish would not merely be fulfilled but exceeded.[^115]*

The appended list names the prices and former owners of six estates, among them two convents in Posen and the Archbishop of Gniezno. The estate at Bialokosch appears as No. 2 on the reproduced list, at a price of 2,580 Rth. Its former owner was the convent of nuns in Posen. Massenbach would keep it until his death and bequeath it to his children and grandchildren. But he too incurred debts. — Bialokosch still bears that name today. It appears on maps, not very far northwest of Posen.

[p. 147]

FL's contact with the King on the matter of "estates in South Prussia" continued, for in a royal letter of 4 December 1798 these are again the subject. It concerned price negotiations, not donations, and the name Triebenfeld came up as well. This fits with a remark by FL in a letter of 26 March 1799 to his fifteen-year-old son August in Berlin.[^116] He added in his own hand that the son should send a "deposit" straightaway to War Councillor Triebenfeld. Whether the "deposit" is an indication of a payment? At the end of 1799, in the written agreement ("Transact," see chap. 7.1) made necessary by Marianne's separation from FL, quite considerable possessions in South Prussia are listed.

*[illustration: reproduction of a handwritten list ("Angebotsliste") of estates offered to Massenbach, sent by him to FL]*

The "list of offers" for Massenbach, which he sent to FL.

[p. 148]

## 14 Friedrich Ludwig, the Debtor

### 14.1 How did Friedrich Ludwig manage all this financially?

The question has long since been answered, though not correctly. For the fact is: he did not manage it! He passed on to his succeeding son a mountain of debt, which, however, was probably not attributable to the Ingelfingen activities. In 1795/96 he had still stated that "his edifice" (meaning his life's plan) was now firmly founded and essentially complete, needing only to be finished off in order to pay back the borrowed money, which, as he hoped, would in future be easily possible for his son out of surpluses.

When he then "resigned" in 1806 (stepped down as reigning Prince of Hohenlohe-Öhringen), the calculated sum of total debts (both personal and those of "the House") amounted to 280,000 florins — an enormous sum even by the standards of the time, one that had presumably still been underestimated. And matters continued to worsen even after his mother-in-law had fully recognized the disaster.[^117] She pressed August in Öhringen not to assume any further guarantees for debts. She herself came to an agreement with FL over 20,000 florins; but this became known, the creditors were standing by and would have seized it at once. The transfer was called off. It is thus not surprising that in 1815 the rumor arose that the King of Prussia had set aside 800,000 thalers in order finally to free FL from his debt crisis. The rumor was false. Insight into this misery is provided, among other things, by Triebenfeld's schedules of debts, he having served for over twenty years as FL's plenipotentiary in this domain.

Adolf Fischer judges FL as follows (and in agreement with Friedrich von Cölln):

[p. 149]

> *In money matters he was like a child who does not know the value of money. As often as he received any, existing debts were always paid off, which is why he did not lack credit for a long time. Even after the catastrophe of 1806 he was for a considerable time unable to accustom himself to the necessary restraint.[^118]*

And yet he knew and made use of methods of financial management still common today: savings-and-credit models like those of building societies, the issuing of shares to raise additional capital, the taking out of mortgages on real estate. Looking back, it is astonishing that he had declined the offer of the certainly not inconsiderable sum of money from Frankfurt (see p. 51). Might this have gone against his professional self-conception as a military commander?

### 14.2 Debts, Credits, Mortgages ...

It is, alas, so: Friedrich von Cölln was right. Finance was not FL's great strength. He (or rather his General Plenipotentiary Triebenfeld) left behind an extensive correspondence with his creditors, from 1784 until FL's death.[^119] Early on, FL already had to ask for help or borrow money. In 1790 the Duke of Courland lent him 300,000 thalers; in 1798 the Schlabrendorf couple lent 169,000.[^120] He then asked the Duke of Courland to become godfather to Alexander (see p. 72).

In addition to Bytków (in the year 1801), FL acquired in 1804 the great domain of Koschentin (with Boronow, a neighboring locality) in Upper Silesia, which also remained the property of his descendants. But it had been expensive — 925,000 thalers! He first had to raise this money — and it is very likely that this extreme sum was a quite essential reason for his continuing financial misery. The Princess Sacken had acquired Landsberg, neighboring Koschentin, shortly before drawing up her will. It went, like Koschentin, later to her grandson Adolph. —

[p. 150]

The princely administration in Öhringen also felt itself responsible and at least kept books on the matter.[^121] The bundle of files for the period from 1804 to 1812 contains the relevant correspondence, with reports from the court, chamber, and domain administrations in Ingelfingen, Öhringen, and Silesia, rulings from government authorities, payment demands from creditors, other letters from private persons, promissory notes, cost statements, receipts, and more.

Almost everyone had debts, to banks and to sellers of estates; mortgage interest could not be paid, so the Berlin General Invalids' Fund would take the matter to court (the document that concludes this case is found in the appendix on p. 243f.). On 25 September 1806, that is, in the tense period before Jena and Auerstädt, FL evidently had occasion to set out his financial situation.

The following letter may have been addressed to the helper Zastrow; this is suggested by the salutation "Your Well-born" in the running text, as well as by the signature, though an original signature is missing:

> *Through the colonization, carried out with royal approval, of my South Prussian domains of Zagorow and Trombiczyn, a net yield from them of 40,247 reichsthalers has been ascertained and, after careful examination, confirmed by the Kalisz War and Domains Chamber. This should put me in a position to fulfill a wish long cherished by me and to attempt to settle my debts entirely. It is not a question of whether it is possible to obtain from my land the sum of 480,000 reichsthalers, that is, as much as the whole of my debts, without exception, amount to, against which I would then mortgage the two domains provided, establishing thereby complete security with first and sole mortgage, and would also determine annually a sum for the payment of the interest and amortization of the capital, whereby this debt could*

[p. 151]

> *be discharged within a few years. I most respectfully request that Your Well-born undertake this business and, given your extensive acquaintance, negotiate a loan of this kind. The power of attorney necessary for this I shall send by the first post, in whatever form you please. I have likewise instructed my General Plenipotentiary to hand over to the same immediately all parameters necessary for conducting this business, and to pay out, without delay, a [gratuity] of 5 % to the men of business assisting in this matter, as soon as the affair is concluded.*  
> *Freiberg, 25 September 1806[^122]*

According to present-day accounts from the locality of Zagorów,[^123] FL received the estates as a gift from the King. Unfortunately there is no record of this in the archive at Neuenstein. The five estates — Trombczyn, Nowa-Wieś, Oświery, Łazy, and Szlewek — are said to have had a value of about 80,000 thalers. After a few years he is said to have sold half to another German "investor," the large landowner Karl Gottlieb Weigel of Hennersdorf in Silesia and lord of the manor of Kopoyno (in South Prussia, hence a neighbor).
