The remainder of the property was intended for settlement purposes. The name Weigel reappears in the financial and simultaneously debt statement drawn up at the end of FL's life.

It is furthermore of importance that, after the military defeat, the whole of Prussia was in great distress. The near-contemporary author Klöden (1840) wrote the following on this subject:

> *The provinces [still] occupied by the French up to the Vistula were bled most dreadfully by Napoleon's accomplices [...]. Billetings, direct taxes, deliveries, war contributions, etc., knew no end, and were extracted with merciless severity. Silesia had to pay 288,000 thalers monthly, East Prussia and Lithuania [...] 8 million in cash and 4 million in goods, the principal trading houses in Berlin alone 620,000 thalers,*

[p. 152]

> *Pomerania was treated no less harshly. [...] No fewer than 7,139 officials, thrown out of bread from the South Prussian provinces, begged for the scantiest daily bread, and nothing remained, in order to protect them from despair, but to make deductions from the pay of those serving officials still in office. [...] The cruel dismissal of all officials [in South Prussia] aroused throughout the whole of Prussia great bitterness against the Poles, which the crafty French policy gladly saw, because the Duchy of Warsaw was intended to watch over the revival of the Prussian monarchy, and, when the time came, to check it. [...] However one may think of the earlier partitions of Poland, it is nevertheless certain that much had been done for the Poles living under Prussia's sceptre, indeed far more in a few years than previously under the long Polish government. Efforts had been made to turn slaves into human beings and to secure for them a human existence; great sums and noble energies had been devoted to this, and they had been showered with benevolence, which was now repaid in the manner indicated. Everyone will respect the patriotic feeling with which a people fights for its independence; but it must not lead to ingratitude for benefits received, or indeed tempt one into cruelty.[^124]*

So much for some facts and an impression of the mood of the time.

It is not necessary here to present FL's financial situation in all its aspects. Gerlinde Krauss has done this in detail, focusing primarily on FL's mother-in-law, Princess Sacken.[^125] This Princess controlled an inconceivable extent of estates and other property and evidently knew exactly what needed to be done in each case (today one might say she also knew the rules of finance and real estate management). But cash was generally scarce. FL, according to Krauss, found himself in this "post-war period"

[p. 153]

compelled to sell his South Prussian acquisitions Betsche, Zagorow, Trombezyn, Botkowo, Winiec — or at least part of them (see p. 140f.) — far below their value in order to obtain cash. Even the Princess complained about the billeting of French troops with her at the town palace and their costs. But independent of this, her own new acquisitions, such as Bellin Castle, where Marianne lives with her new family, and further estates in Mecklenburg, are also considerably encumbered. In 1809 this amounted to around 90,000 thalers, so that Marianne considered using the 4,000 thalers due to her annually from her inheritance for debt repayment.

With his creditors, FL still had one more chance, in the view of the author Fischer,[^126] because with new loans he first paid off the old debts. But things grew ever tighter, and the Princess became aware of this too. She wrote him at least one, already quoted here, very plainly worded letter — that he ought to have turned to her much earlier with his transactions — and asked her grandson August not to assume any further guarantees for his father. She was a knowledgeable and shrewd woman. She did not live to see her son-in-law's further financial decline; she died in 1811. Shortly before that, she had apparently still been willing to help him with 20,000 thalers.

Triebenfeld remained in constant contact with FL. The correspondence is extremely extensive and proves that he personally attended to FL's possessions in South Prussia as well.[^127] Thus he reported that it was necessary to hire a chief forester in Zagorow ("he must be content with 300 florins annually"). In this unfortunately undated letter, he also asks FL for his opinion regarding an exchange of estates (which never took place).

The financial misery continued to worsen, and Triebenfeld too had a good deal to endure. He wrote, for example, on 14 March 1808 to FL:

> *I received Your Most Serene Highness's most gracious letter here in Berlin; I would long since have answered it, had I not been*

[p. 154]

> *placed under strict French guard and arrested. A fate I never deserved and which has given my civic existence a very bad turn. I was sent here from Memel at the end of December with important commissions, and I was already close to the goal of my wishes — soon to be able to depart again — when, without my suspecting it, two French gendarmes were sent to my lodgings with the order that they should keep me under strict watch and arrest until I, as General Plenipotentiary of the Prince of Hohenlohe, should have paid all the outstanding interest owed to the Invalid Fund alone, 5,000 reichsthalers. Shock and shame at this incident nearly drove me to the desperate resolve — the outstanding interest for 2 years amounts to 5,000 reichsthalers, where was I to get this amid the terrible want of money, all the more since there is no royal treasury here, and all revenues are in the hands of the French authorities. I proved with all the [jurists] that I was not the debtor. I showed the legal instrument by which the banker Oppenheimer had already, three years ago, undertaken to pay this remainder to the Invalid Fund at a discount of 7%, that he had had it entered under his own name, and that he was the one who must pay. I further proved that Your Serene Highness had not collected a single [penny] from the estates for two years, and that the Poles were hiding behind the order given by Napoleon not to pay here. But none of this counted for anything [...] and I had to sit until a remedy was found. I had 2,000 net on me; the upright Lieutenant-General and Minister von Zastrow stood surety for 5,000 net in paper money, which we then converted into specie and received 2,875 reichsthalers for, though we must repay 5,000 reichsthalers in specie for it by 1 June — before the last farthing is paid, I shall not be free of the execution nor released from my post. Meanwhile, through this affair, not only my honor, standing, and credit have been diminished, but the highest interests have been so*

[p. 155]

> *neglected that it has become [...] to me. I cannot now, for honor's sake, leave here until I have found a remedy regarding the 5,000 thalers [...], for I cannot allow the honest Minister v. Zastrow to incur any unpleasantness. Meanwhile I know of no other remedy than that Your Serene Highness should as soon as possible send back to me the blank draft for 6,000 net which you graciously credited to Eisenhardt, so that I may take back the Con[...] mortgage of 10,000 presented for it from the bank, and thereby be able to help. But may Your Serene Highness be pleased to endorse this draft to me, or note upon it yourself, that you have [received] no money against it, and return it accordingly. I shall then immediately press for the return of my mortgage, and [...] I shall instead receive the money here from a certain Generalin von Bülow, in order to satisfy the banker [...]. The noble Minister v. Zastrow petitions with me before the French authorities that Your Serene Highness's Trombezyn estates be restored, and had this disgraceful incident not befallen me, I should already have been in St. Petersburg since that time, and could there have accomplished much through the Grand Duke. In the confident hope of a very speedy grant of my most urgent request, I remain, Your Serene Highness's*  
> *most humble servant*  
> *v. Triebenfeld*

One cannot fully understand (or decipher) all of this at first glance (for instance: were the French authorities to decide that the Trabezyn estates be returned to FL, had they been taken from him — or had he sold all or half of them? What role would the Grand Duke in St. Petersburg have played?). But it is clear that Triebenfeld, too, despite his detailed knowledge of the "real estate business" and of financing possibilities, and despite his resourcefulness, is running into difficulties here. "Arrest" also means seizure by legal execution, and it will play a major role once again at

[p. 156]

a court hearing. One hope remains: von Zastrow, presumably owing to his old bond with FL.

The letter later became, for FL, the starting point for a letter to His Royal Majesty of 5 April 1809, which survives in the archive as a draft. In it FL once again set out the circumstances that had given rise to Triebenfeld's arrest, and assured him that he had found a way out for a small remaining debt. This, however, relates only to the matter referred to above.

As General Plenipotentiary, Triebenfeld truly had a great deal to do. An activity report with a list of 13 points, which he sent to FL on 7 March 1809 from his country estate at Kontopp, is reproduced in the appendix in transcribed form (as far as possible) (p. 241). What strikes me as interesting, among other things, is that administrative officials from South Prussia (Zagorow) sought him out at his residence in these matters. In a postscript he wrote that he had had all of this recorded by a court, "in order to begin this my career in truly brilliant fashion." From this it might be concluded that Triebenfeld had left state service and was now devoting himself entirely to the business of a financial and real-estate adviser — presumably then with further clients as well.

### 14.3 The Proceedings before the Royal Prussian Kammergericht, 1811

An extensive case file, a suit brought by the Royal General Invalids' Fund, has survived as a record of the Berlin Kammergericht and is presented here, despite all difficulties of comprehension, as an example and in unabridged form — as testimony to the Prussian penchant for meticulousness — in the appendix on pp. 243f.

It stands to reason that the King had knowledge of FL's financial circumstances, and Triebenfeld was presumably obliged to carry out a precise survey of the debts. The corresponding schedules have survived. The legacy (bequest) of 80,000 florins was merely a rumor!

[p. 157]

FL himself also attempted to find a remedy. He wrote a begging letter[^128] to His Majesty the King, in which he set out an optimistic plan for how he intended to bring about an improvement through pledges of his property. In addition, in 1813 he turned to Massenbach,[^129] who was living in Bialokosch, somewhat west of Posen, and asked him to assist in collecting 5,000 reichsthalers. He names a Count Tercinoky as the owner of the estate of Zagorow, who he says had owed him such a payment since Christmas 1812. He enclosed with the letter a certificate stating that this sum was to be paid out to the "bearer" and "holder" of the document. Massenbach replied that the Count did not respond to any of his letters and "appears not to exist" as far as he is concerned. — There is a further uncertainty on this point, since the town of Zagorow does in fact record a partial sale of FL's property to the landowner Weigel.

### 14.4 Interim Summary II

"Having debts" and "making debts," at least bank debts, was something quite ordinary at the time. FL was certainly a front-runner in this respect, and it is solely to demonstrate his situation that many documents are reproduced in the appendix. But, as cited above, Triebenfeld too, who "owned" an estate at Kontopp near Grünberg in Upper Silesia, and Massenbach as well, had debts. It was already noted earlier that even at Bellin Castle, the residence of FL's ex-wife Marianne, a mortgage of 43,000 thalers was registered, for which Count Hardenberg had stood surety. When he wanted to end this arrangement, Marianne temporarily found herself in difficulties. Her son Adolph, otherwise quite rarely mentioned, had a rescuing idea on this occasion and offered relief from his inheritance from his grandmother. — The peculiar feature in FL's case is the scale of the debts and the hopelessness of ever emerging from this misery.

[p. 158]

## 15 Excursus V: The New Kingdom of Württemberg

In 1806 the Duke of (Old) Württemberg had become King, as Frederick I, of the greatly expanding land. The area and population doubled through the mediatization of the principalities and the end of the independence of the Imperial cities. Among the principalities may be named Hohenlohe, Waldburg-Zeil, and Thurn und Taxis; among the Imperial cities, above all Ulm and Esslingen, but also many smaller ones such as Schwäbisch Hall. Frederick, the former Duke,

> *could begin to order and rebuild the state, with its so disparate components, and thereby also to unify it. He did so with force and energy, but without leniency, mildness, or reconciliation with the old order.[^130]*

This also entailed a new constitution; he had summarily abolished the old one on 30 December 1805. Until the new one took effect in 1819, he — and, from 1816, his successor William I — ruled as in an absolute monarchy. Württemberg was, after all, a fully sovereign state. Emperor Francis II had laid down the imperial crown on 6 August 1806. The mediatized lords of the principalities had become subjects of the new state.

It was foreseeable that the princely houses would have to surrender privileges and rights as well, for instance in policing, jurisdiction, financial administration, and taxation. The King then announced to the Hohenlohe princes, in a single decree, that their lands had fallen to him and his successor to the throne with full sovereignty (exception: Hohenlohe-Schillingsfürst passed to Bavaria; Hohenlohe-Kirchberg did so as well at first, but was later returned). The essential form of government was the issuing of "Royal Decrees."

[p. 159]

FL registered this constitutionally difficult situation in his former territory only at the margins of his attention, but he did correspond with August about it.

A whole series of privileges did remain with the princes: their private property was not touched, and all domains, the right of lower jurisdiction, hunting and fishing rights, and other things that "did not belong essentially to sovereignty" remained theirs; likewise tax exemption for their domains and estates. Of significance for FL, among other things, would have been: the requirement to leave all foreign military service (Prussia now being "foreign"). He would have been permitted to visit his estates abroad only with royal permission (though that would probably not have become a serious problem, since Prince August II already shuttled back and forth between Slaventzitz and Öhringen himself). He would have been obliged, under penalty of forfeiting a quarter of his income, to reside in Stuttgart for at least three months a year, and a good deal more besides.

On the ground in Stuttgart, the Estates Assembly was already in session — strictly speaking, without the basis of a constitution. Draft constitutions from the government authority (the King's Privy Council) were rejected for years, until finally a paper drawn up under his successor William I was accepted.

[p. 160]

## 16 The Correspondence with the Former Private Tutor Kahlert (1807–1818)

### 16.1 Introduction to the Person

The former private tutor Johann Gottlieb Kahlert (1756-1831) had rendered great service in the education of the children of Marianne and FL and had taught them in Breslau, presumably in Ingelfingen, Wiesbaden, and Berlin, that is, he had accompanied them on their travels and shared in their household moves. In all, for fourteen years, from 1789 to 1803, he stood in the Prince's service as private tutor and educator. In numerous letters to his children, FL had him expressly greeted. After the conclusion of this task, Kahlert kept his own household in Breslau, where he was a private scholar or perhaps also a professor — it is unclear which. From the children's grandmother, the Princess Sacken, he received a pension of 500 thalers (Prussian Courant); she had furthermore stipulated that FL must add a further 300 thalers to it.

He became a correspondent of FL.[^131] This appears to be of particular significance for 1807 and 1808, when the latter was living under house arrest in Öhringen as a French prisoner, and in occupied Prussia — and naturally chiefly in Berlin — the preoccupation with the defeat at Jena and Auerstedt and the capitulation of Prenzlau was the topic of the day. Kahlert reported on this (providing, as it were, a press digest) and communicated much about the quite varied "literature"; by this he meant political books and articles in periodicals. The most frequently named author was Massenbach; some works had appeared anonymously. The scene appears quite lively. Kahlert acts like a "capital correspondent," the cities being Berlin and Breslau (the capital of Silesia and his place of residence). Öhringen and then Slaventzitz are the provinces.

[p. 161]

Twenty-six letters can be examined in the Neuenstein archive, five of which fall within the years 1807/08, and thus were sent to Öhringen. Kahlert writes them as an independent free man, with great respect for the recipient. FL also replies and poses questions, as can be gathered from many of the openings of Kahlert's letters. He regularly gave thanks with formulations typical of the age: "[...] as I venture to assure Your Highness that my correspondence is not unwelcome to you, I take the liberty of troubling you anew with my writing." He also had a feel for what might be worth knowing for the "Most Serene Prince and most gracious Lord." From two letters, of 10 December 1807 and 16 April 1808, both of which also refer to preceding letters of the Prince's, the following is quoted by way of example and at somewhat greater length. They yield a picture of the times — one which FL, in Öhringen and then also in Slaventzitz, could otherwise not have obtained.

### 16.2 Kahlert as "Correspondent from the Capital"

Letter of 10 December 1807, from Breslau to Öhringen. The topics are: an (alleged) advance in the property and financial matters that Triebenfeld was pursuing for FL. – The news of a decree of the King abolishing serfdom in the countryside, so that now any citizen could purchase noble estates, which had caused a great sensation,

> *[since] the peasants will now begin to withdraw from all their obligations toward the manorial lords [...] the nobility [is] in a bad position. The King has [with regard to the French authorities, that is, the occupying power] agreed that 270,000 reichsthalers would be paid monthly. [...] Danger to the pension funds for the generals.*

[p. 162]

There follow reports on military persons who would have been known to FL, for instance his former aide-de-camp von Blumenstein, who had received the post of vice-commandant.

Then Kahlert turned to "literary products." Two periodicals with the titles "Feuerbrände" ("Firebrands") and "Die Zeiten" ("The Times") were causing "a great stir, because they mostly contain articles in which men of distinction are attacked." The editor of the "Feuerbrände" was von Coellen (whom we already know as Friedrich von Cölln), who wrote, according to his own account, "to draw the King's attention [to certain matters]." In another journal, the "attacked persons had had defences printed." Concerning von Massenbach a biographical sketch had appeared, intended "to put his merits in their proper light":

> *Another recently published journal, called 'Löscheimer' ('Fire-buckets'), which has the intention of extinguishing the ill impressions that the aforementioned periodicals may have produced, makes it its duty to convey to us laudable anecdotes of the Prussian military, but the Duke of Brunswick is entirely disparaged. In the author's opinion he alone is to blame for the whole calamity; he was said to have been nothing but a mere drill-master who had not kept pace with the spirit of the age, but, proud of his imagined self-conceit, had rejected all better counsel for no reason other than that it did not originate with him. All the reproaches accordingly made against the other commanders of the army would be unjust and fall solely and exclusively upon the Duke. [...] In the newest number of the Annalen there appear, under the joint title Remarks on the Report of an Eyewitness of the Battle of Jena, [some observations]. Here I have read with some pleasure the following statement concerning the Capitulation of Prenzlau. It runs roughly as follows: If the Prince of Hohenlohe had intended nothing further than his own military glory, he would have had*

[p. 163]

> *to fight yet another battle and let the half-starved little band under his command perish. The consequence for the state would have been none other than that Prenzlau would have been burned and some further thousands would have bled in battle. The Prince did not do this, but instead sacrificed himself along with them; he deserves therefore no censure, but rather posterity will erect a column of honor to him for it.*

Letter of 19 April 1808 from Breslau to Öhringen. From it an extensive review of the press is reproduced:

> *[...] I continue, in accordance with your wishes, to give you some news of the latest publications in the matter of literature. [...] Of all other writings, two books at present attract the thinking reading public on account of their interesting content, both recently published in Berlin: The first is called: Materials Toward the History of the Years 1805/1806/1807, Dedicated to His Countrymen by a Prussian. It is composed in the French and German languages and is said to be by the Cabinet Councillor Lombard. The author's unmistakable tendency is, without dispute, to show Minister Haugwitz, and above all the King, in an advantageous light. Of the Duke of Brunswick it is said that, instead of all his former qualities, only two now revealed themselves: indecisiveness and weakness. Age had worn down a man who had counted so many fine moments in his life. He could resolve on nothing before the last man had arrived. We had, nonetheless, a vanguard [...] under the command of General von Tauentzien, who lacked neither talent, enterprise, nor competence, and behind him the corps of the Prince of Hohenlohe, whose reputation as a warrior is well established, p. 208, on him our fate depended, he led the left wing. He would have had to withstand the first blow and the crossings*

[p. 164]

> the interests of the French. Instead, orders from the Duke forced him to withdraw across the river and join the main army, so that the fighting might be done en masse. This left the great gap between the Bohemian [...] and our positions open to the French. The second book worth reading bears the title: 'Portrait of the Social Conditions in the Kingdom of Prussia up to 14 October 1806, by the author of the new Leviathan.' Berlin and Leipzig, 1808. The work contains much that is well considered. Already in the preface the author declares himself against those who have set it in their heads that a state exists only through agriculture. He is convinced that the true strength of a state rests not on physical foundations but on the [assent] of its citizens, and that this assent cannot be spread too widely. There follows a description of a new order of estates. Although I do not agree with the author in many particulars, I must nevertheless confess that in most of them he says a great deal that is true, well considered, and excellent [...] the author certainly deserves to be read. As for the fact that Colonel von Massenbach has had his defense — that he is not the author of the notorious *Gallery of Prussian Characters* [that is, as already noted, Friedrich Buchholz] — inserted in the *Minerva* [another journal], this will probably already be known to Your Serene Highness. I regret with all my heart that he has fallen into this disadvantageous reputation, and I wish that his vindication might convert the very great number of his enemies. It is incomprehensible to me how he has made so many adversaries [sic] for himself among the military, when otherwise everything was in his favor. In the March issue of the political journal, Lieutenant-General von Blücher has, in a satirical manner, had a call to the author inserted in the *Lichtstrahlen*, which accuses him of having brought about the disaster at Prenzlau through his failure to obey the orders of Your Serene Highness. Since it is now

[p. 165]

> claimed that the article in the Prussian journal was also [by] Massenbach, this declaration too is directed against him. [...]

Before a description of the effects of the occupation on the population, particularly the soldiers' families, Kahlert inserted a pleasant story:

> In Glatz a mint has been established which strikes [Bohemian] thalers. Unfortunately, however, a striking error had to occur right at the first die, in that through an oversight [...] the words *Moneta argentea* [...] had been abbreviated thus: Mon. argent., and by chance the one stroke in "Mon." was not stamped on, so that it now read Non. argent., which gave rise to much mockery [...]

Kahlert obtained these publications, which had just recently appeared, from the Frölich bookshop.

In the subsequent letters from FL's time in Öhringen, politics continued to occupy first place: remarks on the occupation and the burden of billeting, the hope for a withdrawal of French troops (expressed as "the departure of the guests"). — An edict on censorship forbade booksellers to stock any writings that contained offensive attacks on the government and the military.

> The booksellers thereupon declared that it was quite simply impossible for them to comply with this order, since they would then have to give up their entire trade, as at present all literary products contain such attacks, and moreover [...] would surely also be permitted to read, since Berlin might well supply [publico] very much, and the whole edict thereupon remained without effect. — With regard to the inequality of billeting, one now hears daily the ugliest complaints.

[p. 166]

> Several very wealthy people have, through French protection, managed [...] to free themselves from it, and the burden now falls with all the greater weight upon the middle class.

In a further letter Kahlert came to speak of von Cölln:

> On all sides people are now busy bringing to light the most odious matters and slandering those who stood, whether in the military or [...], at the head of affairs. One of the most zealous contributors to such pasquinades is the former War Councillor von Coelln, known to Your Serene Highness. I remember very well with what courtesy His Highness received this man at Scheiting [probably Scheidnig, the summer residence near Breslau], and instructed me to show him the finest part of the garden — and now this wretched scribbler has taken the liberty, in the third part of his *Confidential Letters*, of having odious remarks about His Highness's military measures printed as well.

What follows is an account of the deserved punishment of von Coelln, who is also once called "Collini", then further of the action of von Held (see p. 142), and finally he mentioned a writing in which "Triebenfeld in particular is treated in the most disparaging manner, and on this occasion much that is odious is also said about his relationship with Your Serene Highness." There follows again a report on the reception of the capitulation of Prenzlau, Blücher's conduct, and also on the painful death of the Duke of Brunswick, who had been mortally wounded in the Battle of Jena-Auerstedt. — All this FL was, in the opinion of the letter-writer Kahlert, to learn while in Öhringen. But there are also other topics, above all after FL had arrived at Slaventzitz in Silesia.

[p. 167]

### 16.3 Kahlert as Sender of Literary Works and Other Items

Kahlert did not merely inform FL about new publications concerning the events of the war. He also supplied him, in his new provincial solitude, with "literary products" on at least somewhat different subjects. On one occasion he simply copied out a long journal article by hand and sent it to him. FL also asked him directly for "entertaining reading." Kahlert procured the books for him, making use for this purpose also of the Breslau "Streitische Lending Library," which was located near the house "Prince Hohenlohe." The reading lists in letters from the last two years indeed contain "entertaining reading," but also more demanding historical works:

- Charakteristik Friedrich II., installments 1–3.
- Vie privée, politique et militaire de Prince Henri de Prusse.
- Über König Friedrich II. Öffentliche Vorlesungen, gehalten zu Berlin von Adam Müller.
- Anekdoten von König Friedrich II., issues 2–6.
- D. Tralles Erzählung seiner mit König Friedrich dem Großen, der Kaiserin Maria Theresia und der Herzogin Louise Dorothea von Sachsen-Gotha gehaltenen Unterredungen.
- Friedrich des Zweiten Briefwechsel mit seinen Geschwistern, Dukan de Gander.

Another compilation contained:

- Memoires de M. le Baron de Besenval. Parts I and II.
- Mon sejour aupres M. de Voltaire.
- Lebensbeschreibung des Grafen Schwerin.
- Memoires du Prince Eugene de Savoie.
- Heinrich von Bülow.
- Annalou Elisabeth Landgräfin von Hessen.
- Memoiren des Herzogs von Lückinfarn.

[p. 168]

He also named reading matter for the Landgravine Auguste (FL's daughter) as well as for Princess Sophie (FL's sister, who had stood by his side since October 1816).

FL must have been pleased by the news that his former aides-de-camp Pirch and Blumenstein — now bearing the rank of general — intended to visit him. Blumenstein had an inspection tour to southern Silesia ahead of him.

The next letter names the following books:

- Geschäfte Gustavs Waso, König von Schweden von Archenhol.
- Zimmernmanns Verhältnisse mit der Kaiserin Catharina II.
- Peter der Große von Claudius Reisen durch das Innere von Afrika.
- Das Privatleben des Königs von Preußen Friedrich II. u. Voltaire.

The letter of 1 January 1818 begins as follows:

> *As I cannot have the good fortune on this day to offer Your Serene Highness my most respectful congratulations on the new year in person, I beg gracious permission at least to do so in writing, and at the same time to commend myself to the continuance of Your favor.*

It again concerned reading matter for Princess Sophie, who had sent books back to him, "only I did not receive the box with the books until yesterday evening, so I was not in a position to obtain others in exchange on this day, since the library is closed on the bridge days between holidays."

Besides politics and literature, health was almost always a topic as well (particular remedies were requested), on one occasion also "health porcelain," which was unfortunately not available in Breslau. Kahlert asked whether he might order the available "blue porcelain." Also mentioned are spices and baked goods from "Redlich" in Breslau, which he sent to him. An increasingly frequent topic was the two long-married daughters, Adelheid and Auguste, who were at that time

[p. 169]

going through pregnancies. Kahlert recalled them very fondly as children. They also had direct correspondence with him.

The next letter bore the date 27 January. It begins with congratulations on FL's forthcoming birthday (on 31 January) and touches once more on nearly all the usual themes: high politics, fears regarding the outbreak of a war, the exhaustion of the lands, family matters concerning the son Alexander, the delivery of a book by Kotzebue, and "an enclosed box [...] of Redlich's confectionery product in the form of a sugar cake, since these spices are ascribed a very strengthening power." He closes with the formula "to the most serene Princess Sophie I tender my respects and remain with the utmost submission/Most Serene Prince/gracious lord/your most humble servant/Kahlert." This was at the same time the last letter; FL was to die on 15 February.

And one further note: books — indeed, an entire library — did exist at Slaventzitz.[^132] It still existed after the First World War. A few folio volumes from it ended up in the Neuenstein archive. They are predominantly textbooks on the art of war. It seems FL and his guests had no interest in having any more of these sent to them by Kahlert.

[p. 170]

## 17 Family Letters IV (Marianne to Cosmar, 1811, and to August, 1816/17)

Four letters by Marianne are presented here.[^133] For her, political events did not play so decisive a role. What stood in the foreground for her was the separation from her children and her new circumstances of life with her two small daughters. But she knew, of course, learned it from letters, that FL's own circumstances of life had also changed dramatically after the defeat at Jena and Auerstädt, including the surrender of his princely office, his captivity in Öhringen, and his banishment to Upper Silesia. Something of this is reflected here.

FL is never named directly in her extensive correspondence; "the Prince," "August's father," or "Slawentzitz" — that is, the place, or rather the castle — are the usual circumlocutions.

The following letter of 1811 was addressed to Cosmar, the Berlin Councillor of Medicine and Justice, who had attended to Princess Sacken until her death on 6 July 1811. She had appointed him executor of her will and endowed him with considerable rights.

At a meeting in Berlin with her son August, presumably also with Cosmar and her husband Sacken, there must have been a dispute, which Marianne presented as a misunderstanding. The letter is reproduced here because — almost hidden — it touches on several themes that shed light on Marianne's inner state, which must indeed be assessed as unstable, and also hints at "circumstances," by which the economic situation might be meant — or perhaps something else?

> *My dearest Cosmar! I am too occupied, too unhappy on account of the unfortunate misunderstanding, whose effect upon

[p. 171]

> *me you have sufficiently seen, and now not, so far as possible, to forestall the consequences thereof through any account of the truth [sic]. The love of my son and his good opinion is my everything — for he deserves mine unreservedly, and I pay it to him with the most maternal feelings, which are the greatest blessing to my heart. — I wished to write to him myself at once — on second thought it seemed better to me to [...] [give] you this explanation — which I implore you — to communicate to him at once — with all frankness, then — never did I mention to my son anything of our circumstances [see above], with regard to the testaments — of which, moreover, I still know too little word for word — and besides, also in our meetings, I so gladly seek to indulge my heart through the pure outpouring of feeling, both mutually and also as a remembrance of our beloved mother and of my children — thus my son never mentioned his father in this connection, still less was there any talk of what I am supposed to have said — and on the other side, I could never have mentioned anything of it to my husband — since that was not at all the case, and I would likewise never have done so — because all carrying tales back and forth is thoroughly repugnant to my character — still less would I permit myself such an untruth, since truth is the divinity I worship and my highest law on earth. — I never mentioned my son to my husband except in a vain attempt to do justice to how full my heart was of him, of his conduct, of his loving manner toward me and my children. — And besides, it was my outpouring of maternal feeling, and of the most heartfelt gratitude — The one occasion that alone could have given rise to this misunderstanding — was on an occasion and prompting — the mention of which here is indeed necessary for my justification, but is painful to my heart — You know, dear Cosmar, how earnestly Sacken wished to draw closer to my son, for no other

[p. 172]

> reason than that he too, through closer knowledge of his character, might appear to him in a light — that would unite us all more closely — that my husband wished this was natural, and that for me, every day, indeed almost every hour, urged its accomplishment, you know — as well as my own uncomfortable position in the matter — I regarded the relationship in which August stands too clearly to wish to make it still more difficult for him — on the other hand, mine toward my husband was no less pressing; to calm him and dissuade him from the idea, I told him what is really true, what you yourself have often repeated to me — how delicate and complicated August's position is at present in every respect toward his father — and how difficult and burdensome it must also become for both, in consequence, through the testament — wherefore I held it my duty, on my part, to avoid everything that might occasion misunderstandings, bitterness, and the Prince's displeasure toward my son. — Sacken recognized this with deep feeling, with tearful eyes — and pressed me no further — You must swear to me likewise so [...] — that he did not quite understand this, or interpreted it otherwise, I did not suspect — for I was glad to cut short this discussion, and did not indeed grant it — how little, with the purest of intentions, could I have suspected that I would bring such grief upon myself. Once more, dear Cosmar! The love of my son is a treasure that must be preserved to me undimmed, if I am not to lose everything along with it — this explanation cannot be a matter of indifference to him either. I am indescribably despondent — this has affected me inhumanly — and because he is with the King — these are the reasons that today deprive me of the pleasure of seeing him — instead I expect from you tomorrow some reassurance, and from him, that he may manage to give me the evening, since I depart on Friday./Marianne Sacken*

[p. 173]

This letter was furnished by its addressee, Cosmar, on 24 July 1811, with an addition, and forwarded to "Your Serene Highness" August.

What can we glean from this letter?

- On the one hand, there was a meeting at Marianne's residence in Berlin with August, Marianne's husband Sacken, and Cosmar, at which a quarrel evidently arose between Sacken and August; the "misunderstanding" in Marianne's own phrasing. It was probably about the will and financial matters.
- Von Sacken, in Marianne's view, wished to draw somewhat closer to August.
- August had an appointment with the King, presumably that of Württemberg.
- August's situation is "delicat" and complicated with regard to consideration for his father.
- At least in the remark that she had previously regarded the "relationship in which August stands" as too good, one must think of the economic situation.
- The true reason for the quarrel must have been the utterance of something she did not wish to have said, and which probably had to do with the effects of the will.
- Her greatest fear is that she might lose August's love.

The background to the fear of difficult circumstances for August could be that, around this time, following the incorporation of Hohenlohe, the Kingdom of Württemberg "took into administration" the local "Kameralverwaltung" (financial administration) of Hohenlohe-Öhringen,[^134] presumably on account of the burden of debt that August had had to take on there.

Or did August wish to achieve something specific with the Prussian King in these days? Frederick William III had already granted his discharge from the Prussian army in 1806. He, the King of Prussia, was of course not responsible for Württemberg, but

[p. 174]

he was responsible for almost everything connected with FL. So: the debts again? Or the reorganization of the Prussian military apparatus? The former was a perennial subject, the latter had just been discussed as an intention, and some held that FL should also play a role in it (see p. 119). — So much for a speculative glance into the future.

The next of Marianne's letters to be presented bears the date 24 October 1816 and was written in Bellin. We find family matters, and once again the treatment of a financial problem:

> *My beloved August!/I have just returned from Leipzig and Halle, where I spent very happy days in the company of my good Auguste, her respectable and amiable husband, and Brother Adolph — on the return journey I spent 2 days in Berlin dans les horrens des Affairs: which have now reached, for each of you, the height of confusion. Cosmar has been very harshly accused by a second sentence, yet no other has been appointed in his place: he has quite lost his head and is more incapable than ever, and has even less good will to conduct our affairs. He cannot now become worse — for this reason, out of all the revolutions, a [...] boundary must arise for me — and since the old state of things was as bad as could possibly be, it can only get better — only the present is unbearable. I too am caught in the most fatal complications, since Count Hardenberg in Vienna holds a capital of 43,000 Rth. in the Bellin estates, and indeed as first mortgage. The good man is a moneylender, he torments and presses me without ceasing; Adolph has therefore expressed to Cosmar the wish that, instead of his moneys being placed and remaining on foreign mortgages, Hardenberg should be directed to other mortgages ahead of his own, and that in exchange the 43,000 should be acquired as first mortgage in Bellin for Adolph; all the more so since this is entirely secure — as these moneys belong to the first mortgage. Cosmar is not disinclined toward this either —

[p. 175]

> however much he otherwise ruled as a sovereign, he will now conduct this business only if [...] you and all your siblings, as testamentary substitutes of Adolph in the Majorat, declare yourselves by deed: that you not merely approve, but indeed desire, the transfer of the 43,000 out of Adolph's estate onto Bellin, and the acquisition in exchange of the Hardenberg claim, all the more so since Adolph has claims on the Bellin estates that mostly rank behind these 43,000 Rth. I believe that your [brothers-in-law] must co-sign the deed as honest curators of their wives. This would relieve me of a great burden, until I can [...] arrive at the fulfilment of my wish concerning the estate [...] — to which I likewise hope, through your kind and loving assistance, in the sense in which I have already asked you before, to arrive in time. Forgive me, good August! for having to burden you so much with my tiresome business. — But so long as they oppress and torment — and if I must now bore you and others with it too, then one feels, in my view, a small [...] of purgatory. I shall reserve for another letter soon the pleasure of entertaining you with better subjects. Angelika and Auguste send you their best regards — and I ask you to commend me to your beloved Louise and to embrace your children in my name. — I also ask you to greet Alexander briefly from me; it has grieved me greatly not to have seen him, I wish it so dearly. — It was one of his comrades in Berlin, a certain von Maltitz — I would gladly have spoken with him, but I could not manage it. Do put in a good word for me with him, dear August, that he might send me his portrait — I know that he has recently had himself painted, so I ask only for a copy of it, in miniature. Farewell, good August/Your tenderly loving mother Marianne*

[p. 176]

What is to be noted here is the clear distance from Cosmar, who will later also relinquish his duties for the family. But what of Hardenberg (1750–1822)? In his various capacities for the Prussian state, first in Ansbach-Bayreuth and then in the negotiations for the Peace of Basel, he had stood in close, and presumably also friendly, contact with FL. He evidently maintained the relationship with Marianne, or with her mother, when in 1799 Marianne needed a new home (and did not go to Oppurg, as had indeed first been considered, see p. 79), but instead, presumably in 1803, moved into the manor of Bellin with her new partner/husband Count Osten-Sacken.

Hardenberg was its previous owner (since 1781; for 1802/03, that is a very brief period immediately before the change of hands, a v. Lepel is also listed)! At the time of purchase there must have been financial arrangements from which he now wished to withdraw. In 1815/16 he negotiated in Vienna for Prussia. It remains unclear why he now pressed Marianne to redeem the 43,000 reichsthalers. She, in any case, was not delighted about it ("money-lender"). With the son Adolph's willingness to help (his name is spelled alternately with "ph" or "f"), she apparently found a solution. – Until now one might have thought that financial troubles were confined to FL, but no! Marianne herself had, on the neighboring estates of her second husband in Mecklenburg, debts of 96,200 thalers in the year 1809, toward the settlement of which she intended to apply the annual 4,000 thalers from the Transact.[^135]

Another letter:

> *Berlin, 13 April 1817/Beloved August/After my long silence my heart longs once again to behold the features of your hand. I wrote you two letters, which remained unanswered, which perhaps you did not receive. – Let us therefore forget the past and grant my present request, and let me soon come into possession of a fine page of your hand in which I may find the assurance of your approval

[p. 177]

> [sic] and of your continuing love for me. – Through your sisters I received news of you, and followed you with faithful remembrance and heartfelt sympathy on all your ways; I rejoiced heartily at your good relations with the King of W. – and even more at the joy of the reunion in the [home] of your own. – on the beautiful moment that led you into the arms of your Louise and your angel children – I wish you happiness and rejoice in your happiness, good August! – Forgive me my egotism, good dear August. – yet with every thought of reunion the longed-for moment also stands before me, of one day enjoying this same joy myself, and with it seeing the fulfillment of my most heartfelt wish confirmed – too vividly before my eyes for me not finally to seek to bring the reality nearer to myself – Good August! Would this not be possible on your return journey? – I await from you a sketch of a plan, the working-out of which would be my finest care and joy, especially if I might have the happiness of making the acquaintance of your Louise and your angel children. – How happy my two girls would feel to see you again, and what joy for me to be able to present them to you now. I enjoy the pleasure of seeing Adolph often. He pursues his Wilhelminian career with great zeal and punctuality, and thereby earns praise and esteem from his superiors – the Uhlan uniform suits him very well – and in that respect he cuts a very fine figure – to be sure his appointment as Lieutenant is not brilliant – but since the royal princes must begin with that – nothing more can be said about it; he will certainly not remain long in the subaltern ranks, and I know from his commanding officer himself that he will very soon be proposed for Cavalry Captain – meanwhile his existence here is so agreeable that it can well be borne with patience for a little while longer – he knows that I am writing to you, and charges me with many heartfelt greetings to you – I am to convey to you very many compliments

[p. 178]

> from G. Pfuhl, the W. envoy – he is very devoted to you – and speaks to me quite often of you, August! – He seems a worthy, good man, who knows how to judge without prejudice or one-sided regard. He has also said much good of Alexander to me – you can easily imagine how his conversation on subjects so dear to me made it very interesting for me – I shall leave Berlin the day after tomorrow to travel to Bellin, where I beg you to send your letters to me. – Now, dear August: I close with the most heartfelt thanks – which, though on the last page of my letter, surely stand in the right place in my heart – truly for your kind and loving attention and completion of the two matters here so important, the documents; if I never come to my end with it, then your mutual friendly cooperation, all of you, will increase its value for me, whether and when I attain to it – I must expect from time; the particulars of it you may learn from your father, who knows the whole course of the matter and has taken so noble and kind an interest in it. Give my regards to your beloved Louise and embrace your dear children for me – Angelique and Auguste commend themselves to your loving remembrance./Farewell, dear August/ever your tenderly loving mother/Marianne/At Adolph's request I open this letter again – he begs your forgiveness a thousand times over for not yet having answered you – but the drill hours and his duty overwhelm his time and strength – he sends you the enclosed with the wish that it might have your approval [sic].*

A true mother's letter, in which Marianne struggles hard for the love of her eldest son. Had she really never seen his wife and children until now? August has been married since 1811. He knew his mother's daughters from her second marriage, but had not seen the now 14- and 16-year-olds for a long time. – Striking how well she is versed in

[p. 179]

military matters. – For the first time she writes to him of his father ("noble and kind"). He will die in less than a year.

Another letter to August of 17 October 1817:

> *My dearly beloved August!*  
> *With the most heartfelt feelings of love and gratitude I bring you the expression of the same for your loving letter, and your kind remembrance of my birthday. The manner in which this was celebrated this year bound me, indeed, with beautiful ties to life and let me find in it a great worth never before found. – Good August – only you were missing, for the days to have been lived through in perfect happiness. – Emilie will however testify to you how your remembrance and the heartfelt wish for your presence were ever close to us – my longing to see you again and to introduce Angelika and Auguste to you is indescribably great – A pure feeling, which is founded, indeed, more on trust in your love than on an excess of modesty, lets me hope that you too would share our joy in our midst. – I tell you nothing, my good August! of our Baroness here in Bovden, nothing of my feeling upon seeing my children again; Emilie will surely take charge of the [organization] of the celebration, and the [...] cannot be described in words. So let your own heart paint for you this, for me, so beautiful, so blessed moment: – Thank God that Emilie's health permitted her to enjoy it as well – her children are my whole passion, and truly dear, lovable little creatures. Emilie made me the gift of the beautiful painting of your Mathilde, who through this magnetism a distance has already stolen herself so into my heart that I cannot at all detach myself from the lovely little figure – I take you at your word, good August – and beg you indeed to keep your promise, and to send me still the paintings of the two brothers of this little angel; – kiss this dear*

[p. 180]

> *little trio warmly for me and commend me most kindly to their mother – I learned of the danger in which she found herself – hers and your two [children] besides, my good August – just as I certainly feel with you, deeply, faithfully and honestly, your joys and everything that concerns you – distance will never weaken the devotion which a mother who loves you inexpressibly has dedicated to you forever [...] the moments of happiness were only too short for me – and those of separation and pain followed only too closely upon one another – moreover, 'on m'a fait mourir a petit feu' – for first the parting from my so dearly beloved Adele – then the separation in Cassel from Auguste – but especially from my Emilie, your Albert, whom I also love infinitely – and from the dear children – moved me more than I can describe, – I remained there another day and a half, whose finding again did my heart indescribable good: His outward appearance, which must please less biased eyes than those of a mother – united with the heartfelt feeling, with the great love and attachment which he shows me at once and too vividly for him in my heart – so that the separation from him was not very painful to me – although the hope of knowing him closer to me in future, seeing at last fulfilled a wish ever dear to my heart, ought to have eased the parting for me. – Now I am back here again – everything bleak and empty, I might say horribly empty in this house where but a few days ago only glad, happy hours passed for me in the circle of my loved ones – I remain until Monday in this solitude, because I must await the post. Then I hasten by way of Hanover home, where I await with longing letters and news from you, my beloved ones! I almost forgot to tell you that I saw Mr. Gustaw in Doberan a few days after he left you in Canstatt – I rejoiced doubly at this because he told me of you and your Louise. His change I noticed less, because I had seen him more often*

[p. 181]

> *— moreover, comme il n'a jamais peche par la Beaute, so he had little to lose there for me, and I find that he has gained in the jour [...] and in point of amiability – to be sure, the costume which he had carried over from Switzerland to our North, and in which he assured me he had presented himself to you – was as strikingly hideous as possible – yet he still knows how to appreciate beauty and good taste as before – and gave me proof of his adoring reverence for your Louise – let Emilie tell you the particulars of this.*  
> *Adieu, good August! Angelique and Auguste commend themselves to your love, as I do to your heartfelt remembrance,*  
> *your mother who loves you dearly, Marianne*  
> *I ask that you give the enclosure to Emilie.*

Marianne had celebrated her birthday (6 October); she was now 54 years old. The place name "Bovden" may refer to Bovenden in southern Lower Saxony (formerly the Herrschaft Plesse, she calls it "our Baroness"), where parts of the family had met and from where she had traveled home via Hanover. Not a word about her marriage or her husband. She was proud of her daughters.

The choice of words expressing her love, to virtually everyone, ("heartfelt," "most heartfelt," also "longing," repeatedly used), appears rather stereotyped. – Is this Biedermeier style or specific to Marianne? She surely still had something to make up for or work through (the separation of 1799); it is fairly certain that she suffered from this her whole long life; the document "A Word of Trust" (see p. 83f.) she had indeed deposited, but who knows of it? – To assess the separation nowadays, after more than 200 years, as an emancipatory act by a woman may seem tempting; but for this too we have no basis. The long sentence

[p. 182]

> *distance will never weaken the devotion which a mother who loves you inexpressibly has dedicated to you forever [...] the moments of happiness were only too short for me – and those of separation and pain followed only too closely upon one another*

certainly referred to the parting after the birthday celebration, though it also "fits" the separation of 1799. Over-interpretation?

[p. 183]

## 18 Sophie Christiane Louise, Princess of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen

She, FL's sister sixteen years his junior, has already been mentioned several times, above all because, living unmarried in Ingelfingen, she had taken charge of the children after 1799. Marianne, their mother, knew the children were in good hands with her. She had come to value Sophie Christiane during visits to Ingelfingen, and perhaps had even grown fond of her sister-in-law, who was nearly the same age as herself.

In 1814 Sophie Christiane had visited FL in Slaventzitz; at any rate, he reported in a letter on her return journey. Toward the end of his life she stood intensively by his side and must have been especially close to him. She came to her brother in Upper Silesia in October 1816, despite the long journey from Ingelfingen, where she still lived in the Princesses' Wing. She took on incidental costs — for example, replacing horses — and gave his daughter Auguste a new carriage, among other things. After his death, Sophie Christiane remained on the spot for several more weeks. To August she wrote after FL's death:

> *You have done infinitely much for the departed, but could not possibly fulfil all his wishes, since your situation did not allow it. Even in the last period you granted what lay within your power, by paying the outstanding salaries of his servants, by approving the certain 4,000 thalers for the acquisition of Bytkow, all of which greatly calmed and pleased him. He often said: August has after all come to love me dearly; how good that he came here and saw everything for himself. If only it had happened sooner! Louise is a clever woman; she contributed much to it. Well, my blessing remains with you both; I wish for nothing but your well-being.*[^136]

[p. 184]

August thus committed himself financially, taking over the remaining debts from the purchase of the Bytkow estate. The outstanding salaries were settled either by him or by Sophie Christiane.

Sophie Christiane Louise of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen returned to Ingelfingen on 20 April 1818. She died in 1831 and found her final resting place in the Princes' Vault between her parents. The castle at Slaventzitz continued to be used by the family.

[p. 185]

## 19 Letters That Still Reached FL Before His Death (1818)

Only a few of these selected letters referred to a serious illness, present or past. Of particular interest are those from his nephew Eduard and his sons Alexander and Adolph, since few testimonies from them survive. They report on their everyday lives.

### 19.1 Sender: Frederick William III

King Frederick William III responded to the customary New Year's greeting, but also included an additional personal message; some are addressed to FL, since his birthday is, or had been, on 31 January. For several years, as may be inferred from the King's responses, FL had regularly sent greetings to Frederick William at the turn of the year, which were likewise reciprocated. In 1818 this looked as follows (no salutation):

> *From Your Serene Highness's letter of the 1st of this month, I have had the pleasure of learning of the good wishes which you express for me on the occasion of the new year's beginning. I recognize therein your devoted and loyal sentiments, and it shall give me joy if the future also proves fortunate for you. With regard to the appointment of your son, I refer Myself and my letter of [...], which will by now already have reached Your Serene Highness.*  
> *Berlin, 16 January 1818*  
> *Frederick William*[^137]

The remark "the appointment of your son" refers to Alexander, who, after his military training in Stuttgart, had transferred to Prussia.

[p. 186]

### 19.2 Sender Johann Gottlieb Kahlert

Kahlert's letters (ch. 16) must have been a highly welcome distraction for FL from all his troubles. The last letter, which reached him about two weeks before his death and bears witness to the former tutor's deep attachment, is treated here as a family letter and reproduced as a copy of the original — note the handwriting:[^138]

*[illustration: reproduction of a handwritten letter in old German cursive script]*

Salutation and beginning of the letter

[p. 187]

*[illustration: reproduction of the closing formula and signature of a handwritten letter, dated Breslau, 27 Jan. 1818, signed Kahlert]*

Closing formula and signature by Kahlert

### 19.3 Letter from His Nephew Eduard

Eduard von Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen (1801–1830) was the son of Georg von Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen (1757–1803), FL's younger brother. Eduard had lost his parents early; his mother, née Baroness von Klüchzner, also died in 1807. He then grew up in Öhringen. A letter from the ten-year-old, in French, to "Mon très cher Oncle," written in pen on lined paper in a careful hand, has survived. The uncle was already living in Slaventzitz at that time, but the boy knew of him and this was not the first time he had written — chiefly about his progress in French. There must have been at least an occasional correspondence, for FL had presumably asked him at the end of 1817 to report on his studies.

The following last letter, dated 23 January 1818, to Slaventzitz is reproduced here together with a number of grammatical obscurities, although — or precisely because — it is also a

[p. 188]

document of its time from a student (admittedly a particularly well-off one) at the University of Tübingen:

> *Gracious Uncle!*  
> *For your gracious wishes on my birthday I give humble thanks and add my most heartfelt wishes for your welfare and long life, to which wishes your approaching birthday gives me occasion. Gracious Uncle! live happily and joyfully — these are the best wishes I can offer you for your birthday celebration. In accordance with your gracious command I shall give you a description of my courses. From 8 to 9 o'clock I attend Professor Dr. [Schuebler]'s lectures on Agronomy. This is a mixture of chemistry, agriculture, geography, and mineralogy; on the whole the ensemble is quite good, and it teaches the [components] of good and poor soils and what vegetables thrive best in them. There is also somewhat less physics involved, particularly with regard to the physical properties of soil types. Prof. Schuebler has come from [G.], where he delivered the lectures, and did not have time to work everything out fully, but it is to be hoped that next half-year he will certainly lecture quite well. From 9 to 10 o'clock I ride at the riding school here, which is quite well appointed and where I have already made fairly good progress. From 10 to 11 o'clock I attend Chemistry with Prof. Christian Gmelin. He has only just returned from distant travels, where in Stockholm, London, and Paris he visited and heard the great Schweiker everywhere. He lives quite in a lofty sphere and combines with his lectures a good many experiments, in which he is especially strong. From 11 to 12 I attend Euclid with P. Pfleiderer. This Pfleiderer is a very great mathematician, but unfortunately a man of 86 years who is also beginning to falter in speech, so that in the end one can no longer understand him at all. From 2 to 3 o'clock I attend Analytical Geometry with Prof. Bodenberger, who is an extremely skilful man. He also analyzes conic sections at the same time. From 5 to 6 o'clock I attend the Introduction to*

[p. 189]

> *Theoretical Philosophy with Prof. Sigwart, who was formerly tutor to Gustav von Langenburg. His delivery is somewhat monotonous, but otherwise what he presents is excellent. Herr von [Forstner] has now also been appointed here as a professor, after a new cameralistic faculty was opened, in which he too managed to find a post. He has already begun lecturing and has met with complete approval, only his manner of expression is far too high-flown. The Catholic faculty is now also here, under the Landvogt of Ellwangen, [Herr] von Heller, who was formerly in Öhringen; his son is now also studying here and sometimes comes to celebrate with us. The son of Privy Councillor von Braun is likewise here. Yesterday I received a letter from Adele, who wrote to me that Louise in Öhringen is very ill, which grieves me greatly for August's sake, who was just at Fürstenau for the wedding of Sister Albrecht's. Would you have the graciousness to pass on the enclosed little letter to my aunt; and so, repeating my wishes for your welfare, I remain, with reverence, your obedient [nephew/Eduard von Hohenlohe[^139]*

One could verify this at the Archive of the University of Tübingen. Riding from 9 to 10 o'clock! A career from tutor in Langenburg (Prof. Sigwart) to professor of philosophy seems noteworthy. Eduard had contact with the studying sons of court officials. The mention of an illness of Louise, August's wife, attests to how close the family exchange of news was. August wrote more precisely about this in his (last) letter to his father; see p. 192f.

### 19.4 Two Letters from His Son Alexander

With Alexander, born 1798, FL kept up occasional letter contact after 1809, partly in French. After his early childhood in Ingelfingen, he had come, like Adolf — who was probably born a year earlier —

[p. 190]

to Öhringen, to his brother August. There he soon had, as a companion, his cousin Eduard, of roughly the same age (two letters written by the two of them in French, not reproduced here, closely resemble each other). Alexander was the object of FL's worries over the restrictions imposed by the King of Württemberg. Two letters written by him in close succession are reproduced below:[^140]

Dated 13 January 1818, he wrote:

> *Dearest Father! I cannot describe how glad I am to learn from Aunt of your recovery, yet now, best of fathers, I hope that you do not doubt my ever-faithful and childlike reverence and childlike love [...]. I learned of your illness here very late, from Louis von Kirchberg, who was here [...], but fortunately I learned through a letter from Adele from Fürstenau that you, best of fathers, were once more on the road to recovery. That God may grant you a truly firm health for a good long time yet is my highest wish and my daily prayer. August [is] here at this moment, in order [I believe] to arrange lodgings in Cannstadt for his wife, who wishes to take the waters there [if this is correct, the letter was presumably written in Stuttgart]. That I have not yet myself hastened into your arms and expressed my childlike feelings to you in person is the fault of the new military organization, which is expected daily. After a brief drill regulation [...] to which I am assigned, the present drilling season is being put to very heavy use. The King is not infrequently present at our exercises and seems to have much patience with us. Once this is settled, my first business will be to request leave to come to you, my father, and I hope that this so agreeable moment is no longer far off. I am consumed with impatience to see you again, best of fathers, after so long a time,

[p. 191]

> *and also to come to an understanding with you on one point, which I do not wish to entrust to the pen, but which is of the utmost importance to me. I share brotherly in August's misfortune; she will, I hope, not doubt this. — Emilie's confinement went off very well. Both she and the child are enjoying the best of health. The weather here is splendid, and everything here gives grounds to hope for a truly fruitful year, which would be much to be wished, since grain prices have risen to the highest degree — for example, a monthly ration of horse-feed costs some 36 reichsthalers, where it formerly cost only 11 reichsthalers./Be so gracious, best of fathers, as to commend me to all those around you. — With the deepest respect and childlike love I remain/Your obedient son and Alexander Hohenlohe*

Not two weeks later, on 27 January, the next letter follows. This one must have been written in Berlin, for the King was worried, as we read, on account of the measles epidemic prevailing there:

> *Dear Father, Would that I, dear father, might follow this letter in person, so as to bring you in speech my childlike feelings and good wishes for your birthday. Only your well-being can be the foundation of my own, and this sheet would be too small for my wishes. The King is still unwell and is being especially careful this time, because measles is going about here, and already three royal princes and just as many princesses are laid low with this illness. This is the reason why I have not yet been presented to the King. I expect it daily, however. Since the Court, for fear of the measles, lives entirely in seclusion, I have nonetheless paid visits in other houses, as for instance to Count Moltke. — Adolf has at this moment gone away for a few days to Count Lenau's, for his hunts. I too was*

[p. 192]

> *invited, but could not accept this kind invitation on account of my present situation. — To my very great annoyance I have learned from Auguste that Carl von Hessen-Philippsthal has become not a Russian but a Hessian general. This letter, I hope, will find you again in good health. Be so gracious as to commend me to gracious Aunt's favour, and likewise to those gentlemen who remember me. With the greatest respect and childlike love I remain/Your obedient son/Alexander*

### 19.5 Letter from his son Adolph

> *Dear Father!/It was with the most heartfelt regret that I learned through Lombardini that your health, so precious to me, has still not improved; it would make me very happy, and I might perhaps be able to entertain you – to be permitted to help shorten the time for you. Rest assured, my dearest Father, that I feel happy only in the fulfilment of my duties toward you. I have therefore instructed Lombardini to inform me, should you permit it, that I may keep you company. Prince and Prince Wilhelm, as well as Prince Blücher, who show me much favor, inquire diligently after you and send you their best regards./In faithful love I remain your obedient son Adolph H./Berlin, 8th Febr. 1818[^141]*

### 19.6 Letter from the Son August of 22 January 1818

> *Dear Father! Your forthcoming birthday gives me the occasion, so gladdening to my heart, to express toward you those filial wishes and feelings which I constantly hold for you. May God grant you, dearest Father, health and contentment in full measure, and grant me the happiness of always possessing your paternal love and your goodwill.*

[p. 193]

> *My good wife, who is devoted to you with all her heart and grateful for so many proofs of your kind disposition, joins her heartfelt wishes for your well-being to mine, and is truly grieved that she cannot convey them to you herself in writing, but unfortunately her constant sufferings render her unable to hold the pen for you. When I returned from the wedding of Count Carl von Erbach at Fürstenau, I found poor Louise once again bedridden with a bilious-mucous fever, which so wore her down that, although she has lately recovered tolerably, she still cannot take a single step across the room without being supported by two persons. This great weakness naturally arouses my lively concern for the moment of her confinement, which I consider to be nearer than Louise herself does, [...], but her strength leads me to believe that it might come sooner. The children are, thank God, quite well, and Fritz is visibly benefiting under his new [...]. We all heard with great interest that you, dear Father, are now better satisfied with your health. May its firmness fully correspond to our wishes. Mine is likewise not particularly good, and the complete lack of hunting this winter — since throughout all of Hohenlohe we must forgo all [...] hunts owing to the quite small number of [...] — also robs me of the foremost occasion for exercise, which is always beneficial. Be so good as to thank dear Aunt on my behalf for her kind, pleasant letter of [...]. From it I learned that Gen. v. Kirch had written that Alexander has been appointed to the Garde du Corps, which pleased me; I therefore believe him to be no longer at Schlaventzitz. Aunt wishes to hear something of Louise's second lady, Countess [...], of whom Alexander told her [...]; it had indeed been her plan to keep this quite charming young girl at least for some time as a companion, but unfortunately her mother was so seriously ill that she could not spare the care of her daughter — and Countess [...] therefore left us again for good after a*

[p. 194]

> *sojourn of 6 weeks, to the regret of both sides, since she had become [...] attached, and she herself is a most agreeable companion and especially very musical. A short while ago we very nearly lost good old Ohlnhausen, who lay dangerously ill with an inflammation of the abdomen, but has fully recovered./With most filial reverence and love I remain, dearest Father/your most obedient son/August[^142]*

Note: There are also letters from the daughters to their father FL. They extend up to the year 1817.[^143] For reasons of space, they had to be omitted here.

[p. 195]

## 20 The Final Phase of Friedrich Ludwig's Life

FL did not fare well in his old age; besides his severe short-sightedness, he had been plagued for years by attacks of gout. The sequence of courses at table had long remained that of a princely court. Toward petitioners he behaved generously. Polis, his master of the horse and successor to Bally, died before FL did. His son August occasionally made arrangements directly with the "Master of the Household" Lombardini.

Blumenstein, his former aide-de-camp, had risen in the military and visited him on the occasion of a troop inspection. Pirch presumably came still later. From him comes a horrified report on FL's outward appearance and the meager hospitality he offered. FL was soon unable to pay his servants; the wages still owed at his death were assumed by his son August (see p. 203f.). The Governor's Palace in Dresden, which had suffered only minor damage in the fighting (and had been repaired), and the summer house in Alt-Scheitnig were rented out. Blücher lodged there on several occasions. Works of art acquired before 1806 had to be sold off, as did carriages and sledges. This too was presumably the task of Court Councillor Hahn. The entire estate at Alt-Scheitnig, together with the park FL had laid out there, was sold privately and taken over by the city of Breslau in 1815.

With Hohenlohe, with Ingelfingen or Öhringen as his former residences, he had had very little to do since his "banishment" by the King of Württemberg. Nevertheless, Öhringen administrative officials occasionally turned to him. He maintained regular correspondence with the Director of Salines Glenck and with Herr Kahlert, as well as with his son August.

In Hohenlohe, too, August had already done good work earlier, in 1808. As head of the Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen-Öhringen line, he had

[p. 196]

renounced, in settlement of an old inheritance dispute, both the Old-Ingelfingen third share in the Langenburg half and the claim to the Öhringen half of the Upper County of Gleichen, in favor of Hohenlohe-Kirchberg and Hohenlohe-Langenburg — this sounds exactly as complicated as it in fact was, as a consequence of the constant territorial divisions that occurred before the introduction of primogeniture.

FL received visits from his family: his son and successor at Öhringen, Prince August II, married since 1811 to Louise of Württemberg, a niece of the King, came several times; other children and relatives also came from time to time: thus in 1810 his daughter Emilie, on her wedding journey with her husband Albrecht, Count of Erbach-Fürstenau; in 1814 his son Alexander; in 1815 his daughter Adele (Adelheid, married to Ludwig, Prince of Hohenlohe-Kirchberg) together with her sister-in-law Princess Louise.

### 20.1 The End of the Life of the Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen

Friedrich Ludwig, Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, died on 15 February 1818 in Slaventzitz and was buried there as well. General von Röder made his death known to the public. All the officers of the garrison of Kosel, situated 12 km to the southwest, paid him their last respects.

There was much to be settled, and the creditors came forward. Thus also came Goldstecker, mentioned by Friedrich von Cölln (see p. 101), with whom Hahn in Breslau had also had dealings, and he immediately affixed his seals to the estate. What von Cölln had meant by the "dumping of South Prussian estates" has already been described in detail. It was known, up to the King himself, that FL's mountain of debt was so dramatically high that intervention had become necessary. The inventory and debt lists repeatedly compiled by Triebenfeld (see appendix, pp. 239/240) still played a role as late as the Congress of Vienna in 1815!

Now,

[p. 197]

after FL's death, the estates of Betsche, Zagorow, Trobeczyn, Borkowo, and Wieniec were quickly sold once more (thus Fischer,[^144] though according to other sources[^145] FL had already begun this himself beforehand, as for instance in 1815 with the property at Alt-Scheidnigg as well). The area around Zagorow now belonged to Congress Poland, that is, to the Tsarist Empire of Russia, which did not make Triebenfeld's task on FL's behalf any easier, since he was indeed present in Vienna. He had tried to obtain compensation for the estates of FL's that had been lost there. For Bytkow and Slaventzitz, both in Silesia, protection existed through the entail (Fideikommiss) secured by the Princess Sacken. Koschentin was in the end taken over by Adolf.

*[illustration: engraving of Friedrich Ludwig's grave monument at Slaventzitz, a stone tomb bearing the Hohenlohe coat of arms, set among trees]*

The magnificent grave monument of Friedrich Ludwig von Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen in Slaventzitz, bearing the Hohenlohe coat of arms. (no longer extant today, artist and year unknown)

[p. 198]

### 20.2 The End of Marianne's Life

Marianne died on 20 April 1840 in Mecklenburg, where she had lived for nearly 40 years, at the Marienhof built by the family in 1837 near the Bellin manor house where she had previously resided. Its manor building has since been much altered, unlike the entrance area of the manor courtyard, which still represents the scale of the complex.

Prince August, her firstborn son and by then already resident at Slaventzitz, had met with his mother in Berlin only shortly before. To what extent regular contact otherwise existed is unknown. Her two daughters, Angelika and Auguste, had already been married in Mecklenburg for more than 15 years by the time of her death, and there were presumably no longer any relations with their father, Count Sacken (but once again, take note: there are no corresponding documents in the Hohenlohe Archive). Marianne was interred in the vault of the church in Bellin.

August invited to his home in Upper Silesia a family gathering of the Hohenlohes, the joint children of Marianne and FL, for a family reunion. Of the siblings, however, Auguste and Alexander had already died in 1816 and 1829 respectively.

Memorial services were held on 1 and 4 June, the first in the Protestant church of Jakobswalde, presumably for the general public, the second in Laskowitz, conducted by a pastor from Budkowitz. Verbatim, very carefully prepared transcripts of both addresses survive, each about ten pages long. Some quotations and remarks on them follow:

In both addresses[^146], the deceased is named at the outset as "the late Lady Princess Mother of His Serene Highness the Lord Prince August of Hohenlohe-Öhringen-Slaventzitz, Amalie Louise, Serene Highness," or as "the late departed Mother of His Serene Highness the reigning Prince and Lord of Hohenlohe-Öhringen-Slaventzitz" (the latter on 4 June in Laskowitz). Indeed, apart from the children and their spouses and perhaps the grandchildren, no one there had

[p. 199]

*[illustration: photograph of the entrance area of Schlossgut Bellin, captioned "Eingangsbereich des Schlossguts Bellin"]*

*[illustration: photograph of the fieldstone church in Bellin, Marianne's burial church, captioned "Die Feldsteinkirche in Bellin, Grabeskirche von Marianne"]*

[p. 200]
