known her at all. It is therefore no surprise that the address is concerned predominantly with the grief of Prince August, his mourning for his mother. He stands at the center. A biography of the deceased is not the subject. Anyone who — the author admits — upon first reading the two extensive texts had expectations regarding the great turning point in the Princess's life, the separation from FL and the children, then still small, will find not a single word on the matter!

In the sermon of 1 June, the pastor, after paying homage to "our most serene, most gracious Prince and Lord," who "as the eldest of the children mourns a dearly beloved mother," turns briefly to the deceased, giving her name, date of birth, the early loss of her father, her mother's second marriage to Prince Sacken, and her own marriage to the then Most Serene Lord Hereditary Prince Friedrich Ludwig zu Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, and the children of that marriage (that is, only those from this marriage). Nothing more is said of her life, of anything personal. There follow examples from biblical stories concerning death and maternal love; then back again to Prince August and his loss of his mother. God, death, rank and birth, and faith are of course also discussed; theologically demanding, yet (one may say so) remaining within a conventional framework. Of the second marriage and the two further daughters, not a word.

The further "Memorial Address on the Death of the Late Departed Mother of His Serene Highness the Reigning Prince and Lord of Hohenlohe-Öhringen-Slaventzitz, delivered on 4 June at Laskowitz" was no less devout toward the Prince. Nevertheless, the text engages intensively with the "mighty of every tongue," with the advantages of their exalted station, with "excess and dissolution," "outrages and misdeeds"! It sets against them the God-fearing and charitable, righteous, exemplary, and virtuous souls.

[p. 201]

> *One thing among all that has been named still endures, one thing has forever remained ineradicable; one thing bears witness to her former existence and to herself and her convictions and articles of faith, her wishes and the state of her conscience, and her words and deeds. Can you then ask: Where? — Certainly not among us; only with God alone, the All-Knowing and All-Righteous, who metes out reward and punishment according to merit, and more surely than anyone else could.*

The passage "not among us; only with God alone," avoiding earthly judgment, occurring still in the final third of the text, in which "excess and dissolution" and "outrages and misdeeds" had been denounced, gives one somewhat pause, but is probably meant rather abstractly, without reference to any real facts of her life. And at the close of the address the theme of "sin" arises — as is not unusual in a funeral address, and often almost formulaic:

> *Whoever among us is without sin, let him take up the nearest stone and cast it at her. [...] but our own heart, conscious of its shame before God, thus implores and beseeches him, the Lord of Mercy, most fervently, to be infinitely gracious to the late departed Princess Mother in His judgment for the sake of Jesus the Savior, [...] to grant her imperishable peace and let His divine countenance shine forever upon her transfigured spirit, should this not already have come to pass. Amen.*

Indeed, it constitutes the value and the merit of such an address that, even without further specifics, the listener is stirred to reflection — on the deceased, and also on himself. To offer comfort to the bereaved is likewise a meaningful purpose of such a sermon; the matter then concerns less the person of the deceased than the living — as is the case here. All the more so, presumably, when the most exalted among the bereaved

[p. 202]

is at the same time the sovereign, the ruler, or indeed the foremost person of the region — and especially so for the Church. One can even imagine that August had asked the preacher not to touch upon the whole matter of the separation and the abandonment of the children.

[p. 203]

## 21 Information on the Male Descendants

### 21.1 The Son and Successor August II

The firstborn (born 1784 in Breslau and died 1853 in Slaventzitz) studied in Erlangen from November 1802 to April 1805. Von Stetten, the father's former aide-de-camp, accompanied him there, having previously secured generous accommodation befitting his rank (three rooms, an alcove; stabling for four horses, servants, "attendance," for 125 florins payable semi-annually). The costs of the period of study were covered by his grandmother Princess Sacken, with an allowance of 3,000 thalers annually. The military sciences were not offered as a subject of study in Erlangen, and to his father's regret, August was not much interested in that in any case.[^147] It must have been the legal sciences, for in a letter from FL to August, exactly one professor in Erlangen is named, Karl August Gründler, whose field of teaching (with an emphasis on Prussian law) was jurisprudence. He (born 1770) had been called from Halle to Erlangen in 1797. In a commemorative volume of the university from the mid-nineteenth century, all the professors are listed,[^148] and there is also information about the students, along with their year of matriculation and subject of study;[^149] not, unfortunately, about all of them, for prefixed to the general register are the names of a few princes, that is, sons from especially high-ranking houses, and these are given without indication of their subject of study (is this perhaps an early form of data protection?). Alongside August one finds there, with exactly the same date of matriculation, Eugen Prince of Württemberg (which can hardly have been a coincidence). He (born 1788) was somewhat younger than August, and had interrupted his early-begun career in the Prussian and Russian military for a period of study (afterward

[p. 204]

continuing it successfully). This Eugen of Württemberg was, like August, a lover of music, and even composed several operas himself. One may assume that both of them heard music at the house of Margravine Caroline, who resided in Erlangen — Erlangen belonged to Ansbach-Bayreuth. August reported this himself; he valued music his whole life long, having learned to play the piano (and later maintaining court orchestras of his own).

After the conclusion of his studies, an educational tour through European capitals was planned, as was customary for the nobility; once again his grandmother financed it, this time with the father contributing as well, together 20,000 thalers. The journey was to go to France, Belgium, and Holland, then on to Vienna. After visiting Munich, Stuttgart, Karlsruhe, Strasbourg, Paris, Mainz, Frankfurt, and Kassel, however, he had to break it off for reasons of state policy.

He met his father in Erfurt, who had his headquarters there, in order to be present, in agreement with him, in Öhringen. By 1805 almost the entire southwest of the Empire was occupied by the French, and upheavals were to be feared. The father had a great deal to do with the political-military unrest in Prussia and Saxony. As the situation grew ever more difficult and opaque in 1805/06, and the future of Hohenlohe was also uncertain, with mediatization impending, FL had quickly decided to have his son declared of age and to make him his successor as Prince of Hohenlohe-Öhringen. August himself had also seen that he would have a more important task in Württemberg than in Prussia. He had himself released from the Prussian military in Berlin by Frederick William III and entered the service of the King of Württemberg. This took place still in the summer of 1806. Frederick William III had, when August returned from "his journey" (as the King put it in a letter to FL — and he certainly meant the abbreviated grand tour), issued him a "patent" as an officer dated 1 October 1802.[^150] August had (or would have) accordingly completed his entire course of study as a member of the Prussian army.

[p. 205]

*[illustration: portrait of August II in württembergischer uniform, captioned "August II as Württemberg Lieutenant-General. (Artist and year unknown)"]*

*[illustration: photograph of the obelisk in the Öhringen court garden, captioned "The Obelisk in the Öhringen Court Garden"]*

[p. 206]

In 1811 August married Louise of Württemberg, the younger sister of his Erlangen fellow student Eugen and niece of Duke Frederick, who had by now become King of Württemberg. The couple had four children: Friedrich (born 1812), Friederike Mathilde (1814), Friedrich Wilhelm Eugen Hugo (1816), and Felix (1818). Three of the children were born in Öhringen, one in Stuttgart.

FL kept close contact with him as the eldest. And later August, now the Prince, often visited his father, who had grown old and was probably very lonely, in far-distant Slaventzitz. It was probably on one such visit that the idea arose of erecting a monument in the court garden at Öhringen, for whose creation the citizens had given up their gardens — possibly in remembrance of the (far more elaborate) monument that FL had had erected in Ingelfingen for his parents' golden wedding anniversary (see p. 15). It became a classicist obelisk. Amid laurel wreaths, the years 1807 (FL's captivity in the castle) and 1815 are to be recognized. A plaque reads:

> *Obelisk 1815. The princely House of Hohenlohe acquired the garden of the Brückenmühle on 19 July 1807. Citizens of Öhringen redesigned it as a part of the park, "Citizens' Joy," for Prince Friedrich Ludwig zu Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, then a prisoner of war in his own castle at Öhringen. In gratitude, in 1815 the Prince's son, Prince August II zu Hohenlohe-Öhringen, had the obelisk erected "To the good citizens of the town of Öhringen." — On the occasion of the "750 Years of the Town of Öhringen" jubilee, the town restored the monument in 2003 with the kind support of the firm Bürkert, based in Ingelfingen and Öhringen.*

For his mother, August was probably the most important correspondent after her departure from Breslau. In a letter to him, she emphasized, among other things, her joy at her son's good relations with the House of

[p. 207]

Württemberg. This need not have been the case at first, when the King made good on the restrictions on the status of princes. It must have turned into a businesslike relationship, for otherwise he could hardly have exercised important political functions there:

After the establishment of the Kingdom of Württemberg, August was a member of the Württemberg Estates Assembly (Ständeversammlung) from 1815 to 1819, serving as its President from 1815 to 1816. After a new constitution was finally accepted in 1819, he was a member of the Chamber of the Lords of the Estates (Kammer der Standesherren) from 1820 to 1849 (the year of his resignation as Prince of Hohenlohe-Öhringen), again serving as President from 1820 to 1835. From 1838 to 1842 he had himself represented there by his son Friedrich, and thereafter by his son (Friedrich Wilhelm Karl) Hugo. In the early years of this political activity he must also have had contact with Christian von Massenbach, who likewise belonged to the Estates Assembly, though only briefly.

With regard to finances, August had not taken on an easy inheritance in Öhringen. When his father took over Öhringen, other Hohenlohe princes had for their part also lodged their own claims to possessions. FL already had a great deal to deal with on this account as early as 1805. His mountain of debt was immense, and there had been malicious tongues because of it. Somehow, however, August managed to cope with it. His inheritance from the fortune of his grandmother Sacken was, after all, considerable. Added to this was possibly also the circumstance that he had married "well." Nevertheless, after Hohenlohe was taken over by the Kingdom of Württemberg, Öhringen came "under the administration" of the Württemberg Kameralamt ("Ministry of Finance").

As noted, a substantial contribution to August's finances had been made, through the Transact of 1799 (see p. 77f.) and also through her testament, by his far-sighted grandmother, Princess Sacken, who died in 1811. She had, early on, as the expected successor of FL, favored him with a far higher inheritance than his siblings, for the "flourishing of the House of Hohenlohe"! In any case,

[p. 208]

he was then still able to help his father, and also to give room to his own passion, music: from 1816 he employed a twelve-member court orchestra in Öhringen, which, after the removal of the court household to Silesia, he increased there to sixteen musicians and maintained until 1850. With him, then, Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen did after all still have a son of the Muses — even if as Hohenlohe-Öhringen!

Upon his father's death, August quickly contributed 4,000 thalers to settle a remaining debt on the estate of Bytkow. Even in old age he remained interested in Ingelfingen and ordered one son to reside there in the castle. That son and his wife did so, and had two children there, though only for a few years, after which they returned to live in Stuttgart, as the King of Württemberg expected of "his" nobility for at least three months per year.

After his resignation and definitive move to Slaventzitz in Upper Silesia, August lived there until his death in 1853 and was also buried there. His successor in Öhringen was his son Friedrich Wilhelm Eugen (Hugo), who was also appointed Prince of Ujest (Upper Silesia). It was only August's grandson, Prince Christian Kraft of Hohenlohe-Öhringen, who consolidated the estate of Bytkow together with the neighboring localities and there operated coal and ore mining (particularly for zinc) with the very greatest success, under the place and company name "Hohenlohehütte" (see p. 127).

### 21.2 The Sons Adolph and Alexander

Prince Adolph, August's brother thirteen years his junior, retained his name Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, made a career in the Prussian military, and was briefly Prussian Minister-President in 1862 (his removal came at the hands of Bismarck). He had helped his mother when she once found herself in difficulties with a mortgage (see p. 157). Prince Adolph resided at the Koschentin estate acquired by FL in 1804, which remained in the possession of his descendants for several more generations (the expropriation took place after the Second World War). In 1829 the castle had been renovated in

[p. 209]

classicist style; the entire estate comprised, in 1860, five manors and 19 villages along with extensive forest lands.

The youngest brother, Prince Alexander, the only one who never married, died on 23 March 1829 at the age of 30 in the "princely palace" in Stuttgart, as a Knight of the Order of St. John. He had completed a military training in Stuttgart but was able to leave the royal Württemberg service and had come to Berlin, where, as he informed FL, Frederick William III "had arranged an appointment" for him. Alexander would now, from there, also come to know his mother.

A Hohenlohe legend has grown up around him.[^151] It is said that, having returned from Prussian service to Öhringen, he shot at subjects without cause (they would, strictly speaking, have been "subjects" of his brother August, or of the King of Württemberg). Alexander died in Stuttgart. After his elaborate burial in the woods by Friedrichsruhe Castle, he is said to have wandered about as a ghost. His grave there was desecrated in 1830 by two grave robbers searching for gold jewelry. The robbers were caught. On 24 August 1830 the solemn transfer of the body to Ingelfingen took place; there the coffin was carried into the well-attended church at the midnight hour; the final interment then took place there in the princely crypt.

*[illustration: photograph of Schloss Koschentin, a classicist-style manor house]*

Schloss Koschentin

[p. 210]

## 22 Excursus VI: Christian August Ludwig von Massenbach

Preliminary note: Christian von Massenbach has often been named in the main part of this book as a person closely connected to FL in his military career. Even after the defeat at Jena and Auerstedt in 1806, he remained in contact with him as an author. Unlike FL, however, he was an intellectual and a rebellious spirit. This makes him particularly interesting, as a kind of contrasting figure, so to speak.

### 22.1 Origin and Career

The von Massenbach family was an Imperial-immediate (reichsunmittelbar) knightly lineage. Since the beginning of the 16th century there had been a Prussian branch and a South German branch; both branches remained in constant contact and "helped each other out" whenever one of the two threatened to die out for lack of an heir.

Christian was the middle of three sons of the South German branch. All three served either in the military or in courtly administration; Christian in Prussia, the other two in Württemberg. The elder, Friedrich Reinhard Wilhelm, belonged to the knightly council of the Kraichgau knightly canton and, from 1809, was Württemberg Chamberlain and head of the War College; the younger, Dorotheus Friedrich Eberhard Ferdinand, was first Privy War Councillor in Berlin, then, from 1811, Württemberg State Councillor, and from 1817 Director of the Württemberg Superior Chamber of Accounts.

The family's ancestral seat, with its castle, was in Württemberg, in the village of Massenbach near Heilbronn. (Ingelfingen is about 50 km from there, Öhringen about 35 km — see the map on p. 14; in a sense, FL and Christian von Massenbach can thus be regarded as fellow countrymen. However, it was initially only the Hohenlohes who had received the princely title; the eldest Massenbach brother

[p. 211]

received his only after 1806.) The estate at Massenbach was administered as a fideicommissum. Today it has become the Baron von Massenbach Forest Foundation.

Christian came as a five-year-old child from Schmalkalden, where his father held the post of Chief Forester, to his mother at Massenbach. He received his first schooling in Ludwigsburg. Later he attended the Karlsschule (Charles's Academy) in Stuttgart, the country's foremost educational institution for the nobility and persons of the court. There he met Friedrich Schiller, one year his junior, who was a pupil, and later a student of medicine, there from 1773 to 1780. He kept in touch with him, and the two occasionally exchanged letters. Massenbach finished school in 1778. This education gave him a thorough knowledge of antiquity, of mathematics, but also of military science, geography, and history; he was able to put these subjects to excellent use in his wide-ranging activity as both officer and writer.

He began his military career in 1778 with the Württemberg Guard. When the Duke of Württemberg decided to appoint a further professor to the military faculty of the Karlsschule, he chose Massenbach, who from February 1782 taught there as professor of mathematics, tactics, and strategy. Massenbach, however, wanted something else. He sent Frederick II a translation of a French work "On the Trajectory of the Ball [...]" (the work dealt with the flight path of a fired projectile). The King thanked him for it and offered Massenbach the chance to come to Potsdam. Massenbach then transferred to the army of the Prussian King Frederick II (just as FL had also done). This transfer took place against the Duke's will. In 1783 he served on the Prussian General Quartermaster's Staff, took part in the 1787 campaign against Holland, and suffered a severe wound to the hand. In 1791 he was a major and aide-de-camp to the Prussian King. In addition, he was active as a teacher at the Military Academy, as editor of the Military Monthly Journal, and as a contributor to

[p. 212]

Nicolai's General German Library. From 1788 he was married.

*[illustration: portrait of Christian von Massenbach]*

Christian von Massenbach, born 1758 in Schmalkalden, died 1827 in Bialokosch (west of Posen). (Painter and year unknown)

### 22.2 Massenbach as FL's Quartermaster-General and the Events of 1806

Von Massenbach had already been near FL during the Rhine campaigns of 1792–94, and then, at the latest since 1805, he served directly on his staff as Colonel ("Obrist") and Quartermaster-General. He was thus FL's most important military collaborator and, in addition to the aides-de-camp, also a constant conversational partner. The correspondence, too, is extensive.[^152] And this was probably often quite strenuous, since they were very different personalities: one can hardly imagine how they endured the years 1805/06 together. Massenbach, the historically and scientifically educated intellectual, much given to talking and writing;

[p. 213]

FL the dynamic practitioner in all military-strategic matters, who always kept his soldiers in mind as well, and who was often not in agreement with the overall Prussian strategy determined by the Duke of Brunswick. Yet King Frederick William II [sic] followed the latter's plans almost invariably, and FL obeyed the King unconditionally.

Concretely, from 1805 onward the matter at hand was the impending struggle against Napoleon's army, culminating in the defeat at Jena and Auerstädt in October 1806 and the march of the remaining army by way of Magdeburg toward a hoped-for place of safety beyond the Oder. On 28 October 1806 came the capitulation of Prenzlau, which had been negotiated by FL with a French general. How this came about was for some time a matter of debate (see also Kahlert's letters to FL in chapter 16). Had Massenbach inadequately, or even wrongly, informed FL about the strength of the French troops? FL's own troops, moreover, were in poor condition. A period of rest at Magdeburg had not been possible, supply was inadequate, and discipline could be maintained only with difficulty. Fischer records an account of an episode, a conversation between FL and Massenbach, on 25 October 1806, that is, three days before the capitulation at Prenzlau:

> *[Massenbach] spoke even of the perniciousness of the alliance with Russia, of the necessity of one with France instead, and said that the moment such an alliance were concluded, he would leave Prussian service in order to enter French service. The Prince told him that if he were to carry this out, he would have to have him arrested at once — but laughed, remarking that with Massenbach the deed was never so bad as the words.[^153]*

It must be noted here that Massenbach was not alone in the Francophile sentiment expressed above. Already between 1800 and 1804 there had been voices favoring an alliance of Prussia

[p. 214]

with Napoleon's France against Russia.[^154] Official policy, however, decided otherwise.

Massenbach has, at least for 1806, a poor reputation among many contemporaries and near-contemporary writers. As an intellectual, he reflected far more on the overall political situation of Europe than would ever be known of FL. FL remained wholly bound by his obligation to his liege lord, the Prussian King, and it was thus his task to protect the Empire from the enemy, France. Whatever progressive impulse for the future of Europe emanated from revolutionary France (not from Napoleon's wars) does not appear to have engaged him. In the longer text already mentioned, which he wrote in 1805 on the political situation in Europe to the publicist Böttiger, FL muses almost entirely on questions of power and military alliances.[^155] Massenbach, after the end of the military hostilities, was to conduct himself as one committed to so-called progress — and would then have to pay for it.

### 22.3 Massenbach as a Political Author: Writings I

His personal range of knowledge was very broad: antiquity, mathematics, cartography, military affairs; and this is also evident in his activity as a writer. Between 1782 and 1802 fifteen articles and books had already appeared. After that, in rapid succession, came:

- Marc Aurel und Sully, 1806.
- Lichtstrahlen. Beiträge zur Geschichte der Jahre 1805, 1806, 1807. Eine Zeitschrift in freien Heften von einer Gesellschaft wahrheitsliebender Militärpersonen, Zivilbeamten, Gelehrter. Heft 1-5. Hamburg und Leipzig, 1807/08.
- Rückerinnerungen an große Männer, 1808.
- Vollständiger Bericht von den Operationen der Königlich Preußischen Armee, welche im Feldzug von 1806 unter den Befehlen des Fürsten von Hohenlohe. Hamburg und Leipzig: Historisch-Politisch-Militärisches Institut, 1808.

[p. 215]

- Memoiren zur Geschichte des preußischen Staates unter der Regierung Friedrich Wilhelm II. und Friedrich Wilhelm III. 3 Bände, 1809-10.
- Historische Denkwürdigkeiten zur Geschichte des Verfalls des preußischen Staates seit 1794 nebst meinem Tagebuch über den Feldzug 1806 in 2 Teilen. Mit Situationskarten und Plänen, 1809.
- Memoiren über meine Verhältnisse zum preußischen Staat und insbesondere zum Herzog von Braunschweig. 2 Bände, 1809.

The fourth volume of the "Memoiren zur Geschichte des Preußischen Staates," which dealt critically with the events of 1806 and was directed against the conduct of the Prussian army, was confiscated and destroyed in 1810. — The publications listed here are only the most important books. The catalogue reproduced in Engels's afterword lists 35 titles, an extract of which is presented here and further below.[^156]

In several of these publications Massenbach himself gave his own account of the events concerning which the King had requested reports from him (the Battle of Jena and Auerstädt and the capitulation of Prenzlau). He even sent manuscripts in several installments from Bialokosch to FL in Öhringen and asked him by letter for his permission to have them printed; he had a need to justify himself.

At least for today's reader of Massenbach's many letters to FL, it is astonishing with what respect they were written. The salutation always indicates the difference in rank ("Most Serene Prince, most gracious Prince and Lord"), and the letter almost always closes "in deep devotion to Your Most Serene Highness," "most submissive servant" — after so much shared experience! Is all this merely the period's customary formulary language of a still-persisting society of estates? There must have been a linguistic and address code within the Prussian state that was maintained even in retirement.

How Massenbach appeared to Goethe, shortly before the engagements at Jena and Auerstädt and the capitulation, Goethe

[p. 216]

himself described unambiguously (see p. 112f.). Goethe was able to dissuade him from publishing a text and had recognized that Massenbach's attitude toward the French Revolution and toward Napoleon was more emotional than politically realistic — at least, that was true at this point in time.

Massenbach, as shown above, wrote a great deal after Prussia's military defeat (and presumably gladly, but also for reasons of earning money). The fact that Volume Four of the memoirs was not permitted to appear, or was destroyed, has brought him a certain recognition down to the most recent times. Jan V. Röhnert dedicated an article to him in the *FAZ* titled "The Thwarted German Chateaubriand,"[^157] in which he writes of an "autobiographical grand project" that unfortunately is not available to us in published form (though it is preserved in an archive). In content it presumably contains little beyond what he reported to FL in his many letters.

Besides his role as a writer, Massenbach also played a further role as a politician after the Wars of Liberation. This will be addressed below, since it further illustrates the contrast with FL. After his resignation in 1806 and the Prussian King's rejecting letter of March 1813 — namely, that he would be given "no further appointment" (see p. 120) — FL stood aside as a public figure, occupied with his mountain of debt.

The circumstances of the lost battle and the capitulation were investigated by a commission appointed by the King under Prince Henry. In the process, FL was exonerated, while errors, including a piece of misinformation, were proven against Massenbach. FL had, however, in a letter to the King, assumed all responsibility. He had, after all, been the commander-in-chief shortly after the death of the Duke of Brunswick on the battlefield. A shadow always remained attached to FL after this, particularly in the estimation of the Prussian King.

The author Fischer described the relationship of FL to Massenbach as the latter's "darkest point":

[p. 217]

*[illustration: facsimile reproduction of a handwritten letter by Massenbach]*

Example of a letter from Massenbach. The salutation and signature deserve particular notice here.[^158]

[p. 218]

> *Men who, while holding strictly to truthfulness, were well disposed toward the Prince describe him [Massenbach] as a person who knew how to impress through the airs of profound thought and through bold assertion even more than through genuine learned knowledge — as an overwrought, eccentric enthusiast who had dabbled in various branches of learning and whose outward bearing truly possessed something capable of deceiving. He had acquired a kind of intellectual mastery over the Prince and had made himself indispensable to him. [...] Toward this man, who must be called his evil genius, Prince Friedrich Ludwig was weak and blind.[^159]*

This negative assessment surely also rests on statements by the previously mentioned Marwitz.

*[...] quite unbelievable [...] how this man [Massenbach] could attain such a reputation and so completely dominate Prince Hohenlohe [...]. When Massenbach spoke of operations in learnedly confused terms, it happened that the prince — whether because these expressions, and thereby the matters themselves, were foreign to him, or because his advancing age and Bonaparte's uninterrupted good fortune in war robbed him of all the confidence he otherwise still liked to display in public — would usually listen to him in silence, sometimes agree, but even more often would, on the following day, order these same things or speak of them as though they were his own ideas. In doing so he would each time assert, with incomprehensible self-deception, that Massenbach had no influence over him, and would very correctly explain why he [Massenbach] could have none, since he was far too fickle in his principles and mistaken in his political opinions.*[^160]

Württemberg had become a kingdom in 1806. A constitution was to be drawn up. Massenbach's family was entitled to a seat in

[p. 219]

the Württemberg Estates Assembly. As representative of his elder brother Richard, he was a member there from 1816 to 1817. He did not hold back on this occasion, delivering speeches against the proposed draft constitution, demanding a unified Germany, a parliamentary form of government, the accountability of ministers to parliament, the abolition of the two-chamber system (Estates Assembly and Diet), and the immunity of deputies (incidentally: Prince August II of Hohenlohe-Öhringen, FL's son, was even President of this body; the two must have encountered one another often). From one of Massenbach's fiery speeches:

> *Nothing great, nothing splendid, nothing enduring through the centuries can be achieved without the realization of German national representation! Without it the whole of European mankind will not rise to lawful freedom. Like children we Germans are regarded, like schoolboys we shall henceforth be treated. Without national representation we Germans will wage wars, as we have always waged them, to enlarge and bring happiness to other nations; we shall remain small and unhappy, ever smaller and more unhappy. – Do we want that? Do we still not feel called upon to give ourselves a great, solid constitution, cast in a single mould? Not called upon to awaken again in ourselves the feelings that glowed in all Germans after the battles at Büren, Liegnitz, Dennewitz, at Leipzig? We must rekindle the glow of that warming substance; or we shall perish in the cold! I wish with my whole soul that the princes be most intimately bound to the peoples, and the peoples to the princes. I am a friend of the people only insofar as I am a faithful admirer of good princes. People and prince are one, one is the state.*[^161]

[p. 220]

And here too he made certain that his contemporaries, and we ourselves, could read it back in published form.

### 22.4 Massenbach's Writings II and His Expulsion from Württemberg

- Obrist Massenbach an alle teutsche Männer. Heidelberg: Mohr und Winter, 1817.
- Obrist Massenbach an alle teutsche Männer. Zweytes Schreiben. Heidelberg: Mohr und Winter, 1817.
- Obrist Massenbach über Fürsten-Erziehung in repräsentativen Verfassungen. Eine Rede, welche zunächst zum Vortrag in der Ständeversammlung Württembergs bestimmt war. Heidelberg: Mohr und Winter, 1817.
- Der Obrist Massenbach, den Thronen, Pallästen und Hütten Teutschlands. Was ist ein konstitutionelles oder gesetzmäßiges Fürsten- oder Königthum? Heft 1-3. Teutschland. Heidelberg: Mohr und Winter 1817.
- Freiherr Christian von Massenbach. Zwei Vota, abgelegt in der Ständeversammlung Württembergs am 2. und 4. Juni 1817 nebst der darauf erfolgten ständischen Adresse an S.M. den König von eben diesem Tage. Auszug aus dem 38. Heft der Verhandlungen der Württembergischen Ständeversammlung 1817. Heidelberg: Mohr und Winter, 1817.
- Des Obersten Massenbach Rede auf der Ständeversammlung Württembergs am Tage ihrer gewaltsamen Auflösung am 5. Juny 1817.

But Massenbach too had a great problem well known to us from FL's case — money. To stabilize his ruined finances, however, he proceeded quite differently from FL: he threatened to have his hitherto unpublished memoirs printed. For handing over the manuscript he demanded a total of 57,500 reichsthalers — taking into account also his unreceived pension. As

[p. 221]

a supplement, he also sent a table of contents to Berlin.[^162] This was perceived as extortion.

Massenbach had, with his conduct and his publications concerning the Prussian state, overstepped its limits of tolerance. The consequence was that Prussia instituted proceedings against him, which however dragged on for years. FL too was asked for a statement; he was still in contact with him in 1815. In his response he evaded the matter somewhat, naming von Bülow (not Blücher) and Grawert as eyewitnesses. He had, after all, immediately "after Prenzlau" taken the entire responsibility upon himself, and had had only an indirect connection with the writings.

Massenbach was expelled from Württemberg in 1817 on account of his political activities.[^163] He first stayed in Heidelberg, where one of his sons was to study and where, with the publisher Mohr und Winter, a large part of his most recent speeches appeared (see above). The permission to reside there, granted by the city police, was withdrawn after a few days on higher orders. Since his inquiry as to the reasons for this went unanswered, he turned to Frankfurt, where he lodged his complaint concerning the proceedings with the Federal Assembly. In the summer of 1817 the Prussian authorities set every lever in motion to have him arrested for high treason. When the Frankfurt Senate finally consented, he was arrested under dramatic circumstances during the night of 18 to 19 August 1817 and taken to the fortress of Küstrin.

It may be assumed that FL took note of this development in Massenbach's affairs. He is likely to have reacted with considerable incomprehension. It is regrettable that it remains unknown whether he spoke of it at least with his son August — who must have witnessed Massenbach in the Estates Assembly — during one of the latter's visits to Slaventzitz.

Only in 1819, that is, after FL's death, did the court martial pronounce its sentence of fourteen years' confinement in a fortress (grounds: high treason, insulting the King, threatened insubordination,

[p. 222]

failure to surrender his service papers). In 1826 he was pardoned. In his cell he had written continually. Nothing of it, however, ever appeared in print.

He died in 1827 on his estate of Bialokosch, which had meanwhile belonged to the newly created Duchy of Warsaw, subject to the King of Saxony, and had come back to Prussia in 1815. The estate remained with his descendants (it is, or was until recently, used — after renovation and extension — as a children's home).

### 22.5 Massenbach in the Judgment of Historians

Massenbach remained a subject for historians and also writers (Fontane, Arno Schmidt) into the 20th century. In his afterword to Massenbach's book "Historical Memorabilia" (*Historische Denkwürdigkeiten*), Hans-Werner Engels gives an overview of some ten publications by the guild of historians (from Treitschke through Häusser to Poten, Knesebeck, Tomuschat, Mehring, and Klessmann) and shows how strongly their attitude or judgment was influenced by the intellectual currents of their time, for example by the by-then long-standing "hatred of the French".[^164] Treitschke: "Prince Hohenlohe, ill-advised by the vain fine talker M." Massenbach belonged to the "swarm of insolent slanderers". Schreckenbach: "a crank of the first order". Mehring: "[...] endowed with a happy talent for bluster". Fiedler: "[...] there was a veritable clique of the half-educated who, like Massenbach [...] dispensed teachings that were highly confused and permeated by a bombastic manner of speaking, writing, and thinking, and could only repel people of plain understanding". Massenbach is said to have gained the upper hand over the prince:

> *This bombastically holding-forth brooder over the science of war occupied himself, even during the retreat, more with the fate of Europe than with the dire situation of the army. Unremittingly he pressed his commander-in-chief to see to it

[p. 223]

> that the alliance with Russia should not come about, for salvation now lay solely in an alliance with France.*

Some of these works also address Massenbach's role in the Württemberg Estates Assembly.

### 22.6 Arno Schmidt's "Historical Revue"

It was above all Massenbach's clear avowal, in his speeches and writings, of the ideals of the French Revolution and of a parliamentary system, that moved the writer Arno Schmidt (1914–1979) in 1949 to occupy himself with him.[^165] He saw in him a kindred spirit, an independent thinker, an early European. He wrote a "Historical Revue" entitled "Massenbach," in which the sequence of years since 1792 is presented as a stage drama, essentially as dialogues among the decisive figures of Prussia during Massenbach's lifetime. Almost all of them appear: the King, the Duke of Brunswick, Marwitz, Zastrow, and also our Prince Friedrich Ludwig; there is also a good deal of gossip; Fouqué, Arno Schmidt's favorite, could not be missing. Schmidt's assessment ran as follows:

> *He was a man who even then strove for the unity of Europe and wished to create safeguards against Russia. One of his prophecies was that in a hundred years the Russians would stand before Strasbourg, and that only America, alongside them, would be a serious factor of power. [...] this, in such consistency the first European, became to me so strangely and fraternally familiar that I confess — I wish to try to renew his memory among others as well.*

The whole piece idealizes Christian von Massenbach, whom we have also come to know as a very difficult, or at least controversial, character, but it is well worth reading nonetheless.

[p. 224]

The great differences between the two personalities, FL and Massenbach, cannot be overlooked. One might ask why FL, of higher rank, did not simply part ways with Massenbach. Perhaps their shared origin played a certain role for both of them: small dominions in southern Germany, from which they had set out into the world (at first, admittedly, represented by the figure of Frederick II); perhaps they both spoke with a South German dialect or accent (one small indication of this might be that FL wrote, in letters, of "Scheitnich" — or dictated it so; the place near Breslau is called Scheitnig — although "ch" at the end also occurs quite occasionally in printed form. FL always signed his letters to the children with "Vatter," that is, with two "t"s, as suits the dialect — the children, for their part, addressed him as "Vater." The expression "Hüttensach" for mining activities belongs here as well). Both were, as "Franconians," somehow outsiders — there were parallels here, and these may have bound them together.

[p. 225]

## 23 Who Was Friedrich Ludwig, Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen?

### 23.1 Friedrich Ludwig and the Kings of Prussia, Frederick William II and III

FL, although born into a very small Hohenlohe dominion, had already risen as Hereditary Prince, in his military capacity within Prussian state service, to become one of the leading generals. The military, and its officers in particular, was bound to the King, who was the absolute ruler and holder of supreme command. His decisions were binding. From his soldiers and his commanders he expected unconditional obedience. FL never called these arrangements into question. The King — beginning with Frederick II, then Frederick William II, and, from 1797, Frederick William III — always remained for him the supreme authority; loyalty, and obedience too, was an absolute requirement.

On matters of high (foreign) policy he rarely voiced his own thoughts; his views of society were marked by skepticism.

The duties of a senior officer in times without armed conflict can include diplomatic missions. FL undertook such missions after the conclusion of the Peace of Basel — see p. 50f. He was, moreover, responsible for the internal constitution of the troops — training and the recruitment of prospective officers, for example. On these matters there survive a few letters to the King, though most of the correspondence will have gone to the Prussian military archive. They concerned individual officers whose reassignment was under discussion, who had been proposed for promotion, and similar matters. They were often answered by von Zastrow, who served on the King's immediate staff; on a few occasions the King himself had co-signed them. He then used

[p. 226]

the address "cousin." On this point: the King was born in 1744, FL in 1746, so they were roughly of an age. The address did not refer to any kinship; the Houses of Hohenzollern and Hohenlohe had nothing to do with one another in that respect. The reason was probably their shared membership in the same generation within the high nobility.

When Frederick William II died, FL mourned his death, but soon noted also that the young successor, Frederick William III (24 years his junior), treated him well. FL would remain in close contact with him until his own death. The relationship was seriously disturbed only by FL's capitulation at Prenzlau, which the King could neither understand nor accept, and probably never forgave him for (see the remark to von Marwitz, p. 108). For several years afterward there was no personal exchange between them — FL was, after all, without military office at that time.

Immediately upon assuming his own reign in 1797, Frederick William III began systematic tours ("reviews") through the individual provinces of Prussia, and in the course of them naturally visited Breslau several times, where FL held his governorship. The festive pageantry staged by FL at Alt-Scheitnig is described in the corresponding section (see p. 70f.). In the few years up to 1806 (the Battle of Jena and Auerstedt), FL was at court several times, both in Potsdam and in Charlottenburg. At both palace complexes he was lodged in historically significant quarters, notably at Sanssouci, the "home" of his idol Frederick II. At the palace in Jena, it was not the King but the local ruler, Duke Charles Augustus, who likewise wished to give him preferential lodging. Although Privy Councillor Voigt in Weimar pointed out that Goethe had only just been given rooms there — Goethe had to give way. FL reported such marks of high regard in his letters; he relished them, as he did the King's visits to him in Breslau.

When Frederick William III continued his father's "settlement policy" in the part of Poland now called South Prussia,

[p. 227]

FL was on hand. But the King's letters in reply also touched on personal matters:

In what was probably the first letter (of 24 February 1798, from Potsdam), the King addressed FL as "uncle" — a mark of respect given the difference in age. There followed an offer from the King to send the Surgeon-General Goerke from Berlin to Breslau, to assist there with a medical case.

The King told FL several times when he had had dealings with members of his family or relations. Thus he wrote of the — dutiful — request of FL's brother, Prince George (a Prussian officer), for permission to marry a young noblewoman named Klüchzner. The King had at first had reservations on account of the financial situation of both parties. But when he received a second letter from the Prince, in which the latter emphasized his earnestness, he communicated his favorable decision to FL as well. The child of this marriage was the aforementioned nephew Eduard.

In 1801 began the regular exchange of New Year's greetings with the King, now using the address "cousin." From January 1806 came the pause already mentioned, lasting until 1810 (probably on account of the capitulation of Prenzlau). Thereafter, however, he would "at all times take great interest in your petitions" — and such petitions there were! He congratulated him on the marriage of FL's daughter Emilie, and in 1816 thanked him for the announcement of Auguste's engagement. In 1813 he mentions a "favorable change in the political circumstances in Hohenlohe." Did he mean by this a loosening of the considerable restrictions the King had imposed on the nobility in the territories that had been newly added? FL had complained to him, for instance, about impending restrictions on the right of residence and inheritance of his two younger sons outside Württemberg.[^166]

In summary, one may certainly conclude that a personal relationship existed between FL and the Prussian kings of the time. It appears not to have been one-sided — although the King sometimes merely reacted to a piece of news, and will have done the same with many other figures

[p. 228]

besides. His repeated attentiveness to family matters concerning FL nonetheless appears endearing. "Hohenlohe" had become a household name in Berlin, and remained so for several generations more.

### 23.2 A Patron of the Fine Arts, of Music, Painting, and Poetry?

Nowadays, at any rate, there is the Hohenlohe Cultural Summer, which draws on the surviving princely houses and their buildings. In 2015 the Stuttgart State Museum was able to mount a major exhibition, "Art Treasures from Hohenlohe" — but, alas, Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, despite its flowering in the eighteenth century, did not appear in it at all.[^167] The other Hohenlohe princely families had themselves portrayed splendidly and with great symbolic weight; in the case of the Ingelfingen line, one must be content that portraits were made at all.

The practice of music likewise plays no role. The children presumably received some musical instruction; at any rate, in a letter to Berlin, where the children were staying at the time, FL gives his eldest son advice on how and where he should have the damaged pianoforte repaired. This son, his successor August II, had a genuine interest in the arts. FL, by contrast, understood the art of leading an army — in that, probably more than his likewise princely relatives.

Let us nevertheless attempt to assemble the little that has come down to us on musical and artistic matters. When the Ingelfingen palace was rebuilt at the beginning of the eighteenth century, FL had not yet been born; Italian artists executed the stuccowork. He was involved in the new furnishing at the end of the century. Landscape paintings adorn the walls, along with depictions with motifs drawn from antiquity. As artists he engaged the Court Painter Johann Jakob Schillinger of Öhringen and the somewhat younger Christian Wagner, who was married to a woman from Ingelfingen. He had also brought Schillinger to Breslau, or rather to Alt-Scheitnig, to paint the interior of the summer house there

[p. 229]

there. He himself probably had little to do with the music played at the summer festivals at Scheitnig (on one occasion the composer Dittersdorf was present). All of this was simply the custom of the time, which he followed.

With Christian Wagner he had a special connection: in a true act of patronage, he financed for him, then twenty-nine years old, a two-year stay in Italy in 1782 (the classic tour) and a year in Dresden in 1787! Quite something! "I shall bring Schillinger along" was a remark made on one occasion when something needed to be settled in Ingelfingen (this Schillinger, to be sure, was not the Court Painter but a master builder — from the same family, originally of Ingelfingen). The painters Wagner and J. J. Schillinger knew each other well; both had attended the Carlsschule in Stuttgart, and Wagner had worked alongside J. J. Schillinger in Öhringen. In Ingelfingen, as already reported, FL appointed him head of the drawing school.

And otherwise? Beyond his involvement with the smithy of the Court Blacksmith mistakenly called the "Mint," nothing further is known of commissions to the sculptor Philipp Christoph Sommer, who had even acquired a house in the Mariannenvorstadt, nor are there any works by Leonhard Kern, which were on view in the aforementioned Hohenlohe exhibition — these came from the other courts. What music was played in Breslau, or rather Alt-Scheitnig, when FL invited guests there for a house concert and a dance? We do not know, and must leave it at this: that he — unlike his son August, who as his successor in Öhringen and later also in Slaventzitz kept a court orchestra — was no votary of the Muses. He evidently did know, however, what befitted a person of such elevated standing as himself — in Breslau, for instance, in the design of Scheitnig Park and the rebuilding of the residence in the city. To his contemporaries, as to us, he was probably noticed chiefly as a soldier and, likewise, as an urban developer.

[p. 230]

### 23.3 Friedrich Ludwig's "Human" Side

Was FL a caring person, a good father (and husband)? What do historical records offer for such questions about the life of a person who lived more than 200 years ago? At a time when there were still binding rules for how people dealt with one another, above all rules that made distinctions of rank clear in correspondence. As Prince and military commander, FL stood very high in the social hierarchy.

Assessments of his "interpersonal" conduct in his professional world as a soldier are accessible on the basis of individual sources: it was, for instance, decidedly courageous of him to wrest the captured young Prussian officer away from his own soldiers, thereby saving his life. That von Zastrow was grateful to him for this, and later, in his own elevated position, helped him as a reward for this good deed, is without doubt a fine testimony to the conduct of both men. FL was also later very popular and respected among his soldiers — by then Prussian ones, sometimes Saxon ones as well — and also among his aides-de-camp; with many of them a personal relationship persisted even after their careers had ended. Whenever improper incidents occurred within his area of responsibility, he strove, even before the King, to whom he was obliged to report them, to secure fair treatment — as once from Frankfurt, where he pleaded for leniency (this letter to the King survives in Frankfurt as a copy. The presumably numerous military reports to the King probably passed, after the sorting of the estate, into the Prussian archive). When von Marwitz was assigned to him as aide-de-camp without FL's knowledge or involvement, FL, at his request, explained after a few days his conspicuous reserve on precisely these grounds ("I did not choose you"). What then developed was a relationship of mutual respect, as von Marwitz himself described in his book.[^168]

[p. 231]

Of the greatest significance for the assessment of his character is without doubt the event that, in the first general perception, was regarded as the absolute low point of his military career: the capitulation of Prenzlau on 28 October 1806. He had declined a battle that, by the assessment of the situation, was hopeless for his side, and had achieved, through negotiations with the opposing French general, that the lives of thousands of soldiers — his own and, mostly forgotten, also those of the enemy — were thereby saved. One must bear in mind, as the history books record, that more than 100,000 soldiers were deployed in these engagements, of whom only 12,000 survived! That he was a credible negotiator, one who could be trusted, was already known, for example from the talks around Frankfurt some ten years earlier. Commanders are generally not made war heroes by capitulations. Even in FL's case it took some time before the first contemporaries, and later historians, came to recognize his decision. The letter, too, that he wrote afterward as a prisoner to his son (see p. 93f.) bears witness to great human solicitude.

And now? What can one find about FL as a private person? What do we nowadays even expect in the way of information about a person's private sphere? What do we know about his contemporaries Schiller or Goethe as fathers, as husbands? Loving letters between the married couple from around 1792 do exist. But when, as with FL, a particular event in this sphere of life sets the course thereafter — the separation of Marianne from him and, at the same time, from their common children, two of whom were still very small — then (perhaps) particular traits of character are also revealed. This is without doubt the case with FL. He showed himself to be a caring, considerate father, interested in and attentive to his children's health and their whole lives. In the separation and divorce documents he very clearly took responsibility for the children upon himself. Marianne made no discernible effort in this regard; she knew that

[p. 232]

she would have had no chance (and she could count on the fact that her mother, the Princess Sacken, would do everything to preserve the "flourishing of the House of Hohenlohe"). The two former spouses had no further contact with one another; there must have been an agreement to that effect. When, in letters, mother or father happened to speak of the other, they used, cautiously, neutral terms — I have pointed this out. There was no war of the roses.

FL had, to the great fortune of the children and also of himself, in the first period of the separation, the support of his sister Sophie Christiane. He was able to go on attending to his professional duties without worry. She was a complete substitute mother for the little ones; Adolph and Alexander would have no memory of Marianne from their childhood.

The other person of the utmost importance in this situation was the tutor Kahlert. FL could accept without difficulty that the fourteen-year-old August, and very probably also the three girls who followed him, were initially with their mother and grandmother in Berlin together with Kahlert. Kahlert had his full trust. FL maintained a correspondence with August that continued even after August went to study in Erlangen. The three girls must, however, then have come to Öhringen fairly soon, since from 1806 FL was a prisoner there, and August had by then advanced to Prince there. What could FL do there for the girls, what were the interests of the nineteen-year-old Adelheid (Adele), the eighteen-year-old Emilie, the thirteen-year-old Auguste? There is no information on this. In two letters from FL it emerges that he intended to hold a ball for Adele. They did have a certain social circle (chiefly the Hohenlohe relations) and, certainly very important at the time, their elder brother. FL himself could do little for them. When in 1808/09 he had to go to Upper Silesia, they remained behind under their brother's "protection." And they were to marry. Around 1810 Emilie, in 1812 Adelheid, and in 1816 Auguste; Adelheid to a Hohenlohe-Kirchberg quite close by. FL's role as father was thus brief, and it began

[p. 233]

but continued in their correspondence. The girls also remained in occasional contact with Kahlert. He would report to FL whenever he had heard something of Auguste and of Adelheid (Adele), or had met them. Adolph and Alexander grew up in Öhringen and in Stuttgart; they too visited their father (and also their mother).

The exchange with August was especially intensive — and had to be. He was, after all, the successor. Of him FL could say, toward the end of his life, as his sister reported, that he (August) had "come to love him (FL) quite dearly after all" — FL had only decided on marriage at the age of 37, and after its end he had then seized the chance, even now, in his good relations with his children from the failed marriage and at the age of over 55, to become a good family man after all.

[p. 234]

Dr. Litz of the Ulm City Archive assisted in deciphering individual words in the manuscripts — even the two of us together could not decipher everything. ### 6. Transcribed list of measures initiated or commissioned (from Triebenfeld in a letter to FL of 7 March 1809, which he sent him from Kontopp):

> *Most Serene Prince, most gracious Prince and Lord*  
> *I have had here the Justice-Burgomaster Hawich of Zagorow, who is engaged as an actual Ducal Warsaw Notary [...], together with the Chief Inspector Heitzer, and with these men have negotiated everything that is to Your Serene Highness's benefit [...] and for this purpose issued the special power of attorney at law. I have accordingly*

- *first: pressed for the dismissal of [D.] regarding the Trabeziner estates and given power and instruction thereto*
- *second. Given power for the complete lifting of the sequestration and drafted the initiative, all the more so as a 3-year general [indult] has been granted by the King of Saxony, and since this depends on the absolute payment of the interest; so I have*
- *third: given P. Habich power to collect the interest, and to pay it to the creditors who already have legally valid [judgments] before them, against receipt, but to remit the remainder to Your Serene Highness. I have also*
- *fourth: given a power of attorney to sue the Jew Oppenheimer et Wolff in Kalisch, and to press at once for the cancellation of the several sums entered in excess of 72,000 Rth, concerning which a lawsuit is already pending in Berlin.*
- *fifth: I have issued a power of attorney that the swindler Henrichs, who must let himself be [...] as sequestrator, be dismissed forthwith and compelled to render account and [...], that Hawich in turn draw up the objections against him, and*

[p. 242]

> *that he be compelled to make good the same, as well as to pay the sums still outstanding from him. Likewise I have*

- *sixth: given a power of attorney to collect from v. Riednewiecki the outstanding lease payments and to seek execution against him.*
- *seventh: I have issued a power of attorney that all unauthorized negotiations and sales of land by Henrichs be annulled forthwith, and that the purchasers, unless they consent to payment at the treasury, be evicted with recourse against Henrichs, [...]. He has, without being authorized, given away portions of the land for nothing and has himself thereby appropriated a little estate out of our estates and erected the buildings from lordly timber and bricks.*
- *eighth: I have given power of attorney for the collection of all outstanding purchase moneys, which are very considerable.*
- *ninth: a power of attorney is issued to compel the [Oleniher] hereditary leaseholders to pay the inclusion of the [...] outstanding entrance moneys amounting to 23,000 Rth, or else be evicted along with their dependents, so that the land and buildings may be otherwise [...].*
- *tenth: a power of attorney given to P. Habich for the pursuit of all boundary lawsuits, and the necessary instructions given to him.*
- *eleventh: that Habich has been given a power of attorney so that he may appear in Your Serene Highness's name at the [...] diet at Warsaw and in all places, in order to secure His Highness's rights of possession and property in his holdings, all the more so as every estate owner must now appear entirely in person, reside upon his estates, or else forfeit his possessions.*
- *twelfth: I have besides this given P. Habich, on my part, a [new] power of attorney together with the original decrees of the French General Government, in order to collect the new*

[p. 243]

> *[interests] advanced by me at the Invalids' Fund, together with costs, etc., and to deliver the same [...] to Your Serene Highness toward covering the Lahr'sche sum.*
- *thirteenth: and finally Habich has been issued yet another power of attorney, in order to [...] collect from the [Kadrimetisch] heirs the still outstanding hereditary purchase moneys [...] amounting to over 10,000 Rth, and to refute the unjust [...] claims formerly and previously raised by the deceased*

*I have had all this taken down at law through the Justice-Burgomaster Scheuch [...], so as to begin this my career quite brilliantly; and since, by virtue of the obligation of 250,000 Rth, I have a right among all the creditors to have a say in the matter, so [...] this will surely command respect — and the desired outcome be brought about. In the meantime, Your Serene Highness shall, at my direction, receive faithfully from time to time reports on the progress of the affair. In deepest reverence I remain Your Most Serene Highness's most faithfully obedient servant [signature Triebenfeld]*

### 7. Excerpts from the Proceedings before the Royal Prussian Kammergericht, 1811

> *Pro fisco*  
> *Summum periculum in mora*  
> *The War and Forestry Councillor von Triebenfeld of Kontopp near Grüneberg in Silesia received on his own security, on 1 Feb. 1805, from the General Invalids' Fund here, a loan of 50,000 [...] at the coinage standard of 1704, in bonds convertible at the time without loss immediately into ready money [...], at 5% interest payable in half-yearly installments, and repayment of the loan upon a notice period of six months fixed for both parties. Concerning this loan, the same did, on 28 Jan. 1805, issue to the General Invalids' Fund a judicial debenture, and did therein, as security for this loan, pledge to the said Fund not only*

[p. 244]

> *his entire estate as collateral, but also, besides this, a bond. Namely one made out to him, von Triebenfeld, by the Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, dated Breslau, 1 June 1804, for the sum of 250,000 [...], and entered, pledged, and conditionally assigned upon the Serene Highness's Zagorower estates, granting the Fund, in respect of its loan, priority over the remaining 200,000 [...] still outstanding to him under the latter bond. Of the aforementioned loan of 50,000 [...], the said [...] Triebenfeld has remained in arrears of interest at 5% since 1 Nov. 1807 until the end of July 1811, that is, for 3 ¾ years, in the amount of 9,375 [...]. All means of obtaining payment of this arrears of interest from him have proved fruitless, and the General Invalids' Fund has therefore seen itself obliged to avail itself of the rights accruing to it [...] against von Triebenfeld as well as against the Zagorower estates pledged to it by His Serene Highness the Prince of Hohenlohe, arising from the above-mentioned instrument of debt and pledge, in respect of this loan together with the interest accruing thereon in arrears, and to this end it has judicially empowered the Royal Saxon and Duchy of Warsaw Tribunal Advocate [Mitschka] of Kalisch, upon transmission of the original documents concerning that loan, to bring suit in its name before the competent Duchy of Warsaw Tribunal, for the present against the possessor of the Zagorower estates, for the arrears of interest outstanding on the capital of the 50,000, and, should it be necessary, to seek the sequestration of these estates. Quite apart from the fact that, according to information received, the Zagorower estates are very heavily encumbered with debt, there arises here also the ill circumstance that, according to the letter of von Triebenfeld of the 5th of this month to the General Invalids' Fund, submitted with a request for its return, together with the copy of the resolution of the Kalisch Prefecture appended to this letter, the capital sums likewise lent by the General Invalids' Fund into the Duchy of Warsaw and secured by mortgage upon estates there*

[p. 245]

> *have been added, together with the interest in arrears, to the Bayorn sums. Under these circumstances, and since it appears from the mortgage certificate communicated to the General Invalids' Fund by the Glogau Superior Court by letter of 21 Sept. 1810, and likewise enclosed here sub petito retardationis, that the estate of Kontopp near Grüneberg in Silesia, belonging to von Triebenfeld, is encumbered with debt to almost the full amount of its purchase [...] in the year 1800, and further, as this letter of the Glogau [...] Superior Court shows, that the estate [Sch.], belonging to von Triebenfeld and situated in the district of the Breslau Superior Court, is likewise burdened with considerable debts, the General Invalids' Fund is, as regards its claim to the capital sum of 50,000 [...] and the interest thereon in arrears, which by now, as noted, up to the end of July of this year already amounts to 9,375 [...], entirely without security. Meanwhile, however, this Fund has learned that His Serene Highness the Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen has received, from the estate of the late Princess von der Osten-Sacken who died here, the sum of 80,000 [...] as a legacy, and that he will in turn pay out this sum to von Triebenfeld in satisfaction of the sum of money owed him; and I have been instructed in this matter, according to the enclosed original, on the basis of the order — enclosed in copy — of the Royal Privy State Councillor and Chief of the General Police Department in the Ministry of the Interior, dated the 28th of this month, contained in the letter of the 29th [...], to lay attachment forthwith, at the [Royal Prussian] Kammergericht, upon the aforesaid 80,000 [...], in coverage of the loan of the 50,000 [...] and of the arrears thereon amounting, from 1 Nov. 1807 to the end of July 1811, to 9,375 [...], together with the interest accruing thereafter. This attachment is also justified in every respect. For the grounding of an attachment in urgent and pressing cases, of which*

[p. 246]

> *here, since danger is present in delay, nothing further is required than*
>
> - *first: that the [...] or [Arrestführer] state his claim and what it consists of*
> - *second: that circumstances from which a present danger in delay may be inferred be demonstrated or definitely [ergebe] and the means of proof thereof be stated*
> - *third: that he immediately furnish a [...] surety both to cover the [...] and the arrest on account of the disadvantage that might arise to the same from the seizure.*
>
> *From the third requirement, concerning the furnishing of surety, the [fiscus] and thus also the General Invalids' Fund, which has Dura fisci, is exempt pursuant to § 33 Part. Title 29 of the Court Ordinance. It thus depends only on the presence of the other requirements for an application for arrest. The claim of the General Invalids' Fund against von Triebenfeld for 50,000 [...] in principal and its outstanding interest at 5% will at once show the original bond of debt [...] presently [...] the reason being that through the action of the Military Department, which concerns this loan transaction and is attached sub petito retraditionis, it is certified, whereby the requisite is then satisfied. It is further shown by the autograph letter of v. Triebenfeld of 5 July [...] through the decree of the Kalisch Prefecture submitted in certified copy, that the hypothec pledged by him to the General Invalids' Fund as security for the [...] 50,000 florins and interest, and possibly [edirted], upon the Zagorow estates has been added to the Bayorn sums and the possessor is to be compelled [exeruturisch] to have the sum owed, together with the outstanding interest, newly entered by judicial process. In this way the General Invalids' Fund is deprived of the entire security of its claim, and since it is notorious that v. Triebenfeld's financial circumstances are very entangled and precarious, the danger is stated that it will*

[p. 247]

> *lose the whole prospect of recovering the 50,000 florins and the outstanding interest thereon, and in this regard be left with [bare] recourse, unless the aforementioned 80,000 florins be placed under arrest. In this manner the second requirement for an order of arrest is likewise satisfied here. The circumstance that v. Triebenfeld is the original debtor of the 50,000 and the said interest, and that the 80,000 florins here in question have been bequeathed to His Serene Highness the Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, cannot be prejudicial in this matter. For the Prince, in respect of the 250,000 florins against whose pledging and eventual surety 50,000 florins were advanced by the General Invalids' Fund to v. Triebenfeld, is a debtor of v. Triebenfeld, and the General Invalids' Fund is, by virtue of its [...] surety, entitled to exercise all rights against the Prince which by [Gedenken] belong to v. Triebenfeld, for it has, by virtue of its surety, stepped into the place of the latter. The arrest upon these 80,000 florins is in every respect well founded, whether the Prince still be the owner thereof or should already have relinquished them to v. Triebenfeld for his satisfaction. Both the Prince and von Triebenfeld are bound to the General Invalids' Fund and neither now offers it any security. Von Triebenfeld does indeed possess estates in the lands here, but they are so encumbered with debt that they afford no security, and thus, pursuant to § 10 [...] 29 of the Court Ordinance, arrest lies against them, even though he is resident in royal lands. I most humbly beg the Royal Prussian Kammergericht to be pleased most graciously to grant this application for arrest, and, since the General Invalids' Fund has learned that His Excellency the Minister of Justice von Kircheisen is said to be the executor of the will of the late [...] Princess von Sacken, to be pleased not only to issue to the same [citisferne] the required order of arrest, but also at the same time to declare the said matter and these 80,000 florins, in respect of the capital of 50,000 florins claimed together with*

[p. 248]

> *the interest thereon, to be attached by decree.*  
> *Berlin, 30 July 1811*  
> *Seelmann*
>
> *His Excellency the Royal Minister of State and Justice, Herr von Kircheisen, is hereby called upon, in the event that the claim asserted by the General Invalids' Fund in its suit filed on the 30th [...] [M.] before the Royal Kammergericht — that His Highness the Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen received from the estate of the Princess von Sacken a legacy of 80,000 florins and has assigned the same to the War and Forestry Councillor von Triebenfeld of Kontopp near Grünberg toward the settlement of the latter's claims against him — should prove well founded, to pay out nothing of this sum to von Triebenfeld on his own authority.*  
> *Berlin, 1 August 1811*  
> *Royal Prussian Kammergericht*
>
> *To His Excellency the Royal Minister of State and Justice*  
> *Herr von Kircheisen, in simili*  
> *To the Royal Chief Councillor of Medicine, Herr Cosmar, here*  
> *To the War and Forestry Councillor Herr von Triebenfeld*  
> *at Contopp*
>
> *No. 1103. We herewith transmit to you a summons of the Royal Kammergericht of Berlin of the [1st] instant, in the matter of the General Invalids' Fund against you, to the end that you give due obedience to its contents in every respect and duly certify proper receipt thereof upon the copy to be presented to you for that purpose.*  
> *Glogau, 6 August 1811*  
> *Royal Prussian Superior Court of Silesia*  
> *Fülleborn*

[p. 249]

## Literature and Sources

The Hohenlohe Central Archive at Neuenstein (HZAN) preserves an immense treasury of documents on the subject of this book, one that a single individual can scarcely exhaust. During the numerous visits there to the reading room, it quickly became clear that the many letters would be an essential source, and that priorities would have to be set and a selection made. I have accordingly dispensed with piles of letters that the Prince's descendants wrote to one another, and placed the emphasis on the correspondence in which the Prince himself (or his wife) was sender or addressee. Standing above the individual sources, so to speak, is the now roughly 150-year-old work by Adolf Fischer (1871), which was also drawn on for many brief items of information without direct citation. The book is the standard work on the ruling families of the House of Hohenlohe. In keeping with the time and the state of knowledge then prevailing, the author placed particular emphasis on matters military history in the broadest sense (which I have likewise made use of, though not in as much detail). His book was reissued in 1991 by the Historical Society for Württemberg-Franconia at the Hohenlohe Printing and Publishing House, Gerabronn-Crailsheim. Beyond this, the following secondary literature was consulted:

- Albrecht, J. (ed.): *Archiv für Hohenlohische Landesgeschichte*; vol. 2. Öhringen 1870.
- Bartl, Heinrich: *Die Saline und das Solebad zu Stotternheim 1826-1949. Ein Verdienst des Salinisten Carl Christian Friedrich Glenck.* Erfurt 2008.
- Borst, Arno: *Valmy 1792 – Ein historisches Ereignis?* In: Der Deutschunterricht 26, issue 76 (1974). pp. 88-104.
- Breyer, Albert: *Die deutschen ländlichen Siedlungen des mittelpolnischen Warthebruchs.* Deutsche Wiss. Zeitschr. für Polen, issue 34 (1938). Digital reissue, 2010.

[p. 251]

- Buchholz, Friedrich: *Gallerie Preussischer Charaktere.* Translated from the French manuscript. 1808.

- Engels, H. W.: Afterword to Ch. v. Massenbach "Historische Denkwürdigkeiten zur Geschichte des Verfalls des preußischen Staats seit dem Jahr 1794 nebst seinem Tagebuche über den Feldzug 1806". Frankfurt/M. 1979.
- Fikenscher, Georg Wolfgang Augustin: *Geschichte der Königlich preussischen Friedrich Alexander Universität von ihrem Ursprung bis auf gegenwärtige Zeiten entworfen.* Coburg 1795.

Additional research was carried out via the Kalliope network of German-language archives. Here, under the keyword "Friedrich Ludwig zu Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen", a number of further items came to light. Upon corresponding request I received the documents by post; almost all of them I was able to use. Thanks are due to the following institutions:

**Kantonsbibliothek St. Gallen Vadiana (Robert Alther Collection):**

**Leipzig University Library (letters from FL):**

**Freies Deutsches Hochstift Frankfurt:**

**Stadtgeschichtliches Museum Leipzig:**

[p. 255]

## List of Illustrations

For many of the illustrations, which I largely wished to take over from the cited literature, the image rights are held by the House of Hohenlohe, represented by Kraft, Prince of Hohenlohe-Öhringen. Upon my request I received permission for reproduction without hesitation – thank you! The same holds for the HZAN itself, for which its director, Dr. Schludi, granted the corresponding permission. The captions to the paintings contain only partial information on the painter and date – these could not be reliably ascertained. All sources of illustrations are listed here with reference to the page numbers in the book:

*House of Hohenlohe-Öhringen*: cover image, p. 15, p. 16 (overview plan of Ingelfingen), p. 18 (portrait of the princely couple), p. 48, p. 88, p. 89

[p. unnumbered]

At the beginning of the "long 19th century," Europe was profoundly shaped and forever changed: the French Revolution, the rise and fall of Napoleon, early industrialization, and the reordering of the continent. In the midst of these turbulent events stood Friedrich Ludwig, Prince of Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen (b. 1746; d. 1818). As a general in Prussian service, he experienced the wartime confrontations at first hand; he had to watch as "his" Hohenlohe was absorbed into the new Kingdom of Württemberg, and over the course of his life he amassed an immense mountain of debt through his entrepreneurial activities.

In this book, Hans-Joachim Seidel examines the Prince's dramatic biography and, through numerous contemporary documents and excurses, offers for the first time an insight into the personal circumstances and life of the Hohenlohe prince.

*[illustration: back cover — publisher logo "Klemm+Oelschläger" with ISBN barcode 978-3-86281-136-6, white text on black background]*
